And tawba must be sincere — the signs of sincerity are genuine remorse, immediate cessation of the act, and a firm intention never to return. If you do that, then that is a valid tawba.

And when you make tawba, you should have a good opinion of Allah. To say "I am so horrible, I am so wretched, Allah will never forgive me" — no. Allah tells us not to think that way. That kind of despair is, frankly, a Christian disease. Many Christians fall into it. Though to be fair, Christianity is complex on this point, because alongside that tendency toward despair there exists the opposite extreme — particularly in Baptist Christianity, where a person can do the most outrageous things and be entirely convinced they are safe. That's why the phenomenon of the fallen Baptist minister — the Jimmy Swaggart syndrome — doesn't actually shock Baptist congregations. The attitude is: the devil got you that time, you'll get him the next. That's not tawba.

In Islam, tawba is real. And when a non-Muslim accepts Islam, that is itself the tawba of a non-Muslim from his kufr — and by the consensus of the ummah, that tawba is accepted. When a person enters Islam, all their previous wrong actions are forgiven, by ijma'. It is as though they were just born. Whereas the tawba of a Muslim is a matter of khilaf: if Allah wills, He accepts it; if He does not, He may decline. But you should hope that He accepts it, because we have raja' — hope. Do not be absolutely certain either way; the uncertainty keeps the fear alive and guards against returning to the sin.

<!-- page 73 -->

Someone asked: "I have heard people say that when you take shahada, all your bad deeds become good deeds — is that correct?"

No, but your good deeds do come with you into Islam. Your bad deeds are wiped out; your good deeds remain. So you lose nothing and only gain. That is a really important point.

On the question of Allah and evil: we do not say that Allah brings evil. We say that within the decree of Allah, good and evil both exist — Allah has measured them out, and they exist in the world. But the Prophet ﷺ said in his du'a: "All good comes from You, and evil does not return to You." So while Allah has created a world in which evil is permitted to exist, we do not attribute evil to Allah, even though we believe it is Allah who has measured it out.

Evil, interestingly, can only exist where there is moral agency — and that means human beings. It cannot exist in the animal kingdom. If a lion kills a baby, that is not an evil act; the lion has no moral obligation, no compunction. You cannot call the lion evil. You cannot call a cobra evil for biting someone — it is a snake, doing what it was created to do. But if a human being takes a gun, robs someone of their hard-earned money, and then shoots them — the robbery is evil, and the shooting on top of it is a heinous evil.

What we say is that Allah created the human being, gave him the capacity to choose, and then measured out what he would choose. Allah ﷻ has decreed that at that moment, that person would make that choice. The person is fully responsible for it. Allah permitted it — empowered the person to carry it out through His Qudra — and it was Allah's Mashi'ah, His will and providence, that allowed that person to will what he willed.

وَمَا تَشَاءُونَ إِلَّا أَن يَشَاءَ اللَّهُ

You do not will anything except that Allah wills it at the same time. If I intend to go and harm someone and begin walking toward them, my mashi'ah has started. But if Allah does not will harm to come to that person — say the person suffers a heart attack and dies before I arrive — then I willed something that Allah did not will. So anything you actually carry out can only be carried out if Allah wills it as well.

And this is the distinction: when what you will is what Allah loves, that is tawfiq. When what you will is what Allah hates, that is khidhlan. Allah is the Muwaffiq and the Khādhil. But you are ultimately the one performing the evil. We do not attribute the evil to Allah, yet we acknowledge that Allah has measured it out in the world. It is part of His qadar. Allah has permitted evil to exist — it is part of His creation. And we are fully responsible for the consequences of our actions, whether good or evil.

Ultimately, had Allah not willed it, no evil could exist. But it does not return to Him — we do not attribute it to Him.

Someone asked: "How do you know, in the moment before an action, whether what you will aligns with Allah's will?"

If it accords with Sharia, there is tawfiq. If it does not accord with Sharia, there is no tawfiq. If what you are doing is done with ikhlas — sincerity — and in accordance with Allah's laws, then there is tawfiq. That is what muwaffaq means: a person whose will is aligned with what Allah loves for them to do.

You should never engage in anything without first knowing the hukm of Allah regarding it. It is impermissible to act without knowing what Allah has decreed about that action. And, thankfully, the vast majority of things are permissible; only a small category is haram, and those can be learned. It is not that complex.

Allah did not make the din difficult. The din is not hard. The problem is that human beings simply resist certain things. Taqwa is straightforward — it has four elements: doing what Allah commanded, inwardly and outwardly; inwardly with ikhlas, outwardly in accordance with His laws. And not doing what He prohibited, inwardly and outwardly — refraining not out of fear that someone might see you walking into a bar, but out of fear of Allah. That is the inward dimension. And outwardly, not doing it whether you are alone or in company. That is all taqwa is.

And if you have taqwa, Allah promises you every good in this world: He promises you makhraj — a way out. He promises to rectify your children. He promises to forgive your wrong actions. He promises to open the gates of heaven and send rain and bless your agriculture. He promises you a good life — hayāh ṭayyiba. Those are the worldly promises. And then the otherworldly ones: He promises to forgive you, to enter you into paradise, to place you in the company of the nabiyyīn — the prophets — the ṣiddīqīn, the righteous, the shuhadā', the martyrs, and the ṣāliḥīn. What a deal. What a bargain.

<!-- page 74 -->

On the matter of ikhraj — publicly exposing someone's wrong actions — the Arabs use that word to describe putting someone in an embarrassing position, particularly regarding sins. It is not a good thing to do. Again, this is a matter of murū'ah, of personal dignity and honor. And the Prophet ﷺ said: "From the beauty of a person's Islam is that he leaves what does not concern him." What happens between another person and Allah is no concern of yours. And it is better not to know, because knowing often leads to thinking ill of others.

Someone asked about disclosing one's age — the Prophet ﷺ was reportedly asked about his age and responded: "Is it any concern of yours?" He explained that if you tell someone your age, either they say, "Oh, I thought you were older" — and they diminish you — or they say, "I thought you were younger" — and you still can't win.

But that ruling is different when there is a legitimate concern, such as marriage. Age is a valid consideration in marriage. It is entirely reasonable for a man to be concerned about a woman's age, and vice versa, because age bears directly on marriage and its circumstances. That is why it is impermissible, for instance, to dye gray hairs and present yourself to a prospective spouse as a young man, and then have her discover the truth once married. That is a serious problem — a form of deception.

In traditional cultures, people generally knew good families from bad families — it was common knowledge. A good family meant people who were not engaged in wrongdoing. Unfortunately, that is no longer reliably true. Good families, bad families — the lines have blurred. There are many people who have committed zina, consumed alcohol, taken drugs. And the stakes are especially high now because of diseases and other serious consequences tied to these behaviors.

I think, therefore, there is a valid concern. In the context of marriage and zina specifically — people do have a right to know what they are entering into. Now, the Quranic verse stating that a fornicator can only marry a fornicator or a polytheist applies to the person's condition before tawba. The bariyya was a woman who sold herself openly in the marketplace; it was prohibited to marry someone in that active condition. But if they made tawba, there is no prohibition. Still, given the situation we are in today, I think people have a legitimate right to know. Does that make sense?

<!-- page 75 -->

InshaAllah, let us return to the maḍāra. Where did we get to last week?

"The middle of riya — what a terrible place to be." InshaAllah, may Allah give us all a cure.

Did we reach the section on Imam Mālik, raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu?

حين لم يرعب حين بأس

Yes, all right. So what the text says here is that the scholars hold two opinions concerning those who seek some worldly benefit through worship rather than a benefit in the next world. For instance, if a person recites Surat al-Waqi'a for the sole reason of protecting themselves from financial hardship — because there is a hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said that whoever recites Surat al-Waqi'a every day will not be afflicted with poverty or serious financial calamity in this life. This is well known. Even Ibn Mas'ud, raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu, when asked what inheritance he left his daughters, said: "I left them Surat al-Waqi'a." Surat al-Waqi'a is understood to protect from poverty when recited daily.

The question the scholars discuss is: what if that protection from poverty is the only reason a person recites it? The hadith indicates the benefit is there for whoever does it daily, but what is the intention behind it? Obviously, there is always some degree of iman involved when a person does anything the Messenger ﷺ has encouraged. Reading Surat al-Waqi'a every day is not like, say, investing in the stock market, where the benefit is tangible and worldly in a straightforward sense. The benefit here comes from the unseen world — it is grounded in iman.

He also raises the case of someone who worships in order to attain the next world — seeking paradise as the purpose of their ibada. Imam al-Ūbī al-Mālikī, raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu, said that worshipping for the sake of the next world is jā'iz — permissible — but that it should not be the sole reason driving a person's worship. So it falls under the category of mubāḥ — permitted but not the ideal. That is one opinion.

The people of tasawwuf, however, tend to be extremely critical of doing ibada for any worldly or otherworldly benefit. Their position is that you should perform ibada solely because Allah ﷻ is worthy of taqwa and worthy of maghfira — full stop. No reward expected, no paradise sought, nothing in return. That is the stance of the greatest Sufis. Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi, raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu — who was both a Sufi and a jurist —


He argues that if you read the Qur'an carefully, using your intellect, you will see that Allah is actively encouraging you to strive for His garden and to fear His fire. There is a famous saying attributed to Rabi'a al-'Adawiyya: "Go after the Gardener, not the garden." She also said, "O Allah, if I worship You for the garden, then put me in the fire." That is a remarkably strong statement — I would not say it myself — but it reflected her state, her maqam, her spiritual station. Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi, may Allah be pleased with him, along with Sidi Ahmed Zarruk, both maintain that the worship of Allah in hope of His garden should in no way be belittled, because the Qur'an itself does not belittle it.

<!-- page 57 -->

Imam al-Ghazali identifies three types of worshippers. The first worships Allah as a free person — purely for His sake, desiring nothing else. There is a hadith — though I believe it is either weak or has been commented upon by scholars — whose meaning is that there are people who would not disobey Allah even if there were no fire, simply because Allah is not to be disobeyed. He is worthy of taqwa in and of Himself. The second type, as Qadi Abu Bakr describes it, resembles the merchant — the tujar — asking, "What is in it for me?" This person tallies rewards: if I pray so many rak'as, I receive such-and-such; if I recite so much Qur'an, I earn such-and-such. He desires the garden, and that desire drives his worship. The third type worships like a slave fearing the whip — he acts out of terror of the fire. So you have three categories: those who worship purely for Allah's sake, those who worship desiring the garden, and those who worship fearing the fire.

If you look at the Qur'an, there is clearly both targhib and tarhib — both encouragement and warning. Allah says: race toward forgiveness from your Lord and a garden — سَابِقُوا إِلَىٰ مَغْفِرَةٍ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ وَجَنَّةٍ — and elsewhere: فَفِرُّوا إِلَى اللَّهِ — flee toward Allah. And: وَفِي ذَٰلِكَ فَلْيَتَنَافَسِ الْمُتَنَافِسُونَ — let those who compete, compete for that. The Qur'an contains many verses warning about the fire and many more encouraging good deeds by promising reward. What Qadi Abu Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, is saying is that having tama' — a longing — for Allah's garden, and fear of His fire, is itself ikhlas. It is sincerity, and there is no taint of impurity in it.

So all he is telling us in this passage is that there is a genuine difference of opinion here. If you perform worship for the sake of the next world, some will take issue with that — and those tend to be the Sufiyya. The fuqaha', scholars like Qadi Abu Bakr, Imam Ruyani, and others, consider that position extreme. They hold that worshipping Allah with hope for His garden is ikhlas, because Allah Himself affirms it in the Qur'an. That is, insha'Allah, the stronger opinion.

The next point he raises is equally important: some scholars also consider seeking the pleasure of worship itself to be a concern. And this is something that people who worship seriously over a sustained period genuinely discover — that there is a pleasure in 'ibada that surpasses other pleasures people ordinarily chase. One of the great Hanafi scholars said: "If kings knew the pleasure we are in, they would send their armies with swords to take it from us." He was saying that the pleasure of 'ibada, of 'ilm, of worshipping Allah is so profound that a king, with all his palaces and all his worldly wealth, would fight to possess it if he knew what we had. So some scholars — especially the 'arifin and the people of tasawwuf — warn that this pleasure can become so intense that a person begins performing 'ibada in order to obtain that feeling. He prays the night prayer because of the pleasure it brings.

Some scholars caution against this. But Ahmad ibn al-Hawari — or one of the great early scholars, I am working to recall exactly — said that were it not for these gifts, most people would struggle to sustain their practice consistently. Allah has granted that pleasure as a reward for regular, committed worship, and it is a blessing that should not be diminished. He is simply laying out the range of scholarly opinion on this question.

Then he says: as for the one who likes people to see him doing good deeds — meaning he performs the deed for Allah, but enjoys the fact that others happen to learn of it — that is considered riya' according to some scholars, and not riya' according to others. The basis for the second view is the Qur'anic reference to those who love to be praised for what they have not done. Some scholars drew from that verse the conclusion that it is natural — part of human nature itself — for a person to enjoy recognition for genuinely good deeds. The problem arises when that recognition becomes the reason for doing them. There is a distinction: if you did the deed for that reason, that is unambiguously riya'. But if that was not your reason, yet you find yourself pleased that people acknowledge what you did, some scholars say that is not riya'.

One scholar said: whoever loves to do something and to be known and remembered for it has committed shirk with Allah, Exalted and Glorified. Imam al-Jazuli — a great Maliki faqih from the Maghrib and the teacher of Sidi Ahmed Zarruk — added that riya' can occur even when no one is present. For example: you are reading and come across a rare, obscure point that few people know, and you think, "This would make a strong impression in a gathering" — so you write it down and memorize it to produce it later. He says that is riya'. Full stop. It is a serious problem.

<!-- page 58 -->

If your intention was to benefit others, of course, that is not riya'. But what Imam al-Jazuli was describing is the person who thinks, "This will really get them," or "They will be impressed by this" — which is an obvious trap for people of scholarship in particular. When scholars gather, there is a kind of expectation of mudhakara and musharaka — mutual discussion and exchange — and that can quietly slide into riya'. Sakhr ibn 'Abdillah, a great early scholar and muhaddith among other things, said: "Whoever desires people to know what is between him and his Lord is in ghafla." In other words, it is no one's business but yours and your Lord's. You should not want others to know — unless he sees that people have abandoned good deeds and performs them publicly as nasiha, as sincere counsel and example.

So, for instance, Ibn 'Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, and Ibn Mas'ud were known to go to the masjid before Fajr to perform tahajjud. They could have done this at home, but they did it there so that the Tabi'in would see them and follow their example. Their intention was not self-promotion but nasiha to the Muslims.

Then he says — and al-Nazm here refers to Imam Malik, may Allah be pleased with him, who is called al-Nazm because his student, later the independent mujtahid Imam al-Shafi'i, may Allah be pleased with him, said: "When the scholars are mentioned, Malik is the star" — إِذَا ذُكِرَ الْعُلَمَاءُ فَمَالِكٌ النَّجْمُ. From that, Imam Malik became known as al-Nazm. It is one of his titles, his alqab. He also carries the title 'Alim al-Madina, just as Imam al-Shafi'i is called 'Alim Quraysh and Abu Hanifa, may Allah be pleased with him, is often called al-Imam al-A'zam — the greatest imam. These are titles bestowed upon them by later generations.

Imam Malik did not consider it harmful, as long as the original intention was grounded in sincerity. This position traces back to a question once put to Imam Malik about a man who set out for the masjid for the sake of Allah — his intention was clear: to pray in congregation. But on the way, a thought crept in: "I hope someone sees me going to the masjid." Imam Malik said: "La ara ba'san" — I see no harm in that — "iza kana al-sara'il ikhlas." He does not see it as harmful if the man began with ikhlas and does not follow through on that inner whisper — if he repels it, says "a'udhu billah," and does not entertain it. That is an important distinction to hold.

Then he says: وَأَمَرٌ عَلَى الرِّيَاءِ أَنْ أَفْعَلَهُ مِنْ تَرْكِهِ لِخَوْفِهِ وَأَفْضَلُ ذِكْرُ اللِّسَانِ فَارِغُ الْجَنَانِ أَرَاقِبُ الْقَلْبِ وَاللِّسَانِ. This is a crucial point. He is saying that to abandon an action out of fear of riya' is actually worse than performing it with riya'. Imagine you walk into the masjid and want to pray a few rak'as, but then you think: "Those people might think I am showing off," or "Maybe I am only doing this so they will think well of me." That inner concern is called khawf al-riya' — fear of riya'. If that fear causes you to leave the action, it is itself riya'. Why? Because the reason you abandoned the act was the people. You were preoccupied with them, not with Allah. Leaving an act out of concern for what people think is as much a form of riya' as performing it for their eyes — in both cases, the people are at the centre, not Allah. That is why the scholars say it is better to perform the action even when you sense a trace of riya' in yourself.

This does not mean, however, that you should be careless. If the act of worship is something you can perform privately, it is always better to do so — precisely because of the danger of riya'. But abandoning it out of fear of riya' is worse than doing it despite that fear. The same principle applies to dhikr. Scholars say it is better for your tongue to make dhikr while your heart is heedless than for both your tongue and your heart to be heedless together. If you keep the tongue moving, it may reach the heart — because, as they say, a man's heart lies beneath his tongue. And interestingly, in Chinese medicine, their physiology affirms a direct connection between the tongue and the heart: what the tongue does actually affects the heart, and even the physical movement of the tongue has an impact on it.

<!-- page 59 -->

One more thing worth knowing: the word riya' is derived from the root of ru'ya — seeing. And it is not about others seeing you. It is about you seeing them. The fault lies in your gaze toward the creation rather than toward Allah, Glorified and Exalted. You are the one looking at them; that is where the riya' lives.

One of the many profound things about the Day of Resurrection is that 'A'isha, may Allah be pleased with her, when told by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, that everyone would be raised naked, asked whether people would be looking at one another. He replied: "It will be far greater than that, 'A'isha." People will be in such a state of awe that they will not even be aware of those around them. And this is the very point of worshipping for the sake of Allah alone. When worship is truly for Him, you carry a knowledge of Allah that renders everyone else insignificant by comparison. When you truly contemplate what you are worshipping — consider that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, once saw Jibril in his true form, and Jibril filled the entire horizon. One angel. The entire horizon. Imagine looking out across that vast expanse and seeing a single being filling all of it. And that is Jibril. When you begin to grasp that what you worship is infinite, the thought of other human beings — equally helpless, equally needy, in the same predicament as yourself — loses its grip. They can do nothing for you. No benefit, no harm, nothing. That is the bottom line.

That is why he says: fear of other than Allah and desire for other than Allah are incompatible with tawakkul. Allah says: وَعَلَى اللَّهِ فَلْيَتَوَكَّلِ الْمُتَوَكِّلُونَ — upon Allah let those who trust, place their trust. If you are preoccupied with other people, you do not have tawakkul. And if you lack tawakkul, the reason is an absence of yaqin — certainty. The yaqin you are missing is this: that all good comes from Allah, and that no harm can befall you except by His will. That is what tawhid is teaching us — to be utterly certain that only Allah can harm you or benefit you, and for that reason, fear of human beings dissolves.

This does not mean becoming reckless or impetuous — driving like a madman and saying, "It is all written by Allah." That is not tawakkul. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, told the Bedouin: "Tie your camel, then put your trust in Allah."

Then he says: the root of every disease is a lack of certainty. What is prohibited of these two — fear and desire directed at the creation — is specifically that which prevents an obligation from being fulfilled. If it leads instead to neglecting something recommended, it is considered reprehensible. In either case, flee to your Lord from both. Do not desire the creation and do not fear the creation. And the cure for both, he reminds us, is to know with certainty that no one harms or benefits except Allah.

He then moves to the next disease, which is displeasure with the divine decree — and this will sound very familiar to many people. This disease occurs when a person resists what Allah, Majestic and Exalted, has decreed — for instance, saying, "I did not warrant this happening to me," or "I do not deserve this." And that is a remarkable thing to hear, because you hear it from people all the time.


I don't deserve this. Why is this happening to me? Or, as he puts it next: what did I do to deserve this suffering? What wrong did I do to deserve this? The point is that everything comes from Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala. This is why Imam Abu al-Hasan said that people possess a quality they are entirely unaware of, and it destroys all of their good deeds. Very few people actually recognize it: being displeased with the divine decree. They do not realize they are showing displeasure toward what Allah has ordained. Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala says that they were displeased with what Allah had given them, and so Allah removed all of their good deeds. By contrast, the people of taqwa, when asked what their Lord has sent down, say: "Good — it is all good." That is their answer. Everything is good. Nothing is evil. There is a reason, a lesson, a wisdom in it. This is the difference between the people of Allah and everyone else.

The people of Allah recognize that this world is a place of purification — a mabra in itself. We were sent here to purify ourselves. The very purpose of our existence is to purify ourselves, to draw near to Allah, and to worship Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala. That is it. Once you recognize that, everything becomes an opportunity to learn, to grow, and to draw closer to Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala — everything.

Because there are only four possible states a human being can be in. Only four — no fifth. If you can name a fifth, that would be remarkable. According to Abu al-Abbas al-Mursi, you are either in ni'mah, or in baliyah, or in ta'ah, or in ma'siyah. That is it. You are either in a blessing from Allah, or a tribulation from Allah, in obedience to Allah, or in disobedience. That is the full scope.

Each one of these states calls for a specific response. Allah wants something from you in each case. If Allah gives you a ni'mah — what response does He desire? Shukr. Gratitude. That is the response. And what is gratitude according to the Sharia? It is to use the ni'mah in what is acceptable to Allah — whether mubah, mandub, or wajib. To use it in something makruh does not quite constitute gratitude, but neither is it ingratitude, since it is not haram. Look at those five categories: only one carries punishment. The other four either earn reward or nothing. So: shukr.

He says later in the matn that shukr is to utilize what Allah has given you in what pleases Allah. That is shukr. And so you have shukr al-lisan, shukr al-janan, shukr al-lisan, shukr al-banan — gratitude of the tongue, the heart, and the limbs. Part of shukr is to thank people, to tell others what someone did for you. That is actually considered part of shukr — to say, so-and-so benefited me in this or that. Ihar al-ni'mah, showing the blessing openly, is part of shukr. Allah loves that. To feel the blessing in your heart, and to act with those blessings in a way that pleases Allah.

The next state is tribulation — baliyah. What does Allah expect from us? Sabr. Sabr. Sabr. If He gives you a tribulation, He expects sabrun jamil. Though in truth, you are not even required to have sabrun jamil in its fullest sense. Sabrun jamil is when you do not complain to anyone at all. You can complain — and we will get into that in a moment — but complaining to others is not ideal; it just gives you a degree of release. Then the next state: ta'ah — obedience. What is the response? Shuhud al-minnah — recognizing the gift. The danger is not to become arrogant. If you find yourself in obedience, you must recognize that it is from the tawfiq of Allah that you are there. Do not start thinking you are better than others because you are in ta'ah while they are in ma'siyah. That is the danger of obedience — and in a deeper sense, the inherent danger of religion itself. The religious person begins to see himself as better than the irreligious person. That is Shaytan's opening. Once he has you there, it is rope-a-dope — like Muhammad Ali used to do. He has you until the end of the fight, because kibr is his game. That is Shaytan. Once he has you in takabbur, in takabbur 'ala al-nas, he has you.

So that is the danger of obedience. What Allah expects when you are obedient is that you recognize it as a gift from Him. Recognize that just as He made you obedient, He could just as easily have given the situation of the person who is disobeying Him to you instead, and given them the ni'mah. The Prophet SallAllahu 'Alayhi Wasallam said: do not find fault in others, because if you seek out their faults, Allah will remove those faults from them and place them in you. So there should be a sense of fear — a fear when you see someone in baliyah. That person is in baliyah and he needs to change. And if someone is in ma'siyah, that too is a baliyah, because ta'ah is a ni'mah, and they go together. He is in a baliyah. This is why Imam Malik relates in the Muwatta' that 'Isa 'Alayhi al-Salam said — and Imam Malik frames it as something he heard reported — "balaghani anna 'Isa ibn Maryam qal," meaning: I have just heard, so it is a narration, though he evidently thought it sound enough to include in his Muwatta'. He said that 'Isa 'Alayhi al-Salam was reported to have said that there are only two kinds of people: those in tribulation and those in ni'mah. And he said: do not look upon the people of tribulation as though you are lords over them. Look upon them with compassion, as servants would look upon them.

That is the point. When you see people in tribulation, do not say to yourself, "I am better than these people." Say rather: SubhanAllah, they are in tribulation, and alhamdulillah alladhi 'afani. That is the du'a of the Prophet. When he saw people in tribulation, he said: alhamdulillah alladhi 'afani mimma ibtalahu bihi wa faddalani 'ala kathirin mimman khalaqa tafdilan — praise be to the One who granted me well-being in a way that spared me from the tribulation He gave to these people, and who favored me over so much of His creation. That is where you should stand when it comes to obedience.

Now in ma'siyah — what is expected of you? Tawbah. You must make tawbah. And if you look at this and realize: SubhanAllah, every single thing that happens to me will be one of these four things, and for each one there is a response that will please Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala — then if I please Allah, the thing that happened to me was in fact a ni'mah, because it brought me closer to Allah, and that is the whole point. Perhaps you dislike a thing and there is much good in it for you. Perhaps you love a thing and there is much harm in it for you. Allah knows and you do not. So just admit you do not know — and then comes alhamdulillah, alhamdulillah, alhamdulillah. Ibn 'Abbas said: if a tribulation befalls you, it is itself a ni'mah in that it could have been worse. So you must thank Allah. Alhamdulillah — it is not worse than it could be.

And the best model is to look at the Sira of the Messenger of Allah SallAllahu 'Alayhi Wasallam — to see how he lived, and to recognize that no one endured greater tribulation. He buried all of his children except Fatimah. How many people will ever face that? Out of six children, to bury five? He lost the people closest to him. His most beloved uncle was slaughtered on the battlefield. The beloved wife with whom he had shared years of a blissful marriage died in his most difficult year. Abu Talib, the uncle who had raised him — he had been raised an orphan. There came a time when he had no supporters. The very people who had honored him were calling him a madman, saying he was majnun. How many people experience that — where suddenly your friends, your community, everyone around you calls you majnun and spits on you? He endured sanctions that most people could not bear to even imagine, and that was just one stretch of his life. All of those tribulations — and yet we have no record, not a single word, of complaint. Twenty-three years. No complaints. Alhamdulillah. That is all he ever said. Alhamdulillah.

So that is the situation. To be angry at the qadar is to be in a state of ghafla — in heedlessness, pure and simple.

Now, here is another important point. Imam al-Qarafi, radiyallahu 'anhu, wrote a book dedicated entirely to distinguishing things in the Sharia that appear similar but are not. One of the distinctions he draws is between al-rida bil-qada' wal-maqdi' bihi — being pleased with the divine decree itself versus being pleased with the thing that is decreed. When looking at qada', you have two components. There is the qada' itself, and separately the qadar — and there is some difference of opinion around these two terms. The Maturidis and the Ash'aris actually hold opposite positions on which is which, but the point stands regardless of which label you assign. In one reading, qada' means that Allah has decided to bring something about, and qadar means that He has measured and enabled the means by which it comes to be. The maqdi' bihi is the thing that actually occurs.

Now — should we be pleased with everything that happens, the maqdi' bihi? Why not? Because bad things happen, and do not forget that within Allah's qadar is both khair and sharr. You should not be pleased with sharr, because Allah Himself is not pleased with it. Allah has decreed that there will be evil in the world He created, but He is not pleased with it — meaning He does not reward it; that is what His ridwan is. He does not reward it. So when you see ma'siyah, you should not like it or be pleased with it. Allah does not want you to be pleased with it. But you should not be displeased with the fact that Allah created a world in which such things exist. Do you see the distinction? One thing is refusing to be displeased with Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala and the world He created — because it is His world and He made it. The other is recognizing that within this world, Allah has placed things as a test and challenge for believers, and those are the things you should not be pleased with, because He does not want you to be pleased with them. If you were pleased with them, you would do nothing to change them. Your displeasure with them is precisely what compels you to go out and transform what is wrong in society.

That is the distinction. Imam al-Qarafi offers an analogy — just for taqrib al-ma'ani, to bring the concept closer. He says: imagine you have a doctor, and you go to the doctor, and the doctor says you have gangrene — he has to cut your arm off. So he does. One day that doctor is walking past and overhears you talking to a friend, your arm gone, saying: "I went to this incompetent doctor. I should have gotten a second opinion. He had no business doing this to me." The doctor hearing that is going to be hurt and upset — because he did not want to cause you pain; he wanted to help you, and the amputation was how he helped you. Now, if instead the doctor walks past and hears you say to someone: "I went to this remarkable doctor. He cut off my arm, and it has caused me enormous pain — I cannot sleep at night. It has been the most severe treatment I have ever been through" — when the doctor hears that, he feels compassion, not offense. Because you said nothing against him. All you said is that the treatment was grueling.

And this is the analogy for how we speak about what Allah does to us in this world. If you say, "Allah is really testing me hard right now — it is difficult, but mashallah, there is wisdom in it" — that will not make Allah angry. It is better not to broadcast your troubles to others, and doing so does diminish your patience. But there is a world of difference between saying "I don't deserve this — why is this happening to me?" and saying "this is so hard to bear." The first is a complaint against what Allah has done. The second is an expression of the difficulty you are having in enduring it. That distinction matters enormously.

He also makes this point: we were never commanded by Allah to enjoy hardship and tribulation. Nowhere in the Quran does Allah tell you to take pleasure in horrible things that happen to you. That is not how He made us, and there is no taklif in that. But Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala does censure certain people in the Quran when He says that He sent them a punishment, yet it did not humble them and did not make them call out to Him to lift it from them. You see this today — things worsen year after year, and people simply throw up their hands. Muslim countries — look at what happened to Kuwait. You would think they would all be rising every night for tahajjud, beseeching Allah to never let that happen again. But things return to how they were. There is no istikana, no genuine 'ubudiyya before Allah. Tribulations come, and if they do not change people, that is a deeply troubling state to be in.

Then he moves to the next disease: sum'ah. Sum'ah is one of the diseases of the heart. The clearest way to understand it is that sum'ah is identical to riya', except that riya' pertains to what the eyes perceive, while sum'ah pertains to what the ears hear. In other words, sum'ah is wanting other people to hear about you — needing to tell them what you have done. "You won't believe it — I gave ten thousand dollars to such-and-such charity." You want to tell people about your good deeds. That is sum'ah. And the Prophet SallAllahu 'Alayhi Wasallam said: man samma'a, samma'a Allahu bihi yawma al-qiyamah — whoever does this, whoever broadcasts their deeds to people, Allah will broadcast to all people on the Day of Judgment what they really did. Because everyone has their skeletons. Kullukum khatta'un. All of you sin. Wa khayru al-khatta'in al-tawwabun. Everyone falls short. So if you go around advertising your good deeds, Allah says He will advertise your bad deeds on the Day of Judgment — and you do not want that. So say nothing.

He says: wa sum'atu ikbar al-ta'ati ba'da al-khulus minh min al-afat — you performed the action for Allah, and then you went and told people about it. And so: akhbata al-'amal — you ruined it. Libas al-'aradi al-riya'i wal-'amal tufsiduh, wa lakin in tubtum damad — riya' taints the action and ruins it, but if you make tawbah, it can be rectified. So tawbah is available to someone who fell into sum'ah. And he also addresses the one who performs a deed intending from the outset to tell others about it.


So he didn't act with ikhlas. He actually did it in the first place intending to tell other people about it — and that, too, is sum'a.

The note here clarifies that the difference between seeking reputation by telling others of one's deeds and showing off is that the former operates through hearing and the latter through sight. In other words, they are one and the same disease, but they travel different routes to affect the hearts of others. Ibn Abd al-Salam says that if the reason for disclosure is a desire to encourage others or to acknowledge Allah's blessings upon you, then it is a different matter.

The hadith states that whoever informs others of his good deeds out of a desire for their benefit — and that is the explanation, not the literal text — Allah will inform them of his foul deeds on the Day of Judgment. And whoever displays his good deeds to others, Allah will display his bad deeds to them on Yawm al-Qiyama. It is related by Ahmad and Imam Muslim.

It is a well-established practice among the muhaddithin to cite Imam Ahmad before Imam Muslim, even though Sahih Muslim is the more rigorously authenticated collection. The reason is the jalala — the exalted standing — of Imam Ahmad's maqam in the ummah. You will see this ordering frequently in classical texts.

<!-- page 73 -->

Now he moves on to discuss what lies ahead, and this is a fascinating passage. There was a brigand from Bani Ababa, and the Arabs used him as a proverb — aqtam min Shilab, "more of a brigand than Shilab" — meaning if you want to describe someone as a truly ruthless robber, you invoke his name. The author uses this image deliberately: just as a highway robber severs the road and robs travelers of what they carry, the disease of tama' — covetousness — severs the road to Allah and robs the seeker of his rewards. Tama' is that brigand lying in wait on the path, and so we return once again to the root disease of disordered desire.

Abu al-Abbas al-Mursi relates that he was once in the marketplace intending to buy something when he spotted someone he recognized — a man well known for his scholarship and spiritual standing. The thought crossed his mind that if he approached this person, the man might offer him a favorable price out of deference to a fellow man of religion. At that moment, he says, he heard a call — nudiya lahu, a hatif, an inner voice — telling him: "Salama al-din is to have no desire toward created things." That is the meaning of a sound deen: to strip the heart of tama' toward other people, entirely and without exception.

This is precisely the warning being sounded, because if you look back over the previous diseases discussed, they all share a common thread — the illusion that something can be gained from them. You imagine you are going to get something out of them. And the root cause of tama', according to Sidi Ahmad Zarruq, rahimahu Allah, is ghafla — heedlessness. You forget that Allah alone is al-Razzaq and al-Dari. You forget that human beings have nothing to give you that was not first given to them.

This ghafla that descends on the heart is something Allah warns against repeatedly in the Quran. One verse reminds us: إِنَّ اللَّهَ هُوَ الرَّزَّاقُ — Allah, He is the Razzaq, the sole Provider. Another verse states: وَإِن يَمْسَسْكَ اللَّهُ بِضُرٍّ فَلَا كَاشِفَ لَهُ إِلَّا هُوَ — "If Allah causes you harm, there is none who can remove it except Him." And in the famous hadith, when the Prophet ﷺ addressed Ibn Abbas, he said, "O young man, I will teach you some words." Among what he taught him was this: if all of creation were to gather together to harm you, and Allah had not decreed it for you, they could not harm you in the slightest. And if all of creation were to gather to prevent some harm from reaching you, they could not prevent what Allah had decreed. The pens have been lifted and the ink is dry. It is settled.

Everything that will happen to you is in the hands of Allah Subhanahu wa Ta'ala. So your desire should be directed toward Allah — not toward what other people possess.

<!-- page 74 -->

And what about people who wish you ill — who pray against you? Well, if you are standing upright with Allah, their prayers will gain them nothing. If you are oppressing them, then you have genuine cause for fear, because the Prophet ﷺ said: "Fear the prayer of the oppressed." But if you are not oppressing anyone, you have nothing to fear. A prayer made against someone without just cause does not reach its target — it returns to the one who made it. That is their affair, not yours.

Allah did not command us to make prayers against people except in the case of oppressors: لَا عُدْوَانَ إِلَّا عَلَى الظَّالِمِينَ — "There is no transgression except against the wrongdoers." And it is said that du'a is the weapon of the mu'min. In many situations, the believers have nothing else at their disposal — no army, no political power — only du'a. And to say "only du'a" is to understate it, because du'a is immense. Allah answers the prayers of those who call on Him sincerely.

So there is no need to worry about people's prayers against you unless they have a justifiable grievance, in which case the appropriate response is to seek their forgiveness.

Ghafla is heedlessness. And what Sidi Ahmad Zarruq said is that the cause of tama' is ghafla — you have become heedless of how creation actually works. If you genuinely believe that another person can benefit you independently of Allah, that is ghafla. It is like someone who recovers from an illness and says, "I'm doing great — I took this remarkable medicine and it really worked," attributing the cure to the medicine itself and forgetting entirely. That is ghafla. A believer says: "Alhamdulillah, Allah cured me. I took some medicine, and may Allah have placed healing in it — He cured me through it." When you remind someone of this, they usually say, "Subhan Allah, of course, of course" — and they mean it. But the point is, human beings slip into this forgetfulness constantly. They forget how things truly work. And among the most consequential forms of this forgetfulness is the belief that people can harm or benefit you independently of Allah's will. They cannot. Not in any way, unless Allah wills it.

Now, in matters of armies and military strategy, one must obviously remain in the realm of asbab and deal with things realistically. But when it comes to rizq, to ibada, to riya', to tama', to sum'a — to all these spiritual diseases — the belief that people hold power over your outcomes is pure wahm, an illusion with no foundation.

Ghafla is a disease of the heart, and a serious one. Some scholars went so far as to say it is the only disease. Imam al-Junayd reportedly said he never witnessed a single wrong action that was not ultimately a consequence of ghafla. If the heart is genuinely awake and aware of Allah, wrongdoing becomes impossible. He considered it the source disease, the root of all others.

<!-- page 75 -->

The word ghafil — heedless — is related to mughafal, which means a simpleton, someone easy to deceive. Ghafla, then, is a state of being fooled, but the ghafil is fooling himself — he has simply stopped seeing things as they are. It is a kind of careless stupidity, though not a permanent one. It can be cured.

Then the text turns to ask: why would you speak ill of someone? What is the point? There is some tama' lurking there — some desire. Why would you want to speak badly about a person to someone else unless you wanted that listener to think poorly of them? That is tama'. You want something out of it. There is no other reason to do it. Lying, too — banat al-ghurur, the deceits of the ego — falls into the same category. People lie because they want something. Tama' will also occupy and distract the heart during prayer. And it produces insincere flattery of others — saying things you do not believe, like the salesman who tells every customer they look wonderful in what they are trying on, because he wants to make the sale. He may privately think the item looks terrible. But he will not say so. He says, "That looks really good on you" — because there is tama' in his heart.

There is a well-known hadith that the truthful, honest merchant will be with the martyrs on Yawm al-Qiyama. Consider what that means: to be a merchant without tama' in the heart. How is that possible? It is possible when the merchant sees everything as coming from Allah. If a customer arrives, Allah has brought his provision to him. If the customer leaves without buying, Allah did not decree it. The merchant can simply be a human being. He does not need to lie. He does not need to manufacture enthusiasm he does not feel.

<!-- page 76 -->

In traditional Muslim societies, the marketplace was organized in a way that defies modern marketing logic entirely. All the merchants selling the same goods would be clustered together — the sukk al-atarin, the sukk al-iskafiyyin, each trade in its own row. In Damascus you still know exactly where to find the clothes merchants and where to find the cobblers. They all sat side by side. Not only that, but traditionally they often prayed together, belonged to the same guilds, trained the same apprentices, attended the same circles of knowledge, and shared the same sheikhs. They were, genuinely, close friends.

What would happen — and this is not an idealized fiction — is that if a merchant felt he had sold enough for the day, he would close his stall, go home or to the mosque, and let his fellow merchants serve the remaining customers. There are still a few people like this alive today, though they tend to be quite old, and their sons — back from business school with their MBAs — often think they are being foolish.

Compare that with a culture whose business literature is full of titles like *How to Swim with the Sharks*, and whose advertisements picture a young man in a hotel mirror declaring he is going to "eat the competition for breakfast" — followed by a line about taking his children to an amusement park afterward. That image captures something true about the dominant culture: competitive capitalism in its purest form. Eliminate the competition. Whoever stands in your way, consume them. This mentality scales — from the individual merchant to the geopolitical stage — and the same logic that drives one businessman to undercut a neighbor drives nations to bomb other nations into ruin. It is, at its root, the same disease, just magnified to catastrophic proportions.

<!-- page 77 -->

This is precisely what the Prophet ﷺ warned of when he said that what he feared most for this ummah was al-tanabus — the frenzied competition over dunya. He described it as the disease of civilizations, the thing that ultimately destroys them. Because if your philosophy is "us against them," the problem is that everyone else's philosophy is also "us against them" — and you are their "them," just as they are your "them." In the end, the logic of mutual destruction runs its course.

Some scholars observed that the vessels of tama' are all mujawwafa — hollow, open-ended, impossible to fill. You always want more. The desire is never satisfied. The Greeks had a word for this kind of longing: Eros — not in its later reduced sense, but as an insatiable yearning, always reaching toward something just beyond grasp. The Prophet ﷺ captured this perfectly when he said that nothing will fill the mouth of the son of Adam except the soil of his grave. If he had one mountain of gold, he would desire a second. Nothing suffices.

He also said that two types of people will never be quenched: the seeker of knowledge and the seeker of dunya. So you choose. Those are the two insatiable drives in this world — and you are either one or the other. You are either seeking knowledge or seeking dunya.

Now, it must be said: if tama' is directed toward Allah, it becomes something praiseworthy. Desire itself is a human faculty — it is built into us. The disease is when desire is directed toward other than Allah, when you imagine that benefit lies in created things. The same principle applies to hasad: envy in its blameworthy form is resenting someone for what they have. But if you see someone surpassing you and it moves you to emulate them, to do what they are doing so that you might reach their level — that is a constructive use of the same impulse. These faculties can be turned toward your benefit or your destruction. It depends entirely on where they are pointed.

Now, listen to what comes next — it is a truly remarkable passage.


He says: if you ask desire what its trade is, it will answer, "Earning humiliation." That is its *herfa*. If you ask about its father, it will say, "Doubt concerning the divine measuring out — what is not coming to me." And if you ask about its goal, it will say, "The deprivation of the very thing it desires."

Now, another matter.

<!-- page 78 -->

In the *khatimah* — and this is genuinely important, so I want everyone to listen closely — what he is saying is that your desiring something relates directly to what has been allotted to you in this life, which is called *rizq*. There are some vital concepts here. One is that Allah rarely pairs two names with the same attribute. You find it occasionally — *Rafi' al-darajat*, things like that — but He is both *al-Razzaq* and *al-Raziq*, and we are called *marzuq*: the recipients, the passive form. What we receive is the *rizq*, and what gives it is Allah.

I mentioned last week that scholars differ on the definition of *rizq*: some say it is only what you actually benefit from; others say it is everything you possess. The dominant opinion is that it is what you benefit from. There is nothing on earth except that its *rizq* comes from Allah — He has given every creature its provision. For Allah has divided the *arzaq* of people, their provisions, and there are two kinds: *rizq bopani* — inward provision — and *rizq lahiri* — outward provision. The outward *rizq* is your food, your health, the things we can observe. The inward *rizq* is your company, your knowledge, what you genuinely learn, the character you develop — all of that.

Now, one of the striking things about life is that the same people keep appearing in it. It is a remarkable phenomenon. There are five billion people on this planet, yet when you think carefully about the people who truly matter in your life, the number is actually very small — and they keep returning, again and again, because they are part of your *rizq*. They are written into it. So your company will largely consist of the same people cycling back through your life. And within that *rizq* there is both trial and blessing, hardship and good. Some people hold a pessimistic view of this; others hold an optimistic one — especially once you understand that all of it comes from Allah, and Allah is the one bringing it about.

Here is what is truly important to grasp. Allah has given the human being wealth as a *ni'mah* and placed it within him, and in His divine decree He has distributed the *arzaq* among people. But some of one person's *rizq* He has placed with another person, and there is a *sabab* — a means — through which you must go and retrieve it. So here is this person, and he holds your *rizq*. It is not his *rizq*; it is yours. But Allah has placed it in his possession. How you receive it depends on the circumstances. If it is food he has that was written for you, he will invite you to dinner — he may not even particularly like you, but if it was written for you, you will end up at that table. Perhaps you were not even invited; you arrived with someone else. That is *rizq* — it is there, it is *maqsun* for you, and he cannot consume it, because it is not for him.

That is why the Prophet, ﷺ, loved welcoming guests more than almost anything. The guest arrives with his own provision. You lose nothing by feeding a guest — that provision was not written for you; it was written for him. And he departs carrying the wrong actions of the household with him: when you are generous and courteous to a guest, Allah removes wrong action as a *kaffarah*. The guest, then, is a tremendous blessing from Allah.

<!-- page 79 -->

Now, because of the *asbab* — these means are so mysterious — people begin to think, "My *rizq* comes from him." That is the *ghafla*, the heedlessness. Because Allah has arranged things so that your *rizq* travels through someone else, people begin to reason: "He benefits those people; perhaps I can get something from him too." And so the *tama'* enters — you see the *sabab*, the means, and lose sight of the *musabbib*, the true originator, the one who causes those means to exist. This is precisely where *tama'* becomes dangerous, and the clearest sign of it is when you grow angry at not receiving what you want. That is the surest indicator. When an expectation goes unfulfilled and you become angry, that is the sign.

This is obviously not about legal rights — *huquq*. If someone owes you something, has it, and is withholding it, that is injustice, and your anger at injustice is legitimate. What we are speaking about here is *rizq*: what is coming to you, what is yours by divine decree.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ said in the hadith — conveying what Jibril told him — that no soul will die until it has completed every portion of the *rizq* allotted to it. It must complete it.

I was once on a plane in Mauritania, flying from Néma to Nouakchott. A man boarded at the front in a wheelchair. The moment we landed in Nouakchott — just as we touched down — the seats went back and the crew began performing CPR. He had died. And then, in the way that is not so extraordinary in Mauritania, the man who had come to meet us at the plane turned out to be a close friend of the deceased. So we learned the man's whole story. He had been a minister, well respected, around forty-four years old. He had fallen ill and had gone out into the desert to recuperate. Someone administered an injection; he went into a coma. They brought him anyway — they were transporting him to Nouakchott. The point is that he had an appointment with the Angel of Death in that place. He had been out in the middle of the Mauritanian desert in a tent, but he was destined to die in Nouakchott. So all the *asbab* were arranged so that he would reach the place where his death had been decreed, because there was no other place he could die — it was *maqsun* for him to be in that place at that time.

And so it is: if Allah intends for someone to reach a certain place, He will furnish the reasons to go there. The same holds true for *rizq*. This is why the Prophet ﷺ said — Jibril conveying it to us — that all your *rizq* is written for you. Then he said: have *taqwa* of Allah. Do not do anything *haram* to obtain your *rizq*, because it is coming to you regardless. Do not go about begging or soliciting from people. In fact, among the pledges the Companions took with the Messenger of Allah ﷺ — one item of the *bay'ah* — was that they would not ask people for money. That was explicitly part of the covenant. *Iman* should free a person from all such anxieties.

<!-- page 80 -->

And then he said something further: do not let the delay of your *rizq* make you disobedient to Allah. If it is not arriving as quickly as you wish, do not let that delay become the cause of your falling into disobedience. That is a vital principle.

Now, everyone in *salah* — the next disease is extended hope. We will cover that next week. Last week we covered covetous desire, *tama'*. And in speaking of it, he says the Arabs have what they call *samm sa'ah* — a fast-acting, immediately lethal poison. The immediately acting poison of this love of *dunya*, of *tama'* and all the diseases of the heart, is *tatwil al-amal*. He defines it as habituating yourself to the assurance that death is a long way off.

Now, on one level this is a necessary human condition, and there is actually a remarkable wisdom in *tatwil al-amal*. Among the wisdoms the scholars have noted is this: if people did not have extended hope, they would not bother planting trees. If you believed you might die at any moment, you would not farm, you would not plan, you would not raise children with any thought for their future. The result would be that each generation would have to deal only with immediate circumstances, with no infrastructure to pass on. But because human beings do carry hopes of living on, they build, they farm, they create the infrastructure that the next generation inherits.

The famous story — from one of the Persian tales — is of a Persian *shah* who passed an old man planting an olive tree. The king asked: "Will this tree be of any benefit to you? Surely you will die before it bears fruit" — because a good olive tree can take several decades before it yields well. And the old man replied with what became a proverb among the Arabs: *"They planted before us, and we ate from what they planted. So we plant, that those who come after us may eat."* The king gave him a reward. Then the man said, "Already the tree has benefited me." So the king gave him more.

The point is that *tatwil al-amal* is a *rahma* from Allah — He has placed it in human nature, because without it people would not go to school, would not plan, would not build anything lasting.

<!-- page 81 -->

One thing worth noting about modern society is that because of the apocalyptic character of the age we are living in, people are beginning to lose hope in the future. Many young people are becoming nihilistic — dressing in black, adopting a morbid view of the world. The fast-food culture — the idea of getting it now, not waiting — and the credit culture are genuinely eroding people's sense that there is a future worth building toward. In fact, this may be the first American generation that genuinely expects to live worse than their parents. Prior generations of Americans were noted for their buoyant optimism about the future. That optimism is fading — and its loss is itself a bad sign.

On the other hand, what he is saying is that too much hope is equally a deadly poison. It becomes a false hope, an *aman al-aman*. The result it generates is hard-heartedness and indolence — *yulqihu kaswat al-qalb wal-kasal*. Through *tul al-amal*, the heart becomes hard because it stops reflecting on the *akhirah*, stops reflecting on its own mortality and dependence. And so it hardens.

Al-Fudayl ibn 'Iyad, *radiAllahu 'anhu* — one of the great early scholars and *zuhhad*, the people of asceticism — said: *"Khamsun min alamat al-shaqawah."* There are five signs of *shaqawah* — wretchedness. The world is divided into exactly two kinds of people: *sa'id* and *shaqi*. There is no third category. *Sa'id* is felicitous; *shaqi* is wretched.

Now, if you look at Aristotle — foundational to so much of Western civilization — he wrote a book called *The Ethics* in which he essentially argues that the goal of life is leisure and happiness. This is echoed in the Declaration of Independence, written by Thomas Jefferson, which states that people have certain inalienable rights — and among them are life (*hayy*), liberty (*hurriyya*) — rights recognized in the *shari'ah* as well — and then "the pursuit of happiness." Jefferson's original phrasing was actually "the right to be free of debt when you come into the world," because what happens now is that national debt means children are born already in debt — approximately fifty thousand dollars at birth, based on previous generations' overspending. But he changed it to the pursuit of happiness. And what was happiness to a middle-aged white landowner in 1776? More land. In essence, the pursuit of stuff, of goods. This is woven into the fabric of the modern American dream: happiness understood as the pursuit of things.

What Islam did was radically reorient human beings' understanding of happiness. Happiness is not necessarily what we might call a "good life" in the worldly sense. For Muslims, happiness — *sa'adah* — is in the next world. If you are *sa'id* in the next world, it does not matter how your life in this world appeared. We looked at this in the previous class, in the *sira*, when we examined the Prophet's ﷺ supplication: "If You are not angry with me, then I do not care what You do with me" — meaning: if my life in this world is difficult but I have not incurred Your wrath, I am content with that. Because he knew that *al-akhiratu khayrun laka min al-ula* — the afterlife is better for you than the first life.

<!-- page 82 -->

The opposite of *sa'adah* is *shaqawah* — wretchedness — the condition of the person who has no portion in the *akhirah*. That is what *shaqi* means. So al-Fudayl ibn 'Iyad said there are five signs of *shaqawah*.

The first is *qaswat al-qalb* — a hard heart. That is a sign of wretchedness. The Prophet ﷺ once said to a man who had just watched him kiss a child: "I kiss my children — I have ten and I have never kissed a single one." He was boasting of it as a kind of tough-Arab pride. And the Prophet ﷺ said: "I cannot do anything for a man from whom Allah has removed *rahma* from his heart." A hard heart means you have lost your portion of the *deen*. *"Have mercy on those on the earth, and He in the heavens will have mercy on you."*

The second sign is an eye that does not weep. The scholars say: if you want to know whether your heart is hard, look at your eye. A dry eye indicates a hard heart. The person with *riqqah* — tenderness — in the heart has a moist eye; the person without it has a dry one.

The third is *qillat al-haya'* — a deficiency in modesty and shame. People who have no shame. As the saying goes: if you have no shame, do as you please. *Qillat al-haya'* is a sign of *shaqawah*.

The fourth is *al-raghbah fi al-dunya* — desire for the *dunya* — which is likewise a sign of *shaqawah*. The scholars say that no one adds to his *akhirah* except that it diminishes something of his *dunya*, and no one adds to his *dunya* except that it diminishes something of his *akhirah* — because the two are opposites by nature. An increase in one is a decrease in the other.

This does not mean you automatically assume that a wealthy person has sacrificed his *akhirah*. There is a well-known teaching story — a Moroccan tale — about a *zahid*, an ascetic, who had heard of a great *wali* of Allah and set out to visit him. He came down from his mountain riding his lion. He arrived to find the *sheikh* living in a large palace. He was astonished — he himself lived in a cave. The *sheikh* came out to greet him, and the *zahid* asked where he could stable his lion. The *sheikh* said, "Put him in my barn with the cow." He did not realize the lion would eat the cow. The *sheikh* said, "No, no — just put him in the barn with the cow." So in he went.

Then they sat down to a large dinner. The *sheikh* ate a few bites and said, "Astaghfirullah — how luxurious this is." The *zahid* thought: "I live on water and dates." That night the *sheikh* went to sleep, and the *zahid* stayed up all night in prayer — only to hear the *sheikh* snoring in the next room. He was utterly scandalized. He decided that after *Fajr* he was leaving. He went to retrieve his lion — and found that the cow had eaten the lion.


So he asks the man, "Where's my lion?" The man replies, "Your lion is just like you — all outward show." And then he adds, "And you thought my cow was harmless."

But she was a lion on the inside. The point of that story is that you have to be very careful, because outward appearances can fool people. Sidi Ahmad Zarruk has something remarkable to say about this — though we'll return to that when we reach the discussion of having a bad opinion of others.

Those five things, then, are signs of hardness of the heart. What this science is ultimately about is bringing life back to the heart.

<!-- page 75 -->

One of the interesting observations in *Nur al-Yaqin* — the *sirah* by an Egyptian scholar — concerns how many of the Sahaba lost everything they possessed of this world. He notes that their concern was not for material comfort, and he speaks of the first masjid built at Quba, describing it as so simple that a Muslim today would not recognize it as a masjid. He argues that we have become obsessed with adorning outward appearances while our interiors have been left desolate. The Sahaba, he says, were the opposite: they had no concern for beautifying the outward and were concerned solely with purifying their hearts. That difference in emphasis is enormous.

What the text is saying, then, is that hardness of heart will generate indolence toward obligations, which in turn opens inroads into the prohibited. Indolence here means a lack of energy, a failure of will. Interestingly, there are people who have tremendous energy for worldly pursuits yet none whatsoever for the Hereafter. People genuinely say things like, "I really can't pray — I work so hard, I get home exhausted, I fall asleep, I'd like to pray but I just don't have time." That is a sign of a dead heart, because when someone has time for the things of this world but not for the Hereafter, it reveals that they do not truly believe in the Hereafter.

Hasan al-Basri said: "I saw seventy men of Badr. If you saw them, you would say they were madmen" — he was speaking to the tabi'in — "you would call them *majanin*. And if they saw the best of you, they would say these people have no character. And if they saw the worst of you, they would say these people do not believe in the Day of Judgment." Belief in the Hereafter manifests in a person's behavior and concerns. The stronger the belief, the more one is preoccupied with protecting one's standing in the Hereafter.

<!-- page 76 -->

Looking at the footnote: loss of energy even for acts of obedience leads a person to convince himself that he is simply unable to fulfill his obligations — due to his state — and this is precisely what opens the door to the forbidden. He tells himself, "I just can't do it," and that very attitude leads him toward the haram.

So *tul al-amal* is genuinely dangerous.

There is a saying — not a hadith, though it is often quoted as one — that goes: *i'mal li dunyaka ka'annaka ta'ishu abadan, wa i'mal li akhiratika ka'annaka tamutu ghadan* — "Act for your worldly life as if you will live forever, and act for your Hereafter as if you will die tomorrow."

Shaykh Bashir — Usman Bashir, rahimahullah — a dear friend of mine who spent most of his life in Medina and was from Eritrea — once told me that people completely misunderstand that saying. It is almost always used as a justification for working hard at worldly pursuits. People love to quote it: "Ya akhi, *i'mal li dunyaka ka'annaka ta'ishu abadan*!" But he said: if you read it with both lenses at once — your worldly life, for which you have forever; and your Hereafter, for which you die tomorrow — you will naturally begin pouring your energy into the Hereafter and say to yourself, "I have plenty of time to take care of my worldly affairs." The understanding most people carry is exactly backwards, and his reading is really the correct one.

Related to this is the Quranic verse: *wa la tansa nasibaka min al-dunya* — "Do not forget your share of this world" (28:77). This has been interpreted in two ways. One reading is: do not forget what Allah has given you, so that you may expend it for the Hereafter. The other is: the world is here, Allah gave it to you, so do not forget that it exists — but the essential thing remains the Hereafter. Both interpretations are valid positions.

We are not anti-worldly in the way that, say, traditional Christianity tended to be. We are not against the world. The world is a place Allah created for us to enjoy as well — but not to enjoy to the point where we forget what we are truly here for, and that it is a bridge.

<!-- page 77 -->

Then the text cites a verse in which Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala says, "A long time passed and their hearts became hard." The idea is that if you are not reflecting on the Hereafter, the longer you remain on this earth, the harder the heart grows.

If you observe children, you notice something striking: they can cry at the drop of a hat. Part of this is that their hearts are very supple — they can move through extreme joy and extreme sadness within a single day. Their hearts are like this because they are very pure, and so everything affects them in profound ways. As a person grows older, that softness becomes increasingly difficult to recover.

This is one reason the scholars say the Prophet ﷺ loved women among the things made beloved to him in this world — *li raqati qulubihinna*, because their hearts are more tender than those of men. Women, generally speaking, have more compassion and more openness of heart. For men, it is very easy for the heart to harden. And so the danger of *tul al-amal* is precisely that it produces hardness of heart.

Then the text says: *laki nuhufihaqri min nirad an'ab, au kan ifi tarsniki ar-inman lam yu'ab* — regarding the one who is engaged in preparing for the next life, or in producing works of knowledge. The author — himself a scholar — notes that writing books that benefit people is among the highest things one can do: *sadaqah jariyah*, or *ilm yuntafa'u bihi al-nas*, ongoing charity or knowledge from which people continue to benefit. For such a person, desiring a long life is not blameworthy.

<!-- page 78 -->

The Prophet ﷺ said: *la tatamanna al-mawt* — do not wish for death. For if you did wrong in the past, you want more life in order to make tawbah and set things right. And if you did good, you want more time to do more good. Either way — whether your past has been poor or praiseworthy — each new day is an opportunity to turn back to Allah and change. So one should always desire a long life when engaged in good things. If one is not engaged in good things, however, then a long life is a disaster.

For the person who does not waste time, who recognizes the urgency of the human condition and their own impending mortality, and who sets out doing useful deeds — spreading knowledge or anything else that benefits people — Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala says: *yam kuthu fi al-ard* — what benefits people remains and continues in the earth.

Mu'tamir ibn Sulayman once fell gravely ill, and someone asked him whether he feared dying from it. He said, "No, no, no — I have a verse in the Quran that tells me I am not going to die yet." The person asked, "Which one?" He said, "Allah says that what benefits people stays for a long time" — and he was a great scholar, enormously beneficial to others.

<!-- page 79 -->

Hard-heartedness, then, manifests in several ways: eyes that do not weep, an absence of shame and modesty, love of this world, and extended, false hope. There is also a very beautiful tradition that says: *al-amalu rahmatun min Allah, wa lawlahu lama zara'a zari'un wa la walada waldun* — extended hope is a mercy from Allah, for had it not been for hope, no farmer would plant seeds and no mother would bear a child. It is a *rahmah* from Allah — that is the important thing to understand.

It is part of our nature. We genuinely believe, at some deep level, that we are going on. Without that sense, we could not function in the world — we would simply be paralyzed. So the point is not to fall into paralysis, but to reflect on death. The poet says in the *qasidah*: *tafakkar akhiyaf al-mawt, innahu nazirun, wa qassir amal, fayaksur al-qalb* — reflect on death, for it is coming, and shorten your hopes, for that is what breaks open the hardened heart.

One of the beneficial exercises the early Muslims practiced was to meditate on death and on the stages that follow it — to reflect on the stations of the journey itself, including the sirat, and what it means to cross it. It is interesting that athletes in this country are trained to use visualization: a boxer will mentally run through a fight in advance as psychological preparation. This is really the same idea. You are preparing yourself psychologically for a journey you will actually make. Marat al-Raji has a *qasidah* about death in which he says: *fa-sa-tusafiru, radhan al-yasafir* — "You will travel tomorrow, and what a journey." Meaning death. What a journey indeed.

<!-- page 80 -->

The moment you die, there is a severance — and yet you remain conscious, in a state of heightened awareness so acute that this present life comes to seem like a dream by comparison. This is why people at the moment of death often feel as though they are waking up. The Prophet ﷺ said, *al-nasu niyam, fa idha matu intabahu* — "People are asleep; when they die, they awaken."

The exercise many of the early Muslims practiced was to actually lie down and imagine being lowered into the grave. This is not a morbid exercise — it is training. And it is genuinely effective. Some of the scholars said that for a person overcome by *shahwah* — appetites and desires — particularly young people, this kind of reflection is one of the best ways to bring sobriety to the heart.

<!-- page 81 -->

Now, there is an important distinction that Imam al-Qarafi, rahimahullah, draws: the difference between *raja'* and *tul al-amal*. *Raja'* means hope, and it is something altogether different from *amal*. There are many verses in the Quran affirming this: *wa kana lahum fi rasulillahi uswatun hasanatun li man kana yarjullaha* — whoever hopes in Allah has a beautiful example in the Messenger of Allah. There is a *raja'* directed toward Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala, and toward the Last Day.

There is a famous hadith in which Aisha, radiyallahu anha, recounts that the Prophet ﷺ said: *man ahabba liqa'a Allah, ahabba Allahu liqa'ahu* — whoever loves to meet Allah, Allah loves to meet him. Aisha said, "O Messenger of Allah, but what about disliking death?" He said: *kullukum takrahu al-mawt* — that natural aversion to death itself is something everyone shares. But not wanting to meet one's Lord is a different matter — the one whose heart is at peace (*al-mutma'inn*) looks forward to that meeting.

<!-- page 82 -->

The scholars emphasize the relationship between *khawf* and *raja'* — *wa'buduhum bayna al-khawfi wa al-raja'* — you walk with the two sandals of fear and hope. If you are overwhelmed by fear, it pushes you into a psychological state of terror that produces *ya's* — despair. Too much fear leads to despair of Allah's mercy; a person begins to think they will never be saved. This is a well-known religious illness. It is less common today, since people are generally less religiously intense, but it remains real for certain individuals. Some Christians, particularly in fire-and-brimstone evangelical traditions that dwell heavily on hellfire and damnation, can easily fall into this state. The early Puritan settlers actually believed that only 144,000 people would be saved — the elect. Since there were already more than 144,000 Puritans, the odds in that theology were not favorable. The Jehovah's Witnesses hold a similar belief, drawn from the Book of Revelation. That is excessive fear.

Too much hope — excessive *raja'* — is the opposite disease, producing complacency: "I have no problem, I am saved," and so action stops entirely. There is an interesting observation one could make about certain Baptist theology, which holds that once you accept Jesus as your Savior, you are saved regardless of what you subsequently do. The emphasis falls overwhelmingly on salvation through faith, with very little weight placed on fear of hellfire. This is why someone like Jimmy Swaggart can fall publicly, and then return to the pulpit to preach — and the congregation is still there listening. If the same thing happened to an imam, *la thar Allah* — that would simply be the end. No one would retain any respect for that person, because Muslims hold far higher expectations of those they accept as examples and leaders in their communities.

This is, incidentally, one reason why it is *makruh* for the child of an illegitimate union to lead the prayer — not because of any sin on the child's part, but because such a person is not the most fitting *qudwah*, or example, for the community. There is no *ithm* — no blame — on such a child. None whatsoever. What the parents did is entirely between them and their Lord. *Wa la taziru waziratun wizra ukhra* — no soul bears the burden of another's wrongdoing. There were people in the Arabian Peninsula at the time of the Prophet ﷺ who were born outside of marriage, who became Muslim, and who were counted among the finest human beings. The least of the Sahaba, in terms of their deeds, surpasses the best of us — simply because they were given the *ru'yah*, the vision of the Prophet ﷺ in waking life and believed in him. That alone is a bounty from Allah beyond reckoning.

<!-- page 83 -->
