So: *amal* is a desire for long life, for wealth, for all the things we generally associate with happiness in this world. It is a kind of hope — a *raja'*. But there is something else called *umniyah*, and it is *umniyah* that is blameworthy in Islam. *Umniyah* is a hope —


The difference between *rajah* and *umniya* is that *rajah* always involves taking the *sabab* — in other words, actually pursuing the means to obtain what you want. With *umniya*, by contrast, it is *bila sabab*: no means are taken at all.

Say you want to get healthy. You start watching your diet, you start exercising — that is *rajah*. But someone who says, "I really want to get healthy," while continuing to eat junk food and sit in front of the television, doing nothing to change his situation — that is *umniya*. And that, Allah says, is exactly what the Jews and Christians have toward the *akhirah*: they have *umniya*. They are doing nothing to earn the next life; in fact, they are corrupting in the earth. That is why Muslims must be on guard, because — as my friend Abd al-Hakim Murad once told a story about a Pakistani man who overheard two or three racists in London: one said he was moving to Wales because Muslims were only one or three percent of the population there, another said he preferred Scotland at two percent, and a third was going to Ireland at one percent. The Pakistani man said, "Why don't you go to hell? There are no Muslims there." What Abd al-Hakim observed about that is that the assumption, of course, is that Muslims don't go to hell. Unfortunately, there is going to be a great shock on *Yawm al-Qiyama* — because if all you have is *umniya*, and all your Islam amounts to is the *shahada taim*, what they call the *shahada* of birth and death — *shahada tawlidatika* or *shahada tawaffatika* — your testimony recorded at birth on your birth certificate and at death on your death certificate, if that is the sum of it, then you have a serious problem.

<!-- page 84 -->

I was once in Santa Clara; Farif was with me. A family came — their grandfather had died, and they came to the masjid to bury him. None of them were practicing Muslims, and neither had their grandfather been, but when he died they realized, "We are Muslims — how do we bury him? Let's take him to the mosque." So they brought him, and we washed the body and performed the rites. But it is deeply sad when people's entire experience of Islam is reduced to the moments of marriage and death — they need an imam to perform the *nikah*, or they need someone who knows how to wash the body and pray over it. That is *umniya*.

*Rajah*, then — hoping in Allah — means you are actually doing what is required to be pleasing to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala. The scholars also say that hope should be the dominant state throughout one's life, but when you reach your deathbed, you should let go of fear entirely and rest in absolute hope in Allah. At that moment there should be no fear; you should be completely immersed in a sense of Allah's *rahma*, trusting that He will forgive you and accept you, and holding the best opinion of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala.

Now, Imam al-Haythami makes another point that Shaykh al-Khadim mentions in his commentary, and it is important: *bud al-amal* — this extended hope, this drawn-out expectation — is, at its root, a heedlessness about the reality of death. But he says that in and of itself it is not a wrong action. There is no obligatory command (*wajib*) to remember death. When the Prophet ﷺ said to remember death, and also said, "I used to tell you not to visit graves, but now I tell you: go visit them, because it reminds you of the *akhira*" — the point is that remembering death is *mandub*, recommended. In the same way, *dhikr* beyond what is prescribed in the prayer is *mandub*:

*Ud kur Allāh* — *ud kuru* — there is the imperative, the command, but it is for *nadb*, not *wujub*. You are not obligated to remember Allah as a *fard*; it is *mandub*, *mustahab*, strongly encouraged, because it prepares you. So heedlessness of death is not a wrong action in itself.

<!-- page 85 -->

Similarly, making money, being engaged in the *dunya*, being active in worldly affairs — that is *mubah*. Allah did not prohibit it. You may trade on *Hajj*; there is nothing wrong with it. You can combine *dunya* and *akhira* on *Hajj*. You do not have to go in a state of pure spiritual detachment. You can take goods, bring books, sell things — within whatever is permitted. People who shave pilgrims, bringing razors and charging for the service — they are *hujjaj* and they are earning money, and there is nothing wrong with that. Our *din* does not even frown upon it. You should not say *astaghfirullah* and think they are not spiritual people; that attitude is not from our *din*.

So he says: that is permissible — *unless* it was done for *tafakhur* or *takathur*, or for *taswif al-tawba*, in which case it becomes a grave sin (*kabirah*). If you are acquiring wealth in order to compete with others out of arrogance, or to hoard it in a contest of accumulation, or — *taswif al-tawba* — to keep putting off repentance, then it is blameworthy. As the *ayah* says:

*yafjuru amāmahu, yas'alu ayyāna yawmu l-qiyāma*

Man wants to keep sinning in whatever remains of his life and asks, "When is the Day of Judgment?" — as though it is far off, as though he need not think about it. The *ulama* say in their commentaries on this *ayah* that one of its meanings is *yusawwif al-tawba* — he keeps postponing his repentance. This is what is called in this culture "sowing your wild oats" — the idea that when you are young and have the energy for *ma'asi*, you should get them out of your system, because you won't have the energy when you are older. This is a concept in American and European culture. And this is precisely why Allah loves the young man who is in obedience — because when you grow old, you no longer have the energy for disobedience.

Some people live badly for decades, then around forty-five or fifty, death begins to feel real, they make *tawba*, and then they become self-righteous, pointing fingers at everyone else, having completely forgotten who they themselves were. This is a real problem — fifty years old, having done every terrible thing: drinking, cheating, you name it — and then at fifty realizing mortality, going to the masjid, becoming outwardly devout, and immediately beginning to judge others. That is a disease of its own.

<!-- page 86 -->

Now, the next disease is called *tiyara*, and it is a somewhat nuanced concept because it has a related term in the *hadith* vocabulary, *tiyara*. The Arabs, before Islam, when they wanted to make a decision, would go to a flock of birds and startle them — a form of divination. If the flock flew to the left, they took it as a bad omen; if it flew to the right, they took it as a positive omen. The right-flying birds they called *yamun* — from which *Yemen*, being to the right when one faces the rising sun — and the left-flying birds they called *shu'm*, which is why the Levant (*al-Sham*) carries that sense of the left. In the Arabian Peninsula, facing the sun in the morning, Yemen is on the right (*al-yamin*) and al-Sham is to the left. You also get *shamal* — north, the left side — and *janub*, south, from *janb*, meaning side or flank, because you recline on your right side; so the south is on the right and the north is on the left.

This is also reflected in traditional Muslim cartography. Muslims always oriented their maps with the south at the top and the north at the bottom. I can show you maps from the classical books of *tawqit* — the science of timekeeping, the *'ilm al-falak* of the Muslims — and all of them show the south at the top. This is why I have long believed that Muslims should make their own maps.

I first came to this realization years ago in New Mexico, when I was at a flea market with Hakim Archuleta. I found an old antique compass, and it was cheap. The vendor said, "There's only one problem — instead of pointing north, the arrow points south." I said, "That's fantastic." As we walked away I asked my friend Hakim, who knows a great deal about science, why it would make any difference. He said it's simply a matter of polarization — the paint is facing south because that is the *qutb*, the pole. That started me thinking about the question of south and north, and why we have made north dominant.

Then I came across a map by al-Idrisi — the first great cartographer of the world, a Moroccan who drew a world map with the south at the top and the north at the bottom. The European mapmakers drew directly from his work, but they recognized the significance of putting the south at the top: it placed Africa above Europe. So they flipped it, and from that point on their maps had north at the top. Marshall Hodgson makes exactly this observation in *The Venture of Islam* — he says it is unfortunate that Muslims do not recognize that they produced the first world map and oriented it with the south at the top, and that they should return to that orientation, because the north-dominant map reflects a purely Eurocentric worldview that has no historical foundation. A south-oriented map would put Africa at the top and Europe at the bottom; we would say, "I'm going down to Europe and up to Africa."

<!-- page 87 -->

We would also have to say "up to Los Angeles and down to San Francisco." And in Arabic, this is in fact how it works — the south is up. Southern Egypt is called *Sa'id*, Upper Egypt, while the north is Lower Egypt. In Libya as well, people say, "We are going up south" and "down to the sea," which is in the north. That is traditional cosmology, and do not think it is insignificant — the effect of spending a lifetime looking at maps that place Africa at the bottom is real. On a south-oriented map, moreover, nothing looms over Arabia. Look at the map the Australians produced when they decided they were tired of being "down under" — they simply turned it right-side up. I have a map from New Zealand, sent to me by a friend after I told her about this. It is a genuinely revealing perspective: you see how close Ireland is to Morocco, how close Germany is to England, and you begin to understand the depth of the Germanic-English connection in a way the north-dominant map never quite conveys. But that is a digression from the diseases of the heart — a worthwhile one, I think, but a digression nonetheless.

So *tiyara* — the birds flying right or left — is at its root superstition, but it is also more than that, because in Arabic the word for a pessimist derives from the same concept: the pessimist is the one who always sees the worst in any situation, who will say about any plan, "It will never work." The root of *tiyara* is the ignorance that all affairs belong to Allah alone. For the Muslim, this means having a *husn al-zann* — a good opinion — of Allah. The non-Muslim has a bad opinion of Allah; the Muslim has a good one. That is the essential difference. So you should always cultivate this good opinion of Allah.

He now moves toward *su' al-zann* — bad opinion — but before doing so I want to say one more thing about *tiyara*. There are really two types. The first — valid for people of sound intellect and normative experience — is when *al-hukm al-'adi*, empirical probability, establishes that a thing consistently causes harm. If you put your hand near a cobra, it bites you and you die. In that case, you must use your intellect; avoiding it is not *tiyara*, it is rational. If something is harmful by established norm, you must avoid it, and the scholars say that if you do not, you are not considered a person of sound reason — you are acting foolishly. There are, of course, exceptional cases: one of the *awliya* was walking with a student, they came upon a lion on the path, the student was terrified, the shaykh told him to take the high road and continued walking, and the lion did not touch him. But that is not a station available to everyone.

<!-- page 88 -->

Imam al-Qarafi, a great Maliki scholar, says that *tiyara* is a bad opinion and *tiyara* as an *action* — *'amal* — is what results from holding that bad opinion, as long as the normative experience in which harm results does not apply. Walking under a ladder, for instance. In this culture: step on a crack, you'll break your mother's back. Children grow up avoiding the cracks in the pavement. A black cat crossing your path — thirteen years of bad luck. Breaking a mirror. Knocking on wood: "I've been healthy for years" — then you knock wood. These are all *tiyara* and are in fact *haram* to act upon. If the Shari'a gives us specific *adhkar* and practices, those are different — they come from revelation, not superstition. But knocking on wood goes back to pagan tree-worship; those who venerated sacred trees would knock on them. That is the origin. Among the Arabs in the *Jahiliyya*, things like walking between two sheep were considered bad luck. Seeing an owl was an ill omen. In some cultures, a shooting star was a bad omen. All of these are forms of *tiyara*.

The Prophet ﷺ also established that no one will ever be entirely free of *tiyara* — it is part of human nature. There is a book by the skeptic Michael Shermer called *Why People Believe Weird Things*, and much of it addresses exactly this: how deeply embedded superstition is in human psychology. The *hadith* points to the same reality. People check their horoscope before leaving the house. People will not buy or sell stocks without first consulting their astrologer — a completely absurd premise, yet they believe in it and it gives them a sense of control. People consult the I Ching before making any significant decision.


Uh, there's people that do ramal in the Sahara desert, and there are those who flip coins or toss them into wishing wells. All of these things are really part of human nature.

So when the Prophet ﷺ mentioned that one of three things will inevitably come to you in life, one of them was ill omens — tiyara. And he said the way to cure it is this: if you feel that bad omen rising in you, just keep going. So if, in this culture, you're about to walk under a ladder and something stirs in your heart, you just keep walking — that's how you break it. And if a plant falls on your head — no, I'm just kidding. But that really is the way you break it: you ignore it and keep moving.

A friend of mine who has strong iman told me about someone who claimed to be a wali — a claim my friend didn't think was warranted — and he said something about it. The followers of this man turned to my friend and said, "Don't you know? If he makes dua against you, he can destroy you." And my friend replied, "Go ahead — make dua against me right now. I want you to." What he was trying to show them was that this is not how Muslims behave. Real Muslims don't make dua against one another, and a true wali certainly doesn't. And if he truly is a wali, Allah can bring you difficulty without him even intending it.

There is a Sheikh I know in Mauritania, Muhammad Zayn — whom some of you have met — who is from the family of the Prophet ﷺ. He is a deeply righteous man who would never willingly harm anyone. But it is well known that people who speak ill of him or bear bad attitudes toward him tend to find trouble. So you don't want to be careless with the awliya. At the same time, as the Moroccans say: don't belittle any Muslim, because he might be a wali of Allah. In other words, be generally mindful of harming any Muslim — it is simply not a good thing to do.

So that is the matter of tiyara. Now the next disease is having a bad opinion of others — what is called zann.

<!-- page 93 -->

Zann is suspicion, conjecture, opinion formed without any supporting facts. The worst form of bad opinion is harboring it toward someone whose outward appearance and conduct are plainly righteous. Outward appearance was something the early Muslims actually considered significant. While they also cautioned against dismissing those who may look unimpressive — because sometimes Allah veils His friends behind an ordinary exterior — they recognized what the Arabs call wasma: the quality of being wasim, someone whose face carries a kind of nobility. When an Arab delegate came to the Prophet ﷺ and then departed, the companions asked what he thought of him. He said, "His face is not the face of a liar." This is what is called firasa — the capacity to discern goodness or corruption in a person's countenance. So forming a bad opinion of someone who possesses this wasma, who outwardly presents as a righteous person, is a particularly serious form of zann.

Allah says that some forms of zann are wrong action. This is important because ordinarily we are not held accountable for what merely passes through the heart — but there is a hiba of the heart, a form of backbiting that occurs entirely within the chest: entertaining a bad and unfounded opinion of someone, and it is written against you. This is called hiba talqal. It is unlike the passing thoughts that cross the mind without being invited — you are not accountable for those. What does incur accountability is what the scholars call azimah: the thought that settles and takes root. We will go into these levels in more detail when we reach the section on qawatir, because there are degrees — hadith al-nafs, azimah — and azimah is the highest degree, the one written against you. When it runs deep in your heart such that it changes how you treat someone, it becomes hiba talqal — a form of backbiting in which no second person is even present. It is entirely between you and your own heart. And that is not permissible. It is written against you.

So the condition for blameworthy zann is that your heart has reached a verdict — azimah — judging a person based purely on suspicion, without a dalil, without any evidence that actually warrants that judgment. If someone asks you, "Why don't you like that person?" and you say, "I just feel it in my heart — something is wrong," that is a different matter we will come to shortly. But consider the hadith: iyyakum wa al-zann, fa-inna al-zann akdhabu al-hadith — "Beware of zann, for zann is the most deceptive of speech." If you tell someone, "So-and-so is a really bad person," and they ask, "What's your proof?" and you say, "I just know" — that is akdhabu al-hadith. That is a lie, because you have no dalil. Our sharia is built on the principle: umirtu an ahkuma bi al-zawahir — "I was commanded to judge by outward matters." So it is essential to look at behavior, at what is manifest.

Then he says: la ithma fi al-shakk wa adam al-istinad li-sababin fa-adam yakun mujarradan — there is nothing wrong with having doubts about someone, or even a bad opinion of him, if that opinion is grounded in sound reason and not merely baseless. This is a critical distinction.

The difference between shakk and zann is this: zann is when your internal scales have tipped — you have made a judgment. Shakk is when both pans are level. Is he good? Unknown. Is he bad? Unknown. That is shakk: "I simply don't know, and I must be cautious." That is not haram. Zann is when you come to a conclusion — without proof — that the person is bad. Everyone understand the difference? This distinction will genuinely serve you in life.

So how do we deal with people about whom we feel genuine doubt? First, if the person is a fasiq — and a fasiq is someone who commits major sins openly, or who is habitually careless about minor ones, consistently and without remorse — then Allah says: if a fasiq comes to you with a report, verify it before you act on it. You cannot take what such a person says at face value.

Now, if what you have is mere shakk — not certainty, just unresolved doubt — what Sidi Ahmad Zarruq advises is excellent counsel, and though it comes from the ninth century it is even more pertinent today: do not entrust your deen, your family, or your wealth to anyone until you have tested them a thousand thousand times. That is of course mubalaghah — hyperbole — but his point is plain: test people. Observe whether they are genuine and sincere. If you don't yet know, don't trust.

One of the poets said: "A good opinion in these days is foolishness — think the worst of people and stay on guard." And truly, this is what the ulama say: when the times are full of corruption, the sound position is to be cautious and distrustful of people — not from zann, not from having made a judgment, but simply from prudence. You don't extend trust until it has been earned, because so many people lack taqwa. They will smile at your face, speak beautifully of their own faith and virtue, and then cheat you. It happens all the time.

That is also why the ulama say that in corrupt times, it becomes obligatory for those distributing zakat to investigate the circumstances of claimants. They cannot simply hand it over. When people are generally righteous, you take them at their word. But when the times are corrupt and someone comes saying, "Brother, I have five children and no money," you bear a responsibility to verify the claim. The claimants cannot be upset or indignant about this — they must accept it. It is not about distrusting them personally. It is, as they say in this culture, that the bad ones have ruined it for the good ones. The response has to be: "Unfortunately, because this is an amanah from Allah, we have to verify. It isn't that we doubt you — this is a blanket policy, for that reason. And we are sorry that the reality has come to this."

So that is the matter of bad opinion of others. Having doubts about someone — shakk — is fine. Having a bad opinion of someone when there is ample evidence he is truly a bad person is simply common sense. On the other hand: when people make tawba, do not hold their past over them. One of the worst things you can do to a person is remind them of the evil they have left behind. Let people move forward. Do not cling to their past, because it is deeply unfair.

<!-- page 94 -->

And there is another dimension here. A good opinion of Allah and a good opinion of the servants of Allah — no mumin was ever given anything better than these two. Generally, it is praiseworthy to have a good opinion: certainly of Allah, and generally of His servants. Ibn Ammar said that no servant was ever harmed by a good opinion, whereas a bad opinion can injure you. And in our sharia the principle is bara'at al-asliyya — people are presumed innocent until there is real cause to conclude otherwise.

The other point of supreme importance is the opinion one holds of Allah. There is a hadith reported by al-Tabarani: ana inda zanni abdi bi, fa-liyazunna abdi bi ma sha'a — "I am as My servant thinks of Me, so let My servant think of Me whatever he will." Another hadith clarifies it further: ana inda zanni abdi bi — fa-man zanna bi khayran, wajada khayran; wa-man zanna bi sharran, wajada sharran — "I am as My servant thinks of Me. Whoever thinks well of Me finds good; whoever thinks ill of Me finds ill." This hadith is the key to understanding another, found in the Forty Hadith of Imam al-Nawawi, in which it is stated that every servant receives what he deserves, and then: fa-man wajada khayran fa-liyahmadi Llah — "If he finds good, let him thank Allah" — wa-man wajada ghayra dhalika fa-la yalumanna illa nafsah — "and if he finds otherwise, let him blame no one but his own soul."

The underlying idea is that if you are a true mumin, everything ultimately works in your favor. The affair of the mumin is all good. Even the bad that befalls you — the true mumin believes it carries a reason, and that there is good within it. This is a fundamentally different worldview from that of the muta'a'yis, the pessimist, who falls into what might be called sakhtat al-qadar — railing against destiny, saying, "Why is this happening to me? I don't deserve this." The Prophet ﷺ himself said something to this effect: "If You are not angry with me, I do not care" — and he acknowledged that ease would naturally be more comfortable, but that the difficulty of a hard moment is itself a good thing for the mumin. That is what it means to have a good opinion of Allah and a good opinion of others.

The next disease is called ujb.

Imam al-Ghazali says the distinction between ujb and kibr is that kibr requires two people. Kibr is arrogance — it needs the mutakabbir, the one who is arrogant, and the one toward whom the arrogance is directed. If Zayd comes to Amr and says, "You insignificant fool — why didn't you do what I told you?" Zayd is the mutakabbir and Amr is the one being looked down upon. Ujb, by contrast, is like kibr, except that it requires no second person. The person afflicted with ujb is simply impressed with himself. If he has a mirror, he is content — he looks and is genuinely dazzled by how wonderful and magnificent he is.

He defines ujb as follows: you magnify and exalt the ni'ma — the blessing — while forgetting the one who gave it. In this culture, if someone is told, "You're so beautiful," and they respond, "Thank you," as though they had done something to bring it about — that is a strange thing, if you think about it carefully. Perhaps they spent a great deal on reconstructive surgery, but even then they should be thanking the surgeon, not congratulating themselves. The point is: if Allah made someone beautiful, they should be grateful to Allah. The mun'im — the one who bestowed the gift — is Allah, not the person themselves. They did nothing to bring it about.

And even when real human effort is involved — when someone has a genuine talent that took years to develop — that talent, in its raw form, was still a gift from Allah. The labor it took to develop it was itself made possible by tawfiq from Allah. You see this clearly within families.

<!-- page 95 -->

Consider beautiful calligraphy. It looks effortless to the observer. I once sat with Sidi Muhammad Zakariyya — the well-known American calligrapher — and watched him work. He made it look so natural that I thought, "I could do that." They say the mark of a master is precisely this: he makes it look so easy that you believe you could do it yourself — but you couldn't. Imam al-Jurjani said much the same of the master of rhetoric: when he speaks, he speaks with such ease and fluency that the listeners think, "I could do that." They could not.

What you have to bear in mind when you see Sidi Muhammad Zakariyya execute his calligraphy is the years of patient, painstaking repetition that preceded it — practicing waws and ya's and ba's, writing out passages again and again. You see the finished work and say, "Subhan Allah." And that is a gift from Allah. Now if someone given such a gift were to say, "Look at all my accomplishments — I did this myself, I pulled myself up by my own effort" — that is ujb of the worst kind. And there are countless people in the world who think exactly this: that whatever they have achieved, they achieved alone. "I built myself. I earned this." That is ujb al-nafs.

The mumin, by contrast, understands: if Allah gave you the tawfiq to be disciplined, that too was from Allah.


<!-- page N --> markers must be preserved — but I notice the INPUT contains **no** `<!-- page N -->` comments (only the `<!-- context-overlap -->` block, which I must not re-emit). I will refine the prose and output it cleanly, with no page markers to preserve since none appear in this window's content.

One person grows up with discipline; another, in the same household with the same environmental factors, does not. The disciplined one goes on to master their studies, graduates with honors — and all of it is from Allah. As Imam al-Razi says, Allah is the maker of every maker and every made thing. Allah created the one who built the airplane and the airplane itself. Allah created the one who designed the computer and the computer itself. Once you truly grasp that, the urge to be impressed with yourself dissolves.

And then there is this: above everyone who knows something, there is always someone who knows more. This is the lesson Musa, alayhi al-salam, had to learn. When he was asked whether anyone on earth was more knowledgeable than he was, he said he did not think so. So Allah sent him to al-Khadir — who was not a prophet — and made him a student. Even then, he did not prove to be as patient a student as he could have been. The Prophet, sallAllahu alayhi wa sallam, said that had Musa not asked those questions, he would have learned far more from al-Khadir. He did not absorb everything he might have, yet he was still taught. And the greatest lesson he took away was this: there is always someone who knows more than you, so never forget that.

We must choose our words carefully when speaking of prophets. I am not saying Musa was a poor student — he was, I am sure, extraordinary. But he had a lesson to learn, and Allah taught it to him through al-Khadir, alayhi al-salam.

Now, regarding vanity — the reason I translated *'ujb* as vanity is that, in the dictionary, vanity is defined as excessive pride in one's appearance or accomplishments, which maps closely onto the Arabic. The root *ajiba* means to wonder or marvel at something; *'ujb* is to marvel at oneself or one's own achievements. The word *vanity* itself derives from the Latin *vanus*, meaning empty — and so the very source of our vanity is empty and will one day vanish.

The Romans understood this. When a general like Caesar held a victory parade through Rome, riding through the city on a chariot while crowds cheered, a slave would walk alongside him holding a laurel wreath over his head and repeating: *all is vanity*. That slave was there to remind the triumphant general — it is all vanity. This is an ancient wisdom, a *hikma*, a reminder that all these accomplishments will eventually be forgotten. Charles Lindbergh is a case in point: he received an enormous ticker-tape parade in New York City, and within ten years the American government considered him a criminal for his Nazi sympathies. The hero had fallen.

There is a story — I believe it was written by James Thurber, who wrote *The Secret Life of Walter Mitty* — about a man who saves a child, and the reporters rush in to make him a hero. It turns out he is actually a disreputable person who simply wants to profit from the incident. As he stands at the window watching the adoring crowds below, one of the reporters pushes him out. The point is that there is always another side. No matter how much good a person does, there will always be detractors — or the glory itself will simply prove hollow.

Think of the Yankees parading through New York, celebrated as the greatest team in the world, while the city itself is in moral, social, and ethical disarray. That is *'ujb* at a collective scale — *al-mu'ajaba bi-l-nafs* — and it is a deeply troubling condition.

He then notes: knowing that you are incapable, that you can neither produce any benefit nor ward off any harm on your own, should be enough to strip away all vanity. Vanity originates from nothing other than ignorance of two truths: that Allah alone is the fashioner and giver of blessings, and that you are incapable of anything by yourself. Forgetting those two things is how *'ujb* takes hold.

This is a disease one must always be on guard against — especially when one does something well. And it is particularly relevant for those whom Allah has blessed with outward, physical beauty. Becoming vain about one's appearance is a dangerous trap, one that both men and women can fall into, though it is an especially acute danger for women.

The next disease he addresses is *ghishsh* — fraud. He defines it as concealing some fault or harm, whether religious or worldly, even when the other party is a member of a protected minority or someone who has a treaty with the Muslims. Others have interpreted it as adorning something that has no real benefit, or making an evil appear to be good.

There is a hadith in which the Prophet, sallAllahu alayhi wa sallam, said: *whoever tricks us is not from us.* A Muslim is not someone who engages in fraud. And fraud, he notes, can occur in worldly affairs as well as religious ones. In Islamic law, it is prohibited to sell something while concealing a known defect. If you sell a car and genuinely do not know that the engine is in poor condition, that is one matter — but if you know it, you are obligated to disclose it. Failing to do so is a disease of the heart; that is precisely where it originates.

I have noted in the commentary that this disease is conspicuous in modern advertising, which to my mind suggests that only a diseased heart could produce that kind of work comfortably. It also has a historical precedent in the sophists of ancient Greece. The sophists worshipped rhetoric and were, in effect, the first historical relativists. Relativism holds that there is no right and wrong — might makes right, and the most persuasive argument wins, regardless of its truth. That is a thoroughly modern disease as well, but the sophists were its original practitioners. They mastered rhetoric and believed the only thing that truly mattered was being convincing. Truth or falsehood was beside the point; what mattered was getting what you wanted through the art of persuasion.

For the Muslim intellectual tradition, by contrast, rhetoric is the art of presenting truth in the most compelling way — not of dressing up falsehood. It is a fundamentally different worldview. The sophists, by their own admission, set out to make the worse cause appear to be the better one. They believed that men are either wolves or sheep: smart wolves eat the dumb sheep. This is, unfortunately, a widely held view in modern corporate culture — that competition justifies whatever means are available to you. The word *sophisticated* itself traces back to the Greek root that gave us *sophist* — people who thought themselves clever and wise.

Next week, he is going to address what I think is one of the most consequential diseases in the entire book — *al-'ujb al-kabir*, or rather *al-riya'* — and he spends nearly as much time on it as he does on some of the other major diseases. We will take that up next session.

---

Are there any questions?

*On al-Khadir and Musa:* Yes, Musa met al-Khadir before he was sent to the Bani Isra'il. Al-Khadir was a prophet — that is a separate matter.

*On bad opinion versus hatred:* Having a bad opinion of someone means thinking ill of them without evidence. Hatred is something different — it is an actual emotion, a feeling. We will get into that in more detail when we reach that chapter. It becomes truly dangerous when you act on it. The Arabic terms are *su' al-zann* and *bughd*.

*On fraud toward non-Muslims:* What he is saying is that *ghishsh* — fraud — applies even in dealings with a *dhimmi*, a non-Muslim living under the protection of the Muslim state. You cannot cheat a non-Muslim simply because they are not Muslim. You are obliged to be upright in your transactions with everyone.

*On reminding people of past wrongs:* Generally speaking, if something happened in the past and a person has moved on from it, continually reminding them of it is not fair, because it closes the door to change. If they are repeating the behavior, that is a different matter — but for past mistakes they have left behind, it is better to let it go.

*On the word* yusra *and the grammatical point about* 'usr: There is a tradition of saying that one difficulty will not overcome two eases, and the grammatical basis for this is in the Quranic ayah. Allah says: *inna ma'a al-'usri yusra* — and then repeats it. The word *al-'usr* carries the definite article *al-*, which in the second instance is what grammarians call *'ahd* — it refers back to the same *'usr* mentioned before. So it is the same singular difficulty appearing both times. But *yusra*, the ease, is indefinite — *nakira* — in both instances. Because it is a new indefinite noun each time, it counts as two separate eases. The lesson is that with any one difficulty come two eases.

*On energy and vibrations between people:* I think there is something real to the experience of feeling uplifted or dragged down by the people around you. We would express it in our tradition as someone having a good or bad soul, or being a good or bad person. The Arabic *munsjam* literally means to be in the flow, in harmony — and you can see the analogy in music: a cacophonous sound creates a vibration that hurts the ear, while something melodious has a positive effect. So the sense that certain people carry a certain energy is not entirely foreign to Islamic thought, even if we frame it differently.

All of us have had the experience of being around someone who is deeply depressed and finding it difficult not to start feeling depressed ourselves. People do seem to have fields — auras, if you want to use that word — that are either uplifting, draining, or simply neutral.

I would also say this: feeling uncomfortable around someone is not the same as judging them to be a bad person. One of my teachers, Sheikh Hamid, told me a story from his student days. There was a fellow student who liked him very much and always wanted to be near him, but he simply did not feel a connection with that person. He went to his teacher feeling guilty about it, and his teacher said: souls are arranged in ranks. Some souls are simply not harmonious with one another. It does not mean either party is bad — it is just that some people you do not feel a natural affinity with, while with others you feel an immediate and powerful connection.

Some of the scholars say this has to do with our pre-worldly existence — the gathering of the *arwah*, the spirits, before they entered this world. The closer your soul was to another soul on that day, the more affinity you will feel toward that person in this life. The further away, the more distant you will feel. This would explain why you can meet someone for the first time and feel as though you have known them all your life. I felt that immediately with Sheikh Abdallah al-Qadi — the moment I met him, it was as though I had always known him. Other connections develop slowly, over time. There is wisdom in both kinds.

*On distrust as distinct from bad opinion:* Having a bad opinion of a specific person without evidence is one thing. Being generally cautious and guarded as a disposition is something else. The scholars say that in difficult times, a degree of wariness is actually wisdom — it is being on your guard, not maligning anyone in particular.


Don't become paranoid — that's a psychological condition to be avoided. Seriously. You don't want to be the person who says, "Do you hear what he said? Did you see how he looked at me?" That kind of thinking is an affliction.

The next section covers غضب, which means anger. He opens it by describing anger as the swelling ocean of all these diseases of the heart. Just coming to its shore — not even venturing into it — you will see things that astonish you: waves overflowing, an expanse beyond reckoning. Say of it what you will, without constraint, because it is a truly remarkable thing.

There is a hadith — one with some weakness in its chain, though its meaning is important — in which a man came to the Prophet ﷺ from the front and asked, "O Messenger of Allah, tell me: what is the best thing for drawing near to Allah?" The Prophet ﷺ replied, "Husn al-khuluq" — good character. The man then came from the right side and asked the same question, and received the same answer. He came from the left and asked again, and again the Prophet ﷺ said, "Husn al-khuluq." Finally, the man came from behind him and asked once more. This time the Prophet ﷺ said, "Awa lam tafqah? La taghdab" — "Have you not understood? Do not get angry."

In another hadith, a man asked the Prophet ﷺ what the worst thing was that could occur with respect to Allah. The Prophet ﷺ said, "His anger." The man asked how they could avoid it, and the Prophet ﷺ replied: do not get angry yourself. If you do not want Allah to be angry with you, do not give in to anger. "Kaytha ma tudinu tudan" — as you conduct yourself, so will it be shown to you.

Then there is the famous hadith in which a man said, "Ya Rasulallah, give me some advice." The Prophet ﷺ replied, "La taghdab" — do not get angry. The man said he wanted more advice. The Prophet ﷺ again said, "La taghdab." The man asked a third time, and three times the Prophet ﷺ repeated, "La taghdab." This hadith, the scholars say, is the controlling hadith — the key to good character.

Now, when the Prophet ﷺ said "La taghdab," the scholars of Allah — our 'ulama, including Imam al-Nawawi — explained that he did not mean: do not feel anger. He did not mean: suppress the emotion entirely. Because the Messenger of Allah ﷺ himself said, "Innama ana basharun aghabu mithlakum" — I am a human being, and I get angry just as you do. His anger was visible in his face; a vein in his forehead would appear when he was angry.

So the Prophet ﷺ was not instructing us to eliminate the feeling of anger, because anger is not inherently negative. It is, in fact, essential to the human character. Many of the world's injustices would never be opposed if people lacked the capacity for anger. What the Prophet ﷺ meant, according to the scholars, was: do not let your anger take control of you. Remain in command of your anger.

Some of the 'ulama have likened anger to a hunting dog: if you do not train it, it will not bring you what you need. It must be disciplined. Without anger, injustice would go unchallenged — people could act with impunity and no one would object. That is not what Allah wants. He wants us to become angry, but not for our own sake. He wants our anger to be for His sake. This is the same transformation that applies to the other diseases of the heart: you redirect them so they work for you rather than against you.

The reason the author calls anger the ocean is that it drowns many people. They sink into the sea of wrath, and it carries them toward destruction and despair.

<!-- page 99 -->

Now, before moving into the detailed discussion, I want to make a detour — though it is not really a detour, because it is directly related to all of this.

Imam al-Ghazali identified four essential qualities of the human soul. This framework also appears in earlier traditions and is not unique to Islam, but Muslim scholars recognized its truth and adopted it. Fakhr al-Din al-Razi adopted it. Imam Raghib al-Isfahani adopted it in his book on akhlaq. Imam al-Ghazali adopted it as well.

The model examines the human soul and recognizes four fundamental powers. The first is called Quwwat al-'Aql — what the Western tradition calls the rational soul. It is the power within the human being that is related to what a person knows and understands. The second is Quwwat al-Ghadab — the irascible soul — the element that relates to emotions, whose foundation is anger. Imam Jalal al-Din al-Rumi once observed that the wisdom behind the prohibition of wine is that most people carry anger at their core, and when they drink, that root is exposed. People become belligerent; their inherent anger surfaces. He said it was as though Allah, in His mercy, veiled people from exposing what lies at their core. And indeed, for most people, Quwwat al-Ghadab sits at the very root of the soul.

The third is Quwwat al-Shahwa — the appetitive soul, the soul related to desire and appetite, to one's shahwani nature. The fourth is Quwwat al-'Adl, which, according to Imam al-Ghazali, is the power that harmonizes the other three so that the human being functions in a healthy and balanced way — none of them thrown out of equilibrium.

These four powers correspond to what both the Western and Islamic traditions call the cardinal virtues. The Muslims call them ummahat al-fada'il: wisdom, courage, temperance, and justice — or in Arabic, hikmah, shaja'ah, 'iffah, and 'adl.

When the rational soul is in balance, the result is hikmah. Allah ﷻ says: وَمَن يُؤۡتَ ٱلۡحِكۡمَةَ فَقَدۡ أُوتِيَ خَيۡرًا كَثِيرًا — "Whoever is given wisdom has been given much good." Imam al-Ghazali says that hikmah is the mean between baladah — stupidity — and khidha' — cunning. If the rational soul is deficient, the result is foolishness: people do stupid things. If it is excessive, the result is manipulation — the intellect is turned toward trickery and exploitation of others. This excess, incidentally, tends to be an essential qualification for becoming a CEO: that surplus of Quwwat al-'Ilm directed toward manipulation.

Shaja'ah arises when the irascible soul — one's ghadab — is under proper control. It, too, is a mean between two extremes: tahawwur on one side, which is impetuousness, irrational behavior, total loss of self-control in which a person acts in rage and then regrets it; and jubn on the other side, which is cowardice — insufficient ghadab, too much fear to act. One extreme is deficiency (naqhs), the other excess (ziyada). The balanced state between them is shaja'ah. This connects to the hadith: "Khayr al-umur awsatuha," the best of matters is the middle course, and to the Quranic description: "Wa kadhalika ja'alnakum ummatan wasata" — We made you a middle community, a people of hikmah, a people of shaja'ah, possessing these virtues in their proper balance.

'Iffah is the middle way of Quwwat al-Shahwa. It lies between jumud — a kind of rigidity or indifference, where a person has no appetite, no energy, no interest in the opposite sex, no engagement with their shahwa — and fisq, where a person plunges entirely into indulging their appetites and becomes enslaved to the appetitive soul. Jumud is itself unhealthy in the shari'ah; the Prophet ﷺ disapproved of it. The excess, fisq, is the opposite danger. The balance between them is 'iffah, and 'iffah is explicitly affirmed in the shari'ah. Allah ﷻ says that ignorant people, observing poor Muslims who carry themselves with this quality, assume they are wealthy — "yahsabuhum aghniya'a mina al-ta'affuf" — because their dignity and self-restraint make them appear self-sufficient. They are not out scrambling after money through trickery and deception, because their inner state is balanced.

As for 'adl — justice — it does not occupy a mean between two things. It has an opposite: zulm. Justice is simply the opposite of oppression. When the other three powers are in balance, you are just. When they are out of balance, you become a zalim — oppressing both yourself and others. If you completely indulge the appetitive soul, you have become zalimun li-nafsih, oppressing your own soul by subjecting it to what it was not created to do. Allah says: كُلُواْ وَٱشۡرَبُواْ وَلَا تُسۡرِفُوٓاْ — "Eat and drink, but do not be excessive." The middle. Eat what you need. It is now, in fact, a science: based on your body size, you can calculate almost to the gram how many calories you require each day. Eating significantly more or less than that is a form of zulm.

<!-- page 100 -->

And Allah, in His mercy, has placed a natural indicator within the human being: when you are in a state of fitrah and health, you will feel satiated at the right point. To go beyond that point is zulm against your own soul, because your stomach has a right — a haqq. The Prophet ﷺ said: "Khayr wi'a'in mala'ahu ibn Adam, ba'dahu" — the worst vessel the son of Adam fills is his stomach. The haqq of the stomach is that a person leave one-third for food, one-third for water, and one-third for air. That is the 'adalah of eating. To exceed that is to go beyond its proper limit.

The same principle applies to the sexual appetite. If it is expressed naturally and within its proper limits, the human being remains healthy. If he transgresses those limits and goes beyond them — and this is part of what might be called the Viagra culture of our time — the result is excess. As a person grows older, that dimension of the self naturally diminishes, and the soul should be turning toward the akhirah. But for someone who does not hold the akhirah as a concern, he continues pursuing shahawat that he should be moving away from. What was once a physical shahwa has now become a psychological shahwa — the body no longer produces it, but the mind insists on it. That is the imbalance.

These, then, are the elements of a balanced human being.

By the grace of Allah, Imam al-Ghazali says, the only human being who ever perfected the balance of all four was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. He alone achieved complete equilibrium in all three of the foundational powers, which resulted in his being the most just of all men. His character is essentially 'adalah. His very nature is 'adl. This is why the more you emulate him in his behavior, the closer you come to being a truly just human being.

For this reason, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ did not fast every day — that would have been a deficiency in 'iffah, a withholding from himself of what was due. Nor did he go to the other extreme. He said: "Ba'da ayyamin asomu wa ba'daha aftur" — some days I fast and some days I eat. That is the balance.

The Prophet ﷺ did not become angry when it was inappropriate. He became angry when it was appropriate, and that was his shaja'ah. He became angry when a situation demanded a mawqif, a stand. Imam al-Ghazali says that the 'adl of shaja'ah is to be angry at the right time, in the right place, toward the right person, and with the right measure. How does one learn to do that? By studying the Messenger of Allah ﷺ — because he never let his anger get the better of him to the point of praying against people. He never allowed it to lead him to strike anyone unjustly. He controlled it. And that is shaja'ah.

The Prophet ﷺ also possessed 'ilm — knowledge — beyond any human being, and it was perfectly balanced knowledge. There is nothing foolish in his conduct, nothing imprudent, nothing that suggests any deficiency of wisdom. Nor did he deploy his intellectual gifts for cleverness, trickery, or accumulation of dunya. He used them for the sake of Allah, where and as it was appropriate.

Now, one of the things Imam al-Suyuti says in one of his books — and he notes, "I have not seen anyone make this observation before me" — is that, reflecting on what had been said about Abu Bakr being the most courageous of the ummah after the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, he began to think about that quality. This reflection led him to give a talk he called "Abu Bakr and Courage," exploring this virtue. But then something else struck him: each of the Khulafa' al-Rashidun embodies one of these four virtues as his dominant characteristic.

Hikmah is most clearly Sayyidina 'Ali, because he is Bab al-'Ilm — the gate of knowledge. Shaja'ah is Abu Bakr. And everyone knows 'Umar as the embodiment of 'adl. The only one that might give one pause is Sayyidina 'Uthman — but when you look up the word 'iffah in al-Fayruzabadi's Qamus, immediately after its definition he writes: "wa al-'Afan huwa al-'Afif Mughalah" — and al-'Afan is the one who is intensely 'afif. And his name is 'Uthman ibn 'Affan. That was what he was known for. Consider what he was doing on the day he was killed: fasting. His entire state was 'iffah — mastery over the nafs. Even the angels were shy in his presence, because of his profound 'iffah.

So each one of them embodies one of these four virtues as his most prominent quality — while possessing all four. 'Ali had shaja'ah; 'Ali had 'adl; 'Ali had hikmah; 'Ali had 'iffah. But his dominant characteristic was hikmah — that was his gift. Abu Bakr had 'ilm; he had shujja'ah; he had 'iffah; he had 'adl. But what stands out in his mawaqif is shaja'ah, because courage is defined precisely as thabat — remaining steadfast when hearts around you waver. Look across the events of the Seerah, and it was Abu Bakr who remained thabit when everyone else wavered. That is why the Prophet ﷺ said: "I presented Islam to everyone, and the only one who did not waver was Abu Bakr." His heart was shaja'ah. It was his nature.

Now there is another remarkable observation about all of this. The Sirat al-Mustaqim — the straight path — literally means the upright path. Istiqamah is i'tidal. In the prayer, your prayer is only valid when you are mu'tadil — and mu'tadil in Arabic means both upright and perfectly balanced. So the Sirat al-Mustaqim is the perfectly balanced path. And there are two ways to deviate from it.


You either deviate through deficiency or excess — those are the only two ways to stray from the path. You are either falling short of what is obligatory, or you are going too far.

These two deviations correspond to the Jews and the Christians. The Christians went to excess. They went beyond what they were commanded.

<!-- page 122 -->

Rahbaniyyah — monastic renunciation — they invented it; We did not prescribe it for them. They sought it as a means of drawing closer to Allah's pleasure, yet they could not even fulfill what they themselves had initiated. That is the very nature of excess: it cannot be sustained. By its own definition, it is unsustainable. That is why excess is so dangerous. The Prophet ﷺ warned: *Iyyakum wal-ghuluww* — beware of extremism — for Allah does not grow weary until you grow weary. When you push beyond the balance, you exhaust yourself. It is not sustainable.

So the Christian, archetypally speaking, pursues an extreme of *'iffah* — continence — that tips over into something other than its opposite, but something distorted: an excess that has lost its equilibrium. And what always follows an extreme? The pendulum swings to the other side. Drive something far enough in one direction and it snaps back to its polar opposite. The Christian ascetic tradition pushed all the way to one extreme of renunciation, and then flipped entirely — landing in modern American society, where the culture is all *shahwah*, all appetite and indulgence. They arrived on these shores as Puritans. That was the original impulse of the pilgrims: to escape a European society they saw as given over to *shahwah*, to build a pure religious community. And they ended up producing the opposite — a civilization defined by extreme appetite. This is precisely what happens with these polarities.

You can see the same dynamic with *shuja'* — courage. The Christian ideal says: turn the other cheek. Yet, historically, Christians have killed more human beings than any other religious or secular civilization. Now they possess weapons of mass destruction. The technologies of industrial warfare — rockets, tanks, missiles — emerged largely from this same civilization. This is the fruit of going to extremes: the ideal inverts into its opposite.

The same pattern holds with justice. Push justice to an extreme and it collapses into manipulation. The law becomes a vehicle for trickery — lawyers finding every loophole so that those with resources get whatever they want. Justice becomes its opposite: oppression. Knowledge, which was originally claimed as the servant of human flourishing — read the original Enlightenment thinkers; Bacon wrote explicitly about how Christian morality would keep technology in check and make it a force for good — that knowledge becomes a tool for getting what one wants at others' expense. This is the Roman disease: justice for us, oppression for everyone else.

<!-- page 123 -->

That is one form of imbalance. The other is the way of the Jews — the way of deficiency. This is the very complaint Moses, peace be upon him, raised with the Prophet ﷺ on the night of the *Isra'*: his people could not even manage two prayers a day. That was all they were asked, and they could not do it. They said to Moses: *You and your Lord go and fight — we will sit here.* So *naqs* — deficiency — runs through that entire mode.

Now, these are archetypes. I want to be clear: do not take these as absolute, rigid categories. They are lenses for understanding patterns in the human soul. Within the Jewish fold you will find the Christian phenomenon, and within the Christian fold you will find the Jewish phenomenon. These archetypes can appear in a Hindu, in a Buddhist, in anyone. They are ways of reading the human condition.

This is why the one described in *al-Maghḍūbi 'alayhim* — those who have incurred wrath — is the one who possesses knowledge but goes astray, becomes *ḍāll*. And it is worth noting that the Arabic root *bāl* also carries the meaning of falling in love — *bala* means he fell in love — because it is precisely this excessive, consuming love that leads people astray, the love of Qays, of Majnun.

The Muslim is meant to stand in the middle between these two poles. We can think of those poles as *haybah* — awe, majesty, the quality of justice — which is the root of the Mosaic tradition. Moses, peace be upon him, is the lawgiver. His tradition is built on *'adl*, on justice. And on the other side is *raḥmah* — mercy — which is the root impulse of the Christian tradition. The Christian understanding is that everyone is forgiven through God's mercy regardless of what they do, because all they perceive is mercy; they have lost sight of *haybah*. Archetypally, you will find Christians who become fixated on wrath — but the dominant Christian emphasis, if you look at what they teach, is *raḥmah*.

*Haybah* and *raḥmah* must be held in balance. You need both to walk the *Ṣirāṭ al-Mustaqīm*. You need *sharī'ah* — the law — and you need *ḥaqīqah* — the spirit of the law. The law is rules; the spirit of the law is *raḥmah*. Allah sent the *sharī'ah* as a mercy. *Mā arsalnāka illā raḥmatan lil-'ālamīn* — We sent you as nothing other than a mercy to all the worlds. What the Prophet ﷺ brought was *raḥmah*. When inward and outward are balanced — inwardly *īmān*, outwardly *islām* — they produce the *muḥsin*, the person of *iḥsān*, someone whose inner and outer states have become one integrated whole.

This framework is a genuine model for understanding what the straight path actually means. Muslims, according to Ibn Taymiyyah, *raḥimahu Allāh* — and this ultimately goes back to a hadith — deviate in one of two ways: they deviate like Jews or like Christians, toward *haybah* or toward *raḥmah*. They either go in the direction of the hair-splitting, rabbinical, tyrannical scholar — the type the Prophet ﷺ warned against: *iyyākum wa jabarāt al-'ulamā'*, beware of tyrannical scholars — or they go in the direction of the ignorant worshipper, embodied in the Christian model, or in the extreme of *taṣawwuf*: a state where everything is one, everyone is going to heaven, nothing needs to be worried about, pure peace and harmony. When you hear certain people who claim to represent *taṣawwuf* and they sound more like a New Age seminar than anything connected to Islam, that is this other extreme.

<!-- page 124 -->

The balance is to be *Ṣūfī faqīhī* — inwardly animated by the spirit of *taṣawwuf*, outwardly disciplined by *fiqh* and *sharī'ah*. This is why Imam al-Shāfi'ī said: do not be a *faqīh* without being a Sufi, and do not be a Sufi without being a *faqīh*, because one without the other either hardens the heart or leads one astray. The balance is the joining of *raḥmah* — the spirit of *taṣawwuf* — and *'adl* — the spirit of *sharī'ah*.

Now, our tradition is actually closer to the Christian tradition in one respect: the Christians emphasize *raḥmah*, and when Muslims deal with one another, *raḥmah* should predominate over *haybah*. You should be more inclined to forgive your brother than to condemn him. Forgiveness is the higher station. *Wa an ta'fū aqrabu lil-taqwā* — that you pardon is closer to *taqwā*. That is a loftier station in Allah's *dīn* than demanding justice. This is why it has been said that where love truly exists, justice becomes unnecessary — because when the love between people is so profound, no one wants retribution; no one wants to call the other to account.

The first *fitnah* among the Muslims arose precisely from this departure: *'adl* overrode *raḥmah*. Sayyidina Mu'āwiyah, *raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu*, demanded justice. Sayyidina 'Ali, *raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu*, possessed *ḥikma* — he counseled forgiveness, recognizing that insisting on strict justice at that moment would tear the *ummah* apart. The Shia position aside, we, as *Ahl al-Sunnah*, hold a good opinion of Mu'āwiyah. We believe, as with 'Ā'ishah and Zubayr, that his intention was genuinely to seek justice. But Sayyidina 'Ali was reaching for the higher expression of Islam — *raḥmah* — and that is why we consider him to have been given *tawfīq* in his *ijtihād*, while Sayyidina Mu'āwiyah was not given *tawfīq* in his. That is why his group is called *al-fī'ah al-bāghiyah* — the transgressing party. The danger of *'adl* taken to its extreme is precisely that it becomes *baghy*, oppression. They were unjust toward Sayyidina 'Ali, *raḍiya Allāhu 'anhu*. We must be careful when speaking of all of this — we are speaking of the *ṣaḥābah*, *raḍiya Allāhu 'anhum*, and we love them all, Sayyidina Mu'āwiyah included. He was a scribe of revelation; he served Islam greatly. But he made a mistake. He was a human being. He was not infallible.

<!-- page 125 -->

Now — any questions about that?

The question is about the distinction between wisdom and cleverness, between someone who appears wise and someone who genuinely is. For instance, there are people who are clever but whose cleverness does not lead them to recognize the truth. In that case, by the standard of *sharī'ah*, they are considered *jāhil* — ignorant. They only appear wise.

Take the example we mentioned earlier: 'Utbah ibn Rabī'ah, who was renowned for his sound judgment, or Abū al-Ḥakam — "the man of considered opinion" — as he was called before Islam. These were men recognized for their discernment. Even Abū Ḥudhayfa — when his father was killed at Badr and thrown into the pit, the Prophet ﷺ noticed him weeping and asked whether he was troubled by what had been done with his father. He said: *No. I am thinking of how intelligent my father was, how sound his counsel was when I was young, how I always hoped he would embrace Islam — and now I am looking at where he has ended up.* That is the example.

And I think those of us who came to Islam from outside — whose parents are not Muslim — can recognize this. My own parents have given me good counsel, and I can see in them a kind of wisdom. I can see that quality. For someone born into a Muslim family, with Muslim parents, that recognition may not come as naturally. But there are non-Muslims who genuinely possess forms of wisdom and insight. The difference is that the fully realized wisdom — balanced and complete — sees everything true as true and everything false as false. The only person who achieved that absolutely was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. But the closer one draws to him, the closer one comes to the kind of wisdom that allows one to see things as they truly are.

Does that make sense? Any other questions?

<!-- page 126 -->

Right — and others have used these qualities too. The Hellenistic tradition used them. And even modern frameworks reflect them. *Al-ḥikmatu ḍāllat al-mu'min* — wisdom is the lost property of the believer; wherever he finds it, he takes it. And you can see these qualities throughout the *dīn*. They are called cardinal virtues because all other virtues flow from them. Every virtue can be traced back to one of these four.

Let me give an example. Take generosity. What is generosity, really? What is it that a generous person can do that others cannot? Why can't other people simply give? Because they are afraid. They fear losing what they have. So the truly generous person is, at root, a courageous person. It is *shajā'ah* — courage — that enables giving, where others are held back by fear. That is why Abū Bakr was the most generous of all the Companions: when the Prophet ﷺ asked what people had brought, Abū Bakr brought everything he had. 'Umar brought half. Abū Bakr's generosity flowed from his *shajā'ah*, his magnanimity.

Take another virtue. These four are called the *ummahāt* — the matrices — of virtue. All virtues emerge from these four. Take mercy. What is mercy? Where does it really reside among the four? Mercy ultimately belongs to *ḥikma*, wisdom. Because genuine compassion — like Abū Bakr's counsel to the Prophet ﷺ to show mercy to the people — is grounded in the wisdom that Allah gives through mercy what He does not give through wrath. There is a *ḥikma* in showing mercy, a knowledge that mercy is itself the more effective and more elevated path. Work through any virtue and you will find it rooted in one of these four.

Imam al-Ghazālī goes into extraordinary depth in the *Iḥyā'* demonstrating how all the virtues derive from these four matrices. The greatest minds in human history have exhausted themselves on this question — and he truly exhausted it.

<!-- page 127 -->

So how does all of this relate to *ghaḍab* — anger? *Ghaḍab*, because it is one of these core *quwwāt* — faculties — is rooted in something essential to human nature. If you look at the brain as it has been studied, what they call the triune brain — and this is not purely a modern observation; Imam al-Ghazālī touches on something similar — there are three dominant centers. The lowest, sometimes called the R-complex or reptilian brain in evolutionary frameworks, is the appetitive center: the seat of drives and urges — hunger, sexuality, the basic impulses. This would correspond to the appetitive faculty. The midbrain is where emotions are centered, and in this model the root emotion is *ghaḍab* — anger. This is why the first emotions children display are forms of *ghaḍab*: they get angry, they cry, they throw tantrums as they grow older. They learn *no* before they learn *yes*. You can see *ghaḍab* rooted right there in the earliest stages of human development.

Then there is the higher brain — the cortex — which is where reason resides.

There is a beautiful hadith in Imam al-Tirmidhī in which the Prophet ﷺ said: love the one you love up to a measure, for one day he may become someone you dislike. The wisdom here is this: if you have shared every secret with someone and that person later turns against you — a very human experience, and one that devastates people — you are left completely exposed.


And also don't hate people to extremes, because one day those you hate might become people you love. So be balanced in both love and hate. This, again, points to the importance of emotional equilibrium.

When emotions fall out of balance, problems follow. One of the Arabs put it memorably: don't be so sweet that people eat you alive, and don't be so bitter that no one can stand you. Be balanced — that is the idea.

<!-- page 135 -->

Finally, what researchers call the new brain is the neocortex — the seat of rational thought. When all three systems function properly together, the human being is truly Adam, truly human. And this is part of the human condition: some people are excessively cerebral, living entirely in their heads, where everything is theoretical and nothing practical. Others live at the opposite extreme, like reptiles — purely reactive, with no trace of higher humanity. I once explained this in a prison, and some of the Muslim inmates responded to it powerfully. One man came up to me afterward and said, "You know that thing about the reptilian brain? There are a lot of reptiles here. These guys are like snakes, just waiting to strike." It is a striking image — people locked in a purely reactionary mode, with no humanity present. And then, of course, there are those who are overwhelmed by emotion, most commonly by anger.

I brought all of that in to put what follows in perspective. Anger is one of the great excesses of the human condition, which is why the author calls it the ocean — it really is the big one. And there are essentially three reasons people get angry.

The first relates to primal needs: food, shelter, survival, and the like. If a person does not get angry when these are threatened, he is considered ahmaq — foolish — because this is a matter of survival. If someone threatens your life, you must respond. If someone threatens your family, you must respond. That is a courage Allah loves. Allah loves shaja'a — بَلْ قَالُوا هُوَ شَجَاعٌ — and the Prophet ﷺ said, *man mata duna malihi mata shahidan*: whoever dies defending his wealth dies a martyr. That courage, that ghaybah, reflects the principle of al-amr bil-ma'ruf wa'l-nahy 'an al-munkar at its most fundamental level, and if you die doing it, you died in jihad — because al-amr bil-ma'ruf wa'l-nahy 'an al-munkar falls within the chapter of jihad. It is a type of jihad.

<!-- page 136 -->

The second type is also common to human beings, but it relates to position — to what the scholars call ru'yat al-nafs, seeing oneself. This is the anger of dignity: *I am a human being; you cannot treat me this way.* It arises from a sense of being belittled, being treated with disdain, being made to feel lesser than one deserves. Sometimes that sense is justified; sometimes it is not. Some people harbor a complete delusion about their own standing — a grandiosity of soul — and their anger flows from that distortion.

The third type is specific to certain individuals and their particular attachments. A scholar, for instance, will become angry if he sees a book mistreated. An illiterate farmer will not — he doesn't grasp what the fuss is about. But that same farmer will become furious if you throw his pitchfork carelessly, because it is his tool. So this third category of anger is tied to what a person values, what is particular to them.

Imam al-Ghazali says the first type is healthy, unless taken to an extreme where a person begins to oppress others — taking their food, seizing their shelter. That becomes wrongdoing. The second type can be healthy up to a point, but the higher path is to cease seeing oneself so centrally and not to let these things provoke anger at all. The danger lies in the opposite extreme, which is called dha'ah — a disease he will address separately — where excessive self-effacement tips into humiliation. The Prophet ﷺ said, *al-mu'minu la yudhillu nafsah*: the believer does not humiliate himself. And Allah says of the believers that they possess 'izzah before the disbelievers — وَلِلَّهِ الْعِزَّةُ وَلِرَسُولِهِ وَلِلْمُؤْمِنِينَ — and رُحَمَاءُ بَيْنَهُمْ. You do not present yourself before a non-Muslim in a posture of meek, Uriah Heep–style obsequiousness. You carry yourself with 'izzah. It is with fellow believers that you practice softness and yielding.

<!-- page 137 -->

And according to Sidi Ahmad Zarruq, if a non-Muslim says something to you that does not touch your deen — not mocking your faith or your practice — you simply let it pass. وَإِذَا خَاطَبَهُمُ الْجَاهِلُونَ قَالُوا سَلَامًا: when the ignorant address them, they say "peace." Don't get angry over things that bring no benefit. All they are doing is pushing buttons. This is why Allah says: فَاصْبِرْ عَلَىٰ مَا يَقُولُونَ وَاهْجُرْهُمْ هَجْرًا جَمِيلًا — be patient with what they say and leave them gracefully. Turn away. Don't let it consume you. Let it go. There is no benefit in it — none at all.

The fourth type involves ghayrah. When the Prophet ﷺ spoke about this, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas رضي الله عنه declared his own jealous protectiveness, and the Prophet ﷺ responded that he himself has more ghayrah than Sa'd, and that Allah has more ghayrah than he does. In other words, ghayrah — often translated as jealousy, though it is better understood as a protective ardor for what is one's own — is a good sign. It is the impulse that moves a Muslim when he sees his religion being threatened: he becomes ghayur for the deen; he wants to defend it. That is a positive anger. And Allah has made ghayrah part of human nature in order to protect lineage — al-nasab. Without it, men would lose their protective care for their families, and society would unravel through adultery and confusion of bloodlines. Ghayrah in a man toward his wife is healthy and natural, as long as it does not tip into oppression or groundless suspicion. And the ghayrah a woman feels is equally natural — Allah has made it so, and indeed it is permitted. Very few women are entirely free from it, so it is not considered a fault.

<!-- page 138 -->

Now the author says this disease has two cures. The first prevents anger from arising in the first place. The second suppresses it once it has come. Allah praises in the Quran وَالْكَاظِمِينَ الْغَيْظَ وَالْعَافِينَ عَنِ النَّاسِ — those who restrain their anger and pardon people. The famous story of Ja'far al-Tayyar illustrates this: when a servant accidentally spilled water on him and he became angry, the servant immediately recited that verse, and Ja'far said, "I have suppressed it." The servant continued with the next part — *wa'l-'afina 'an al-nas* — and Ja'far said, "I forgive you." Then the servant recited, وَاللَّهُ يُحِبُّ الْمُحْسِنِينَ, and Ja'far said, "You are free, for the sake of Allah." That is what a believer should be: someone who, on hearing the Quran, responds to it immediately.

Similarly, if someone is engaged in backbiting and another reminds him with آيَةٌ, he should stop at once and not resent the reminder. I was once told a story about Sheikh Ibn al-Habib, a Moroccan sheikh, and one of his most remarkable qualities: he never spoke ill of anyone. This was reported by people who had lived with him for over seventy years, seeing him every single day — and not once in all that time did they hear him say anything bad about a person. Ever.

<!-- page 139 -->

Not long ago, someone wrote me a letter — a man I believe to have genuine taqwa — saying he had mentioned me in a letter to someone else and feared there may have been some ghibah in it. So he sent me the original letter and asked me to read it and tell him if I found any ghibah, so that he could seek forgiveness. He was questioning something he'd written. As it happened, it was simply a disagreement between his understanding and mine, which caused me no difficulty at all. But that level of scrupulousness — even when it is not strictly required — reflects something called istihlal. Strictly speaking in the Shari'ah, you don't have to go back to the person you spoke about and confess it; in fact it is often better not to, because many people will simply become angry at you. Others, though, won't mind — it depends entirely on the person.

The Prophet ﷺ gave us another way. He said, *a-ya'jizu ahadukum an yakuna mithla Abi Damdam* — is any of you unable to be like Abu Damdam? Every morning Abu Damdam would give away his honor as sadaqah for people: "Ya Allah, if anyone has spoken ill of me, I give back to them whatever good deeds of theirs may have come to me as a result." So the Prophet said: just do that. Who cares, in the end? If you truly contemplate the grave, the questioning in the grave, the Day of Judgment — if you really sit with those realities — none of this will matter. Once you die, you could not care less that someone once remarked you get angry too easily, or said something that stung you one afternoon. It will all be completely irrelevant.

<!-- page 140 -->

So the author presents two remedies. Then he says: *fadhkur litazadana biha 'ala rahiri kathrata madhi al-hilmi wa'l-tawadu'i wa'l-sabri* — remember, in order to adorn yourself with the quality that removes anger, reflect on how greatly hilm and tawadu' are praised. Because a truly humble person does not get angry for himself. He may become angry for Allah's sake, but not for his own sake — because the only reason people get angry is that they are seeing themselves. If the self is not there to be seen, what is there to get angry over?

Consider the Prophet ﷺ: when the Quraysh called him *Mudhamman* — the opposite of his name, the condemned one — he simply said, "It is remarkable how Allah turned the tongues of Quraysh, who intended to condemn me, against some man named Mudhamman. But I am Muhammad." He did not get angry. He just observed: they are not talking about me; they are talking about Mudhamman. That is the art of letting go. When there is no self to defend, there is no anger. That is tawadu'.

What the author is saying is this: to acquire this quality, meditate on how universally humility and forbearance are praised. Every culture has honored these virtues in its traditions. This one largely no longer does — though it once did. There are still people who value them, but a certain cultural shift introduced what might be called the ethos of chutzpah — an overweening pride, a "if you've got it, flaunt it" sensibility — which is in fact alien to traditional American culture. Historically, Americans did appreciate humility. And humility is praised by all the sages, across every religious tradition.

<!-- page 141 -->

The Prophet ﷺ said: whoever humbles himself for Allah's sake, Allah raises him up; and whoever raises himself up, Allah brings him low. And the Prophet's camel, al-Qaswa', used to outrun every camel among the Arabs, and the Companions took great pride in this. But one day she lost a race to a Bedouin, and they were all dismayed. The Prophet ﷺ laughed and said, "Don't you know that nothing is ever elevated but that Allah must bring it back down?" That is the sunnah of Allah in His creation. If you understand the sunnah, you can laugh at things like that rather than be troubled by them.

So the author says: if you truly reflect on how praiseworthy hilm, tawadu', and forbearance are — and how the Prophet ﷺ was the most forbearing of all people, forgiving those who wronged him, never given to anger, patient through everything — and if you consider that this praise is found in the Shari'ah, in shi'ar, in the sayings of all the sages, and that every prophet without exception was characterized by hilm and tawadu' — none of them were known for arrogance or quick temper — then you are on your way.

And then he said the second remedy — suppressing anger when it comes — lies in recognizing that nothing happens except by Allah's will. When something arises that provokes you, take a breath and say *la ilaha illa Allah*. This is a bala from Allah, a trial, to see *ayyukum ahsanu 'amalan* — which of you is finest in deed. The Prophet ﷺ said: *laysa al-shadidu al-ladhi yasra'u, wa innama al-shadidu man yaghlibu nafsahu 'inda al-ghadab* — the strong man is not the one who can wrestle others to the ground; the truly strong man is the one who overcomes himself when anger comes. That is the shadid. That is real strength.

<!-- page 142 -->

Imam al-Ghazali also observes that some people, in their foolishness, equate explosive anger with virility and courage. They see macho posturing as a virtue of character. You will even find a man who brags about his criminal exploits — how he robbed someone, fought them, dominated them — as though this were bravery. It is not. There is nothing virtuous in it whatsoever.

As for the practical means of suppressing anger, the hadith gives a sequence: if you are standing, sit down; if you are sitting, recline; if neither helps, go and make wudu'; and if that is still not enough, begin to pray. Performing wudu' actually dissipates the heat of anger — for anger is fire, and anger comes from Shaytan. It is the quality of Shaytan. One day the Prophet ﷺ observed a man whose face had gone red and whose veins were bulging with rage, and he said, "SubhanAllah — do you not see how, when one of you becomes angry, his face begins to resemble Shaytan?" And he said that had the man uttered the remembrance of Allah, it would have been lifted from him entirely.
