And I was once in a conflict-resolution situation, and it was really interesting — things started getting heated and both parties stood up at the same time. They had been sitting on couches, and I had to make them sit back down. But that is the nature of anger: you start rising, and the way to overcome it is to sit down.

If you are in a room and you begin to feel anger, leave the room. If someone is making you angry, just leave. Go to another place, relax, sit down, and let your anger subside — then you can return. This is a practice that, if you actually do it, works. If you do not, you will either end up in a physical confrontation — and if the other person is bigger than you, he beats you, and if he is smaller, everyone sees you as a bully. So the best thing is simply to avoid it. There is no benefit in it, none whatsoever. It is empty and meaningless, and it is simply best to walk away.

That is the sunnah: cold water, silence, sitting down, and wudu. All of these came in the sunnah. Imam al-Razi mentioned that one man with a fierce temper actually hired someone to insult him and paid him to do so, just so he could practice listening without reacting until he could master his self-control. That may sound extreme, but the point is that people took these matters very seriously, because anger is a terrible quality and no one wants to be possessed by it.

<!-- page 106 -->

There is a hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ said that anger will even afflict the best of this ummah. No one illustrates this better than Sayyidina 'Umar. He was a man of fierce anger — yet if you trace his growth as a human being and as a Muslim, by the end of his life 'Umar was not someone whose anger ever got the better of him. In fact, it was quite the opposite: he became known for his forgiveness. You can see this clearly in his later years. Even when he was mortally stabbed, the first thing he asked was whether his assailant was a Muslim. When he was told no, he praised Allah that no Muslim had done this to him — he simply did not want that on a Muslim's account. He said *al-hamdu lillāh*, and that was his nature. For all his ferocity — he was truly a lion of Allah — his anger never consumed him.

On one occasion the Prophet ﷺ was sitting with some women of the Quraysh in Madinah — this was before the verse of hijab was revealed — and when 'Umar entered, they all immediately fled into another room and hid behind a curtain. The Prophet ﷺ was laughing when 'Umar came in. 'Umar asked, *Mā aḍḥakaka yā Rasūl Allāh* — "What makes you laugh, O Messenger of Allah?" The Prophet ﷺ said he was laughing at these Qurayshi women, who had fled the moment they saw him. 'Umar went over to the curtain and called them back. He asked them: "How is it that you flee from me, yet you are not shy before the Messenger of Allah, who is far more worthy of your awe?" They replied, "Because you are harsher and sterner than he is — he does not get angry." At this the Prophet ﷺ laughed and said, *Yā 'Umar, law sarakta fī jāddin la-sarakha l-shayṭānu fī jāddin ākhar* — "O 'Umar, if you went down one path, Shaytan would take another path entirely."

That example shows what it means to master the quality of anger. He truly mastered it. Yet at the same time, his anger was rooted in *ghayrah* and in *'adl* — in righteous indignation and the demand for justice. He became angry at wrongdoing because he wanted to right it. Even his coming to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and accepting Islam was driven by that impulse — he felt something was wrong and he wanted to correct it. Everything in him was rooted in his convictions. He had an extraordinarily powerful sense of justice, and the Khatab family were traditionally known for that quality, even in pre-Islamic Arab culture.

<!-- page 107 -->

Now let me address the question that came in. As for children under seven, one must be very careful about expressing anger toward them. For children over seven, however, discipline is necessary — they have to be disciplined. And here it is worth noting something relevant to the three souls and the question about a teacher needing to establish discipline. The classical scholars described three stages corresponding to the first three sets of seven years. The first seven years are governed by the appetitive soul — which is why young children are constantly eating, constantly wanting things; it is all desire and the fulfillment of appetite. The second seven years are governed by *ghadab*, and the third by the rational faculty, by *'ilm*. This is precisely why the hadith says *lā'ibūhum sab'an* — play with them for seven years — because play is the fulfillment of the appetitive soul. The Prophet ﷺ noted that there is no *la'ib* — no play — in Islam except in certain contexts, one of which is with one's spouse. That is a wholesome and praiseworthy kind of play: *mudā'abah*, the playful affection between husband and wife, and the Prophet ﷺ himself engaged in this with his wives. So the hadith instructs us: play with your children for seven years — during this period of the appetitive soul, allow them to explore and indulge their nature.


It is part of what children are meant to do. They are discovering the world, and that discovery is entirely sensory — they have come from a spiritual realm into the realm of sensation, and so they must inhabit their bodies and experience them. This is why things that are quite traumatic often happen to children in those first seven years: they get burnt, they fall from high places. All of this is the process of learning to live inside a body and discovering that the body has limits. It is a hikmah of Allah that these things happen to children, because they literally teach the lesson that the body is bounded — unlike the ruh, which is a more limitless kind of entity. The body is limited, and the Sharia is a protection for the body: touch fire and you learn to stay away from it; stay away from heights; do not endanger yourself. So you allow children to discover the world. Those first seven years really are a time of discovery, and that is why the word hudam, whose root means to lust — raluma, to lust — captures it well. It is a lusty experience: children are very sensuous, they love to be touched, and that is part of how they develop and discover themselves.

Then comes the second stage. He said: *ad-dibuhum li sab'in* — discipline them for seven years, because now their habab needs to be disciplined. They are moving into a new stage and require firm guidance. Discipline here means that when you see them becoming angry, you do not allow it. You do not let children rage at their parents — that is a bad sign. You have to teach them to control their anger. And if you yourself cannot control your anger, it will be a disaster: you cannot give what you do not have. If children see you losing your temper constantly, they will simply imitate what they see, and then you will wonder where they got that temper. It came from you, because you are their model. So the adab of those second seven years is precisely this.

Then he said: *asa'hibuhum li sab'in* — befriend them for the next seven years, because this last stage is the stage of 'ilm. They are now moving into a phase where they need your guidance, your counsel — your shawara — because you will help direct their education, their vocation, and the shape of their lives. That is the traditional model.

<!-- page 139 -->

Any questions? — It is worth noting that the word for 'ilm: what is its root in Arabic? You know the answer, but what about the word akhlam? When they said *sappahu akhlamaana* — the intellect — forbearance is actually a sign of intellect. Why? Because you have your quwwat al-ghadabiyya under control; it is directed by the rational soul. Your 'aql is working, because the 'aql is an 'ikal — *yaqiluhu* — it is what puts the irascible and the concupiscent soul in check. 'Aql means to prevent, to restrain, and that is precisely what the rational mind is supposed to do: keep the ghadab and the shahwa in check. If it fails to do that, you are not 'aqil, you are not halim — you are, as they say, a himar. If someone becomes truly enraged, people say he is like a donkey: he is acting like a beast, not like a human being. And usually he is a himar because his anger has taken over.

But the crucial distinction is tahallum — and this is a very good point that was just raised, and I am genuinely glad it came up because I had forgotten to mention it. There is a hadith that is important here, because some of us will say: "My God, I cannot do any of this — it is all too hard. I am a mess. I get angry. I overindulge." The hadith says: *innama al-'ilmu bi-ta'allum* — knowledge is acquired; you are not born an 'alim, you become one. How? Through effort, through mujahada. The 'alim is the one who struggles to learn: he rises at night, he studies, he reads, he attends lessons, he listens, he takes notes, he reviews. That is mujahada. You were not born with that knowledge; it is something you acquire. Hilm is exactly the same. The hadith says: *wa innama al-hilmu bi-tahallum* — forbearance too is acquired. And what happens when someone becomes a true 'alim? When you encounter such a person, you do not see the student he once was. It seems as though he always had that knowledge — you did not witness all those years of study and struggle. The same is true of the halim: you do not see when he was wrestling with himself; you assume he was simply born that way. But it is something acquired, something that took real effort. Ta'allum comes from tafar'ud, which is takalud — you have to work for it, and it is hard.

Now, here is a very important point. Imam al-Ghazali uses the metaphor of a calligrapher. He asks: what do you do when your handwriting is poor? And this is the metaphor for dealing with anger. You go to someone with an excellent hand, you watch him, you ask him to form the letters for you, you go home and you practice those letters again and again. At first they will be poor — but after a month or two, you begin to notice improvement. It is not mastery, it is far from it, but it is noticeably better. And someone who has never started will look at your work and say, "You have beautiful handwriting," while the master will look at the same page and say, "This is terrible" — because *hasanat al-abrar sayyi'at al-muqarrabin*: you are advancing through ranks, and the standard rises with every rank. When you reach mastery, what happens? I once watched Muhammad Zakariyya — I took a calligraphy lesson from him, a single lesson — and I watched him work, and I thought: "I can do that. That looks easy." Why did I think that? Because he is a master. He is an American master of calligraphy who wins awards in Turkey. But I did not see the years of nights he had put into it. Now he just flows across the paper as if he were born writing perfect calligraphy. Then I picked up the pen and started, and it was like watching something jump up and down — it was very hard. He simply flowed. That is mastery.

So what do you do when you want good character? Three things. First, you learn what it is. You have to learn what bad character is and what good character is — and that is precisely what we are doing here in these sessions. The first stage is learning. Second: *wa alladhina jahadu fina la-nahdiyyannahum subulana* — those who struggle for Our sake, We will guide them to Our paths. You have to find people of good character and spend time with them. There is also a benefit in being around people who get angry easily, because it allows you to see clearly how unpleasant it is. This is why Imam al-Shafi'i, when someone asked him how he had developed such excellent character, said: "I listened to what my detractors said about me, and then I looked into my own soul to see whether it was true. When it was, I struggled with myself until I removed it." Sayyidna 'Isa was once asked the same question — how did you develop such excellent character? — and what the tradition records is this: "I looked at the bad character of people and I avoided it." So part of it is simply mujahada. The third stage is to be with someone who has good character — and although that is genuinely difficult to find, we should never despair. At least we should be trying.

I will tell you this: it really works. I am serious. I am nowhere near where I would like to be, but it helped me enormously to learn these things and to struggle with myself as much as I could. You do change. You do improve. Anger — khattab — was a serious difficulty for me for a very long time, and it is much less so now. Part of that, I will admit, is simply aging. Imam Sayyidna 'Ali said that youth is a kind of madness, and the cure is to grow old. So there is that.

On anger toward parents — the principle is that you should be angry at the act, not at the actor. What disturbs you should be the action itself, but if it is your father or your mother, you must be especially careful. If you are in a position to act, then *al-amr bi-al-ma'ruf wa al-nahy 'an al-munkar* applies, provided it will not lead to a worse fitna. But with parents you must be very careful. A man came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: "My father takes my wealth and it really angers me." The Prophet ﷺ said: "You and your wealth belong to your father" — meaning that he was the instrument by which you were given existence, and that carries an immense weight. Parents hold a very high maqam in Islam. The best approach is to recognize that they are human beings with their limitations. I had a teacher once — not a Muslim, but a very wise person — who told me that he thanked his father because his father had taught him how not to be a father. His father had been so harsh with him that he did the precise opposite with his own children. In the end he genuinely felt that it was a gift. He was not being sarcastic — he meant it. Obviously it would be better if your father showed you how to be a father rather than how not to be one, but the idea is taking the worst situation and making the best of it. *Wa man yu'ta al-hikmata faqad utiya khayran kathiran* — that is hikma. And that is the beauty of Islam: in any situation, it can be turned to benefit.

So, insha'Allah, we are actually coming — believe it or not — to the end. There are only four sections remaining after today, and I think we will finish all the diseases of the heart next week and then move on. He has a few excellent sections ahead: one is called the cure for all the diseases of the heart together — a single, overarching remedy. The Gordian knot, you might say. Everyone knows about the Gordian knot: a massive knot in Turkey that no one could untie, and the legend was that whoever untied it would rule the land. When Alexander the Great arrived, they showed him the knot — and he drew his sword and cut straight through it. The diseases of the heart are like that, and sometimes the sword is the best answer. Then we will do the section on hawajis — thoughts — because one of the most obvious manifestations of the diseases of the heart is diseased thinking: impure thoughts that afflict the mind, but also thoughts that a person might believe are genuinely good yet are in fact from Shaytan. Shaytan is described in the hadith as being like a wolf among a flock: if he finds the shepherd watching from one side, he comes from another. One of the key differences between Shaytan's waswas and the hawa of the nafs is that Shaytan's whisperings are compulsive, while the nafs has its own habitual pull. We know from Ramadan that the shayatin are locked up — *sufidat al-shayatin* — and yet people still struggle. That is where you begin to distinguish between Shaytan, the nafs, hawa, and dunya, because those are the four enemies of the human being: Shaytan, nafs, hawa, and dunya.

<!-- page 140 -->

The next section is on ghafla — *wa ghaflatun 'an ruh al-dunya nahya wa naha wa hukm wa hay'atiha* — and this is a truly important concept. Imam al-Junayd said that ghafla is the root disease. And even though we have heard different candidates for the root disease proposed — 'ibad, and then al-kibr, and then taswif, which is procrastination — procrastination is itself a form of ghafla, so I think those scholars would likely agree. The reason you procrastinate is that you are not truly attending to the matter at hand; if you genuinely saw the urgency of it, you would act. So ghafla is a serious illness of the heart.

He calls it ghafla: inattentiveness, heedlessness, unawareness. The Arabic word for a simpleton — someone you can easily fool — is mughafal, a passive form: one who has been caught unaware. And so ghafla, in his definition, is sahw. As you know, sahw is a vital concept in the prayer itself — the sujud al-sahw. The very fact that sujud al-sahw exists, and is an essential element of knowing how to pray, tells us something: the nature of the human being is forgetfulness. Ghafla is a kind of forgetting.

The word in Arabic for the human being — insan — is revealing. Some say it derives from uns, meaning intimacy, because the human being is a creature that needs companionship, that needs uns; he is not washi, not a creature that can exist in isolation. Others say it comes from nisyan, which means to forget. If you examine the word insan — and insan applies to both male and female, though some linguists note that insanatun is a valid form, insanatun fi tanatun, appearing in a line of jihadi poetry, though rare — the human being is insan, male and female alike:

*al-insanu al-insan al-dhakaru wa al-untha*
*al-insanu min al-dahri lam yakun shay'an madhkura*

So insan is the human being. And in the Quran, when insan is used, it sometimes refers to Adam, 'alayhi al-salam, and the first slip of our father Adam was nisyan — he was caught unaware by Shaytan. That is why the human being must be vigilant; he must have 'asim, a guard. And so the disease of ghafla is sahw — a forgetting — and *yahtariyat al-insan min qillat al-tahafuz wa al-tayaqquz*: it befalls the human being as a result of an absence of tahafuz — vigilance — and tayaqquz — wakefulness.

When Sayyidna 'Ali, radiy Allahu 'anhu, said: *al-nasu niyam, fa'idha matu intabahu* — "people are asleep, and when they die they wake up" — what he meant is that people are ghafilun, sunk in heedlessness about their destiny: where they are going, and what they will be called to account for when they get there. Because if they truly knew, with real wakefulness — yaqaza — they could not do the things they do. That is why Imam al-Junayd said this is the core disease: if the human being truly knew what was coming, there is no way he could act as he does.

This idea is not entirely unlike the Socratic understanding, which belongs to a parallel tradition in the West — the idea that people do wrong because they are genuinely unaware; if they really knew what they were doing, they would not do it. The metaphor given in that tradition is the metaphor of the cave: human beings are like people sitting inside a cave with a lamp behind them, unable to turn around, watching shadows on the wall and taking those shadows for reality. Then one of them escapes, goes out into the real world, sees the sun and the stars and the trees, returns to the cave, and tells the others: "This is all an illusion — what we thought was real is not real; the real thing is somewhere else." And they kill him. That is the role of the prophets: they come and tell people, "You are all asleep — wake up, do you not realize?" So the concept of ghafla is profound, and that is why Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala uses the word in many Quranic verses.

Another word the Quran uses is samid — and samid is similar to ghafla, but there is a greater degree of awareness in it; the samid knows, in some sense, but the ghafil does not. Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala says in Surat al-Qamar: *kunta fi ghaflatin min hadha, fa kashafna 'anka ghita'aka fa basaruka al-yawma hadid* — "You were in heedlessness about this; We have removed the veil from you, and your sight today is sharp." There is a veil of ghafla over the human being, screening him from reality. And Allah says in the Quran that there is a risha — *wa 'ala qulubihim akinnatun* — and this rishawah prevents them from seeing things as they truly are. So the ghafil is not seeing things as they truly are, while the yaqiz — the one who is truly awake — sees things as they truly are.

The Prophet ﷺ prayed: *Allahumma arini al-ashya' kama hiya* — "O Allah, show me things as they really are." And in another hadith: *Allahumma arini al-haqqa haqqan wa-rzuqni ttiba'ah, wa arini al-batila batilan wa-rzuqni ijtinabah* — "O Allah, show me truth as truth and grant me the ability to follow it; and show me falsehood as falsehood and grant me the ability to avoid it." The one who is not ghafil has precisely that state: they see truth as truth and falsehood as falsehood, they follow the truth and avoid the falsehood. And that is the muttaqi — that is him, the person of taqwa: he does what Allah commanded and avoids what Allah prohibited.

So he is saying that ghafla is *ghuflu al-mar' 'amma amarahu Allahu bihi wa nahahu 'anhu* — heedlessness about what Allah has commanded and what He has forbidden. That is why people do wrong: not out of pure malice in most cases, but out of heedlessness about what they have been commanded and what they have been prohibited from doing. And this is why, if you see a believer doing something wrong and you remind him, and he responds with *astaghfirullah* — that is a good person, a muttaqi. You have brought him out of his ghafla. The mutakabbir, by contrast — because kibr is what follows after all that we have been discussing — when reminded of his ghafla, will not accept the reminder. *Akhadhath-hu al-'izzatu bi al-ithm*: he takes refuge in his own pride and refuses. You say to him gently, and he gets angry at you: "Who do you think you are?" That is why the cure for ghafla in a believer is good company — suhba. Be with people of sidq, of sincerity; be with people of taqwa; be with truthful, sincere people who will tell you plainly when you are doing something wrong, and encourage you when you are doing something right. Those are the people who will pull you out of your ghafla.

He then says: *wa hiya ladayhim aslu kulli dhanb* — those of this science consider ghafla the source of all wrong actions, as Imam al-Junayd al-Baghdadi held. *Wa da'uha bi arba'i dawa'* — and the cure consists of four things that carry rectifying qualities: *fa-staghfirillah wa zur wa salli 'ala al-nabi wa-ktub wa thkur*. So he gives the four things.

The first is istighfar, because once you realize you have been ghafil, you begin to see what you have done wrong as a consequence of that heedlessness, and you want to ask forgiveness. Many of the later scholars said that istighfar and salah 'ala al-nabi are the two dhikrs of the final era — the two pillars of devotion for this last period of time. Istighfar is asking forgiveness of Allah, and it is a form of tawba. The Prophet ﷺ said that you should ask istighfar at least seventy times, up to a hundred times a day — *astaghfirullah* — so it is always a good practice. A complementary practice that most of the early Muslims observed was muhasaba. The idea comes from what merchants do at the end of the trading day: they count the money, see what was gained and what was lost, and balance the scales. Muhasaba for the soul is the same: at the end of the day, you look at your day and ask — was there loss with Allah, or gain? If there was loss, you ask istighfar. If there was gain, you say *alhamdulillah* and are grateful. *La'in shakartum la'azidannakum* — if you are grateful, Allah increases you. So that is one way. He goes into shukr as well, since that is one of the maqamat we will reach insha'Allah.

The second remedy is ziyara — visiting righteous people. The salihin occupy the lowest of four ranks: the Anbiya' and Rusul are first, then the Siddiqun, then the Shuhada', then the Salihin. And yet they are all, in a sense, salihun — a shahid must be a salih, a siddiq must be a salih, and the Anbiya' are all salihin by their very nature. Salaha in Arabic means to be sound, and this is related to salama — soundness, wholeness, the person who possesses a qalb salim. The definition of a salih is: *yu'addi haqq Allah wa yu'addi haqq al-'ibad* — he gives what is due to Allah and he gives what is due to the servants of Allah. Haqq Allah is taqwa: doing what He commanded and avoiding what He prohibited. Haqq al-'ibad are all the responsibilities you carry in the world toward other people. So the salih cannot cheat — that is fisq, the very opposite of salah. He does not lie, because the haqq of people upon you is that you be truthful to them. The salih is a person worth visiting.

Most scholars not only encourage visiting the salihin who are alive, but also those who have died. If you are in Medina, you go to Baqi' and visit the salihin there — you give them salam, you say *rahamahu Allahu 'alayhim*. The Prophet ﷺ used to visit Sayyidna Hamza, and he visited the graveyard and said: *kuntu nahaytu kum 'an ziyarat al-qubur fa-zuruha* — "I used to forbid you from visiting graves," because of what the pre-Islamic Arabs used to do at them, "but now that they have been purified of that jahiliyya, visit them." There is a benefit in visiting graves — it is a reminder of the akhira — and there is a greater benefit in visiting the graves of the people of salah.

<!-- page 141 -->

On the question of women and graves: there is a hadith — *la'ana Allah zawwarat al-qubur*, and in one narration *za'irat al-qubur* — and zawwara is an intensive form, referring to someone who visits graves excessively and repeatedly. There is a hadith that is considered mutawatir in which the Prophet ﷺ passed by the graveyard in Medina and found a woman there weeping. He said it would be better to be patient. She replied: *ma asabaka ma asabani* — "You have not been struck by the calamity I have been struck by" — and she did not recognize him. He left her, and then someone told her that had been the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. She immediately went and knocked at his door and said: "I did not know it was you." And the Prophet ﷺ said: *al-sabru 'inda al-sadmat al-ula* — "Patience is with the first blow." Patience is when the affliction first strikes — that is when Allah expects it from you. After a year, if the grief has passed and you have moved forward, that is not sabr; that is simply time doing its work. The sabr is at the moment the musiba occurs, and that is where Allah wants to see thabat — steadfastness — and not a shaking that would cause one to do something displeasing to Him. But the point of this hadith for our discussion is that the Prophet ﷺ did not tell her to leave the graveyard — and scholars take from that the permissibility of women visiting graves, though there is indeed a khilaf on the matter.

As for the adab of visiting graves: what happens at some graves nowadays has nothing to do with what is being discussed here. At some shrines — and this applies in many parts of the Muslim world — people tie knots, women who cannot conceive go to ask the wali to give them a son, people make vows to the buried saint. All of that has nothing to do with a proper ziyara. He is talking about visiting with adab, for the blessing of the visit itself — a ziyara. That is something quite different. And this confusion is part of what has given tasawwuf a bad name: much of what is done under its label is bid'a — in fact, it really is bid'a. Women sitting around in a mawlid without hijab, chatting and gossiping all day — those are all problems. In such cases, the scholars' strategy is sometimes simply to prohibit the practice altogether, because the corruption has become so pervasive. But the best, without question, is to visit the living salihin.

Unfortunately, in our time, this is quite rare. To find a truly righteous person, you often have to travel far and go to difficult places, because many of them have simply retreated to places that are hard to reach. Someone like Murabbit al-Hajj — rahimahu Allah — whom we visited in Shinqit, the father of Shaykh Abdallah — I consider him, in my estimation, from the salihin. When Allah says *ya ayyuha alladhina amanu ittaqu Allaha wa kunu ma'a al-sadiqin* — "be with the truthful ones" — He would not command us to do something if there were no such people and no way to recognize them. It would be foolish to think we cannot recognize people of sidq and salah. There are obviously degrees and levels, but the basic criterion is the Sharia: if someone is outwardly upright in their observance of the Sharia, that is our standard. We judge by the outward, and we judge by truth.

One of the dangers I have noticed in modern tasawwuf — in many Muslim countries — is that there are people who go by the name of pir or shaykh or whatever, and many of them inherited the title from their father, who inherited it from his father, and many of them have very little knowledge, sometimes almost none. And they sometimes use their positions for material gain — extracting money from followers, arranging advantageous marriages for their daughters, and so on. There are a great many charlatans. And nothing is worse than a religious charlatan — nothing. There is a man in Fez, Sidi Ma'ib, whom some here know, and he once said to me: "Allah has made the world vast, and He has made the means of earning halal provision without number — there are untold ways of earning halal. So whenever you see someone using the religion to gain dunya, know that it is a bad thing." If they are using it to accumulate wealth and exploit their position, that is a serious problem — and unfortunately there are many who claim to be shaykhs or Sufi shaykhs who do exactly that.

That said, having a livelihood is a different matter entirely. *Khayru ma akala al-rajul min kasbi yadihi* — the best thing a man can earn from is the work of his own hands. The ulama have taken from the hadith on teaching the Quran that it is permissible to earn from teaching. And even though the early salaf were generally opposed to taking payment for teaching, the later scholars recognized that people must have livelihood and that the world has become more complicated. That is not the issue. The issue is amassing and accumulating wealth — using religion as a vehicle for that. Imam Malik, radiy Allahu 'anhu, was given enormous amounts of wealth because of who he was, but what he did was spend it all out. Murabbit al-Hajj is a fine example of this: money comes from all over, and it all goes straight back out. You can see it — a few cows, a tent, an adobe house, a few robes, some books, and constant giving. People give to righteous people because they trust them, and practicing Muslims are generous, so they want to give. The question is not receiving — the question is hoarding, accumulating beyond one's needs, refusing to let it flow out. When your needs are taken care of and you begin to hoard, that is where the problem lies.

You have to be very careful, and it has been a problem for a long time. The scholars complained about it centuries ago. One scholar — and this man himself was a genuine shaykh, a morabbi, a person of real tarbiyya and outward knowledge both — said that in his time, the ninth century, most people who called themselves Sufi shaykhs were complete charlatans. So it is an old problem. And that is a condition, by the way: anyone who does tarbiyya must have outward knowledge. They cannot be without it.

Unfortunately, in the Muslim world today there is a profound split between tasawwuf and exoteric Islam — between the inward and outward dimensions of the tradition — and they have become two camps pitched against each other. Nowhere is this more visible than on the Arabian Peninsula, where you have the Najdis and the Hijazis: most of the Najdis are staunchly anti-Sufi, and many of the Hijazis are enthusiastically pro-mawlid and pro-tasawwuf, and they are at loggerheads, with real hostility between them. We are out of balance as an umma, and the truth lies somewhere between the two positions. Make du'a for them, and ask to benefit from their respective knowledges.

Ahmad Baba al-Timbukti — the great scholar from Timbuktu who was taken as a slave to Morocco — when he arrived in Marrakech, he was so much more learned than any of the scholars there that they quickly realized they had made a grave mistake. He mentions in his book — Dibaj al-Muvahab, his hashiya on Ibn Farhan's Tabaqat — visiting several of the graves of the righteous in Marrakech and asking Allah at one of them to grant him understanding of two books he was working on. He says that by Allah, he returned to those books and could read them without any difficulty. He was a great scholar, and that was the traditional practice: to ask for the blessing of their knowledges, since most of those buried in such places were scholars, and to ask *rahimahu Allah* — that Allah give them light in their graves and bless them.

Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi — whose grave is just outside of Fez — gave a fatwa permitting the visiting of the graves of the salihin, as did Ibn al-Hajj. For those who say it is bid'a: that is simply not consistent with the position of the vast majority of our scholars, who have not only permitted it but recommended it. And these are outward scholars, jurists — well known in the tradition. Qadi Abu Bakr ibn al-'Arabi's grave is well known outside of Fez; he was poisoned by his enemies and died a martyr there, having also fought jihad multiple times. He wrote two commentaries on the Quran. He brought the Ihya' 'Ulum al-Din from Imam al-Ghazali back to Morocco and Andalusia — he had studied it with al-Ghazali directly and transmitted it west. He is a great scholar, and anyone who has visited that place knows there is a great deal of baraka there, insha'Allah.

But the living — if you have the opportunity to visit them, do so. And you should have high himma: desire to be one of them, so that our umma has people worth visiting. We need these people. Make the intention to be one yourself, insha'Allah, and ask Allah to give you tawfiq — and give all of us tawfiq for that. One of the poets said: *idha al-mar'u lam yartad al-'ula* — if a man does not reach the heights, at least he aspires to them. *Wa man qani'a bi al-duni kana duniyya* — whoever is content with less than heights is himself less than heights. The greatest aspiration is to be close to Allah. Have high himma. And Sidi Ibn 'Ajiba said: if you do not believe that Allah can take you in this very moment and make you one of His awliya', then you know nothing of His qudra. Never despair. Aspire to be from the people of wilaya, the people who are close to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala.

So ziyara is important. When you visit these people — those I have visited from the people of this path — you will notice that they do not engage in idle chatter. You do not go for a social call. You go for the benefit of learning something, and for the benefit of their du'a. Ask them for their du'a, because it may well be accepted, and if it is, you receive the blessing of it. Visit them, ask for their du'a, and show humility in their presence.

I will share a story about Mulla Ramadan, who was from the salihin and was the father of Shaykh Sa'id Ramadan al-Buti. Shaykh Sa'id wrote a book called *Hadha Walidi* — "This Is My Father" — describing what a great man his father was. I once asked Dr. Bukar — a Libyan scholar, I believe he is in New Jersey now — about Mulla Ramadan, because it is always strengthening to the iman to hear stories of these people. He told me — SubhanAllah — that the most remarkable thing about visiting that man was this: he said he had always found the practice of kissing hands rather unnecessary, something he had never been comfortable with. He said he went in, Mulla Ramadan extended his hand, and he felt a kind of pressure on his head — and found himself going down and kissing his hand. When he looked back, there was nothing there. He was


They would pray on the Prophet ﷺ. Imam Malik, may Allah be pleased with him, said he never spent a single night without seeing the Prophet ﷺ in a dream. There is a great blessing in it, and people bear witness to that.

<!-- page 133 -->

The fourth practice is Tilawat al-Qur'an — recitation of the Qur'an, preferably with tadabur, which means reflection and pondering the meanings rather than merely reading the words. Reciting with tadabur awakens the heart; it is one of the things that genuinely rouses it from slumber. Even reciting without deep reflection carries its own benefit. You should have a daily portion of Qur'an — ideally at least a hizb or a juz each day. If that is not possible, then at minimum recite the surahs the Messenger of Allah ﷺ recommended: Yasin, generally after Fajr; and the surahs after Maghrib and al-Mulk after Isha.

For those who have not yet learned Arabic, reading the meanings in English is something, but it is limited — and this is the honest truth — because part of what the Qur'an does operates through the actual sounds of the language. Think of it like medicine. From an energetic perspective, every substance in the world resonates at a specific wavelength. Diseases resonate, and a medicine that cures a disease resonates in a way that counteracts it. The recitation of the Qur'an works through the sounds themselves, the sounds that emanate from you as you speak. We now know from material science that sound waves resonate through every cell in the body. When you recite the Qur'an, you are producing divine words that Allah revealed — and those words carry a sound.

<!-- page 134 -->

I was once with a man at a gas station. He was asking about Islam. A Muslim mechanic was there, and I was present wearing a robe, so he began asking me about it. He mentioned that he had read the Qur'an. I said, "You have never read the Qur'an unless you read it in Arabic." He said he couldn't believe that. I said it was a fact. He asked why, and I said, "Just listen." Then I recited the opening of Surat al-Rahman to him — and wallahi, by the One besides whom there is no god — when I finished, he simply said, "I understand now." He was not joking. Non-Arab people, Ajami people, are often stunned when they hear the Qur'an, even if they are not Muslim. They are amazed by the sounds.

You have to recognize that the Qur'an is not only its meanings — it is also the sounds, which transcend and participate in the meaning in a realm we do not fully comprehend, because everything in reality is meaning. Allah has taken meaning all the way down to the phonemes of the Qur'an. In modern linguistics, phonemes are considered meaningless in isolation; they acquire meaning only when joined into polysyllabic forms. Someone once wrote a nasty critique of Dr. Hamid Algar, complaining that his speech was filled with polysyllabic sentences. Dr. Algar pointed out in his rebuttal that a sentence that is not polysyllabic is, by definition, meaningless. And yet even individual phonemes carry meaning in Arabic. We may not know what that meaning is, but the meaning goes all the way down. Those are the four practices he identifies.

<!-- page 135 -->

Now, Allah warns the Prophet ﷺ about sitting with those whose hearts He has placed in a state of heedlessness. Allah clarifies elsewhere in the Qur'an that when people deviated, He caused their deviation to increase. Those who turn away from the truth, Allah places in a state of ghafla. Ghafla, then, is itself a punishment. A Muslim who is in ghafla is experiencing a consequence of his own disobedience toward Allah ﷻ. It is a serious condition — you do not want to be among the ghafilin.

And yet, in all honesty, all of us will move in and out of ghafla. That is simply the nature of existence in this world. Ghafla ends the moment you die. What matters is that you spend more of your life in a state of wakefulness than in a state of heedlessness. You want to be someone who is not in ghafla.

This is why Sayyidina Umar said that the best remembrance of Allah is when you reach a limit He has set. In other words, when you are on the verge of committing a sin — none of you drink wine, but imagine you were about to — and in that moment you remember Allah and think, "I am about to put fire into my stomach," and that remembrance becomes a zajir, a mani', a deterrent that prevents you from the haram — that is the best dhikr. When the desire for disobedience rises and the remembrance of Allah holds you back: that is what he means. So there are degrees of ghafla, and what he is saying is that the disease is the ghafla that prevents you from fulfilling what Allah has commanded and avoiding what He has prohibited. Anything beyond that, remembering Allah is mustahab.

<!-- page 136 -->

Allah has prescribed remembrance at certain fixed times of day. He did not make remembrance throughout the entire day an obligation, because not everyone is capable of that. He said: remember Me at appointed times, awqat mu'ayyana. But He also encourages us to make much remembrance of Allah — and that is nafl, recommended, not obligatory. The more you remember Allah, the less likely you are to fall into the ghafla you do not want. And again, the disease of the heart is specifically the ghafla that prevents you from fulfilling your obligations or refraining from what has been prohibited.

As for the opposite — those in whose hearts Allah has written iman — one of the meanings here relates to writing itself: to inscribe without diacritical marks or voweling signs. That is one dimension of the meaning. The iman that Allah writes into the heart is of that nature.

<!-- page 137 -->

The next disease is ghil. Ghil is a disease of the heart related to rancor — resentful malice. The root word is a thulathi, a three-letter root that can go three ways: ghull, ghal, and ghil. The ghull or the ghul is a qayd, a shackle. The people of the Fire have a ghull that binds them. The Prophet ﷺ was sent to relieve humanity of their burdens and the things that were binding them — that is what the ghull is. And ghil is when the heart becomes bound, as though shackled, to treachery, malice, or the desire to betray or harm someone. There is a lock holding it in place, just as a handcuff has the mechanism that holds it closed.

What holds the ghil together is resentful malice — something that results from being angry at a person and keeping that anger inside the heart until it festers and eventually becomes a desire to harm that person. This is why among the things Allah mentions in the Qur'an is the supplication: do not place in our hearts ghil toward the believers. You do not want ghil in your heart toward any believer, because it will kill your heart. Literally kill it.

<!-- page 138 -->

One thing Allah does in Jannah is remove ghil entirely. Momins may die still carrying ghil, but you do not want to bring it with you. Allah says in the Qur'an: وَنَزَعۡنَا مَا فِی صُدُورِهِم مِّنۡ غِلٍّ — "And We will remove whatever ghil is in their hearts," and He makes them brothers, reclining together on couches.

In the dunya, Sayyidina Ali had an experience of this. A man who had fought against him — it may have been at the Battle of Jamal — came to him and apologized. And that is the verse Sayyidina Ali recited: We removed from their hearts. He was saying, alhamdulillah, Allah will take that ghil out of the heart. You can carry rancor toward a person and Allah can remove it. So ask Allah to remove the ghil from you.

He then tells us how to do it: ahsin ilayhi — do good to that person. That is what the man did when he went to Sayyidina Ali. He humbled himself and apologized, and that was his ihsan. If you have ghil toward a Momin, do good to him. The nature of ihsan is captured in a hadith: jubbiyat al-qulub ala hubb man ahsana ilayha — by their nature, hearts are inclined to love those who do good to them. Allah has built this inclination into the heart regardless of a person's will. If you do good to someone, Allah has made his heart naturally drawn toward you.

<!-- page 139 -->

Now some of the Arabs noted — and an Arab poet said — that when you do good to a noble person you win him over, but if you do good to a lowly person it can make him more rebellious, more agitated. So there are people like that. But you have done your part. If you do good to someone in order to cure your own heart, that is what Allah is asking of you. You do your best. If the other person does not respond, you have done what was in your power. You are then left to make dua for them and to remember that Allah can forgive them.

He also says this will cause your enemies — meaning Shaytan and his minions who love to sow ghil among the believers — to despair. Shaytan loves to do this. He loves to set the believers at each other's throats. I once asked a shaykh why it is that so many disbelievers seem to live peacefully together without much conflict, while even sincere Muslims often fight one another. He said: because Shaytan is not occupied with the disbelievers anymore. They are already in kufr — he can leave them alone. But he is obsessed with the people of iman. He works on them constantly, trying to make them stray. That is why, as has been transmitted, Shaytan has despaired of being worshipped on the Arabian Peninsula, but he has not despaired of causing fitnah and dissension among the people. We have to be vigilant against him.

<!-- page 140 -->

And tuqnituhu — the way you cause Shaytan to despair when ghil begins to arise in the heart is to reject it and to do good to that person at precisely the moment you want to harm them.

Then remember what came in the authentic hadith about Mondays and Thursdays. According to that hadith, the Prophet ﷺ said that Allah forgives people on those two days, and when the angels bring the records, they say: there are two people here who are in conflict, both believers. And Allah says: leave them — leave them until they reconcile with one another. What this means is that if you carry ghil toward a fellow believer, Allah is not forgiving you. If you are at war with another Muslim, that forgiveness is withheld. All you have to do is extend the greeting with sincerity. That is all. Do not turn your back. If you say Assalamu alaykum and they do not reply, you have fulfilled your obligation. It is then in their hands. Do what you can — that is all you are responsible for. But this is a frightening hadith. You do not want animosity toward another believer, because it will prevent you from receiving Allah's forgiveness.

<!-- lecture 2 -->

Now, to — audhu billah — this is the significant one: fakhr and kibr. Kibrun. Fakhr.

<!-- page 141 -->

Fakhr is, at its core, boasting. Boasting is a disease of the heart. Fakhr differs from mubaha and similar things in that fakhr, as understood here, is boasting about something you have not yourself earned. It is boasting about lineage — saying, for instance, "I am a Sharif," or trading on what your father or ancestors did.

One of the most striking moments illustrating this is from the Battle of Uhud. The Arabs would shout out, "Ana ibnu fulan" — "I am the son of so-and-so" — as a form of war cry, a display of lineage. The Prophet ﷺ would respond: "Ana ibnu al-awatik" — "I am the son of the women named Atika." The Arabs did not boast through their mothers, so he was doing the opposite of what they intended. Many of his grandmothers bore the name Atika, and that was his response to their boasting of fathers.

<!-- page 142 -->

On the matter of boasting about parents, al-Khuddam mentions — and the saying goes — la'in fakhartu bi aba'in dhu sharafin, laqad sadaqta wa lakin bi'sa ma waladu: if you boast about ancestors who truly had honor, what you say may even be true — if they were from Bani Hashim, for instance, then yes, it is factually true — but what a wretched child they left behind. If you are boasting about your parents and have yourself accomplished nothing, then as the Arabs say: wa laysa al-fata man qara kana abi, innama al-fata man qara ha ana — the man is not the one who says "my father was." The man is the one who says "here I am." You do something yourself.

Jalal al-Din al-Rumi has a poem in which he names all the great figures who came before, and then says: but do not be content with the stories of those who went before you — go out and create your own story. Be one of those people that those who come after you will speak about. This is part of our teaching: the idea of being genuinely significant — not for the sake of dunya, but for the sake of being someone who actually does something.

This is boasting. Saying "my father did this" — it is what small children do. Children say, "My dad is a fireman." "Well, my dad is an astronaut." It is childish. Mentioning a parent's ihsan, something good they did for you — that is a different matter entirely, and there is nothing wrong with it. But boasting is something we all recognize.

<!-- page 143 -->

Now he says — the mountain, fawduha shamikh, this high mountain — deem that mountain insignificant if you desire to bring it down to the ground. Kibrun: arrogance. In Arabic, kibur with a fatha gives you kibar, meaning old age. Kibrun with a sukun over the ba gives you arrogance. The root is kabura or kabira — two related roots. Kabura means to grow, to be great; kabira means to grow old. Kabir means great — Ibrahim, peace be upon him, said: "Bal fa'alahu kabirahum" — "the great one did it." So kabir is greatness. Kibrun is something that lives in the heart. When it manifests in outward behavior, you call it takabbur. Kibrun and takabbur are the same disease — takabbur is simply the action of it. Takabbara alayya: he was arrogant toward me, meaning he did something that made it visible.

<!-- page 144 -->

There are many signs of kibr. And I will tell you honestly — of all these diseases, this is the one I find most frightening. Because you have some degree of it. All of us do. It exists on a continuum: it can be severe, it can be mild, and you will rarely encounter someone who has none of it at all. When you do meet such a person, you feel it — their absence of kibr is palpable. But nearly everyone carries some. And the most frightening thing about it is the hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ said:

لَا يَدۡخُلُ الۡجَنَّةَ مَنۡ كَانَ فِي قَلۡبِهِ مِثۡقَالُ ذَرَّةٍ مِنۡ كِبۡرٍ


No one will enter Paradise — or, as the hadith puts it, *kama qad* — no one will enter Paradise who carries even the weight of an atom of *kibr*, of arrogance, in his heart. Arrogance, then, is literally a barrier to Paradise.

<!-- page N -->

This is grounded in the Quranic verse: سَأَصْرِفُ عَنْ آيَاتِيَا الَّذِينَ يَتَكَبَّرُونَ فِي الْأَرْضِ بِغَيْرِ الْحَقِّ — "I will divert My signs from those who show arrogance on the earth without right." The scholars note that the phrase بِغَيْرِ الْحَقِّ implies that there are situations in which *takaббur* is actually appropriate. This was precisely the case with Abu Dujanah's famous *tabakhtur* — his swaggering gait on the battlefield — which carried a *haqq*, a legitimate justification. In that context, what would ordinarily be reprehensible became praiseworthy.

So Allah says He will divert His signs from those who are arrogant on the earth without justification, without any legitimate right. And He also says: كَذَٰرِكَ يَطْبَعُ اللَّهُ عَلَى كُلِّ قَلْبٍ قَلْبٍ مُتَّكَبِّرًا جَبَّارًا — Allah will seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant. And: إِنَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُسْتَكْبِرِينَ — He does not love those who harbor *istikbar*. *Istikbar* is essentially the same as *kibr*, but with an additional nuance: the person actively considers himself great. يَسْتَكْبِرُ — he thinks he is great. And at its root lies *'ujb*, that self-admiration he discussed earlier — مُعْجَب بِنَفْسِهِ, thoroughly impressed with himself.

There is also the hadith in which Allah says, as reported by the Prophet ﷺ: يَقُولَ اللَّهُ تَعَالَى الْكِبْرِيَاءُ رِدَائِ وَالْعَظَمَةُ إِذَارِ فَمَن نَازَعْنِي فِيهِمَا قَصَمْتُهُ — Greatness is My cloak, and majesty is My lower garment. Whoever tries to compete with Me over either of them, I will break him. These are two attributes Allah has reserved exclusively for Himself, and He does not permit any created being to lay claim to them.

This brings to mind something relevant to the subcontinent: there are certain names commonly given there that would probably be better avoided. Ibn Hajj observed that Shaytan entered this *Ummah* through two different doors. With the eastern Muslims, he entered through names of grandiosity. With the western Muslims, he entered by causing them to mispronounce good names — dropping the *'abd* — so that, for example, in parts of West Africa, عَبْدُ الكَبِير becomes simply الكَبِير, or عَبْدُ القَادر becomes القادر. And in Sudan, the pronunciation shifts similarly — القادر, which sounds as though it derives from غَدَر, meaning the treacherous one, when of course Allah's name is القادر. Ibn Hajj identified this as one of Shaytan's stratagems.

As for the eastern Arabs, Shaytan led them toward names such as فَخْرُ الدِّين، شَمْسُ الدِّين، جَمَالُ الدِّين. This is why Imam Yahya al-Nawawi, whose father named him مُحْي الدِّين, flatly refused that name and insisted on being called Yahya. He would not accept it at all. Names in this category — أَكْبَر، عَظِيم، عُزْمَة and the like — carry that same problem. The issue is that such names constitute a *tazkiyah*, a self-endorsement. The Prophet ﷺ had a wife named بِرَّ and renamed her زَيْنَب, and said: لا تُزَكُوا أَنفُسُكُمْ — do not declare yourselves pure. He also said: أَصْدَقُوا الْأَسْمَا حَارِثُ وَالْهُمَامُ — the truest names are Harith and Humam, because every person يَحْرِثُ — he toils — and every person carries concern. Those, then, are the most honest names. And the best names are those that include عَبْدَ اللَّهِ or عَبْدَ الرَّحْمَنِ, or names like أَحْمَد. If your name happens to be one of the others, that is simply what your parents gave you — so live up to it as best you can. As was pointed out with some humor, the trouble is when someone bears a lofty name yet turns out to be مِلْعَقَةُ الدِّينِ — a spoon of the religion, just eating all the time.

Now there is a hadith — and it needs verification, though the transmission appears to be there — that is simply astonishing: يَحْشَرَ الْمُتَكَبِّرُونَ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ فِي مِثْرِ صُورَ الْذَرَّ تَطَّأُهُمَ النَّاسُ ذَرًّا فِي مِثْرِ صُورَ الرِّجَالِ يَعْلُوهُمْ كُلُّ شَيْءٍ مِنَ الصَّغَارِ ثُمَّ يُسَاقُونَ إِلَى السِّسْنِ فِي جَهَنَمٍ يُقَالُوا لَهُ بَوْلَسٍ. It is reported in al-Tirmidhi: the people of arrogance will be raised on the Day of Judgment transformed into atoms, and people will walk over them. Then they will be driven to a prison in Jahannam called Bolas. SubhanAllah. The word *Bolas* is strikingly close to what Arabic speakers call the police — خُذُوا إِلَى الْبَوْلَسِ, جَبَّارُون خُذُوا إِلَى الْبَوْلِسِ. Whether that detail is precisely established in al-Tirmidhi needs to be confirmed, but if it is, it is extraordinary. The word *dharr* can mean either an atom or an ant — both readings exist.

Moving on to the nature of *istikbar* itself: there are two types. The worst form of *takabbur* is refusing to accept the truth and refusing to submit to the worship of Allah — أَعْظَم التكبر امتنع قبول الحق وإذعان للعبادة. Everything else falls beneath that in degrees of severity.

A companion once came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: يا رسول الله إني امرأ أحب الجمال — O Messenger of Allah, I am a man who loves beautiful things. I wear fine shoes and a nice garment. He then asked: أهذا من الكبر — Is this from *kibr*? The Prophet ﷺ replied: لا إن الله جميل يحب الجمال وإنما الكبر بطر الحق وغمص الناس — No. Allah is beautiful and loves beauty. *Kibr* is, rather, rejecting the truth and looking down on people. This is an important hadith, because the man first explained his motivation: he loved beautiful things. The scholars clarify that the problem arises when a person dresses or adorns himself in order to تَكَبَّر على الناس — to demonstrate superiority over others. This is painfully familiar in contemporary Muslim culture. Go to some Muslim weddings and you will see people consumed with outdoing one another — "Abdullah and Khadija are going to show everyone what a real wedding looks like." That competitive one-upmanship is a form of *kibr*, because the underlying drive is تَتَكَبَّر عليهم — to assert superiority. It springs from *kibr*.

So *istikbar* has two faces. On one side, a person wants to achieve greatness and be great. Imam Raghib al-Isfahani says that if this drive is oriented toward something genuinely great, exercised at the right time, and for the right reasons, then it is praiseworthy. *Istikbar* in this sense is not inherently negative — and this returns to the broader principle of transforming these diseases into virtues. A desire to be great for the sake of Allah is a good thing. A desire to accomplish great things is a good thing. The *Sahaba* had towering *himmah* — tremendous aspiration. The Prophet ﷺ's *himmah* was the highest of all, and he lifted them all toward it. They set their sights on al-Thuraya, the Pleiades. They reached for the heights in everything they did, and Allah made them great people. That is praiseworthy *himmah*.

The blameworthy kind, by contrast, is يظهر من نفسه أنه أفضل من غيره — when a person tries to project the image that he is better than others. That is what is censured. The one exception is demonstrating to non-Muslims that Muslims are better — because Allah actually commands this. Allah commands us to show them. Someone raised this very point the previous night regarding the Christian response to human nature. The Christian answer, in simplified terms, is that human beings are irredeemably corrupt by original sin — there is nothing to be done about it. Allah's answer is: show them this is not true. كَذَٰرِكَ جَعَلْنَاكُمْ أُمَّةً وَسَطًى — We made you a just, balanced community — لِتَكُنُوا شُهَدًا عَلَى النَّاسِ — so that you may be witnesses to mankind, demonstrating that all other ways fall short. وَيَكُنَ الرَّسُولَ عَلَيْكُمْ شَهِدًا — and the Messenger is a witness over you.

The Prophet ﷺ was a witness to us — he proved it was true. His Companions were witnesses to us — they proved it was true. Their followers were witnesses in turn. But each generation becomes more diluted, until eventually there are no more *shuhada*, no more living witnesses. This is one reason why so many people in the end of times fall into disbelief: they no longer see the truth embodied anywhere, so they lose it. One of the signs of the end of times, according to Imam al-Biqa'i, is that Allah will hand the *dunya* over to the disbelievers, and Muslims will abandon Islam in pursuit of what the disbelievers have. He read this in the verses of Surah al-Zukhruf: had Allah not wished to prevent everyone from becoming disbelievers, He would have made their houses of silver and given them staircases to climb. Look around today — that is almost precisely what you see. People in Mali and Niger watch television and see those gleaming houses and want them. It is a real and serious problem.

Can the sealed heart be penetrated? Yes. Allah says that even rock can be split and water can flow from it — seals can be broken. The scholars say they are broken by the power of the *warid*, the spiritual influx. A prophet, through the sheer spiritual force he carries — by the permission of Allah — can penetrate even the sealed heart. Sidi Ahmad Zarruq says that when someone with a sealed heart hears the truth, several factors determine whether it gets through: the stature of the one delivering it, the intrinsic strength of the truth itself, and the force with which it reaches the heart. If these converge with sufficient power, it can shake the heart and crack the seal. But if nothing follows — no *tawba*, no action taken — the crack can close over again. This is what happens when someone attends a *khutbah*, feels something stir in the heart, then steps outside and returns to the same life: it seals back up.

Now, before concluding, here is a brief survey of the different types of *kibr* and their distinguishing qualities.

The first type: *kibr* that is *mustaqarr* — fixed and settled in the heart. The person genuinely believes he is better than others. There is a hadith in which a man was being praised in the Prophet's ﷺ presence as a person of virtue. When the man entered the gathering, the Companions said: يا رسول الله هذا الذي ذكرناه لك — O Messenger of Allah, this is the man we mentioned to you. The Prophet ﷺ said: إني أرى في وجهه صفعة من الشيطان — I see in his face a trace of Shaytan. The man gave *salam* and sat down. Then the Prophet ﷺ turned to him and said: أسألك بالله حدثتك نفسك أليس في القوم أفضل منك — I ask you by Allah: did your *nafs* tell you, when you walked into this gathering, that no one here is better than you? The man said: Yes. So outwardly he appeared to be a righteous man, but the Prophet ﷺ saw the *kibr* embedded in his heart — and wanted the Companions to understand how subtle and hidden *kibr* can be.

Imam al-Ghazali says that among all people, scholars are in the greatest danger of *kibr*. And that includes students of sacred knowledge — people in exactly the situation under discussion. This is one of the gravest spiritual hazards of pursuing religious learning. If a person fights it, however — if he sees the *kibr* within but forces himself to resist it — he is at least preventing the bitter fruits of that tree from ripening. The tree is there, the roots are there, but the fruit does not emerge.

The second type is where *kibr* moves from the interior to the exterior: the person begins to manifest it in behavior, looking down on others, turning his face away, frowning in people's faces. Frowning at someone is a sign of *kibr*. Refusing to give someone the time of day is a sign of *kibr*. The Prophet ﷺ never did this. Adi ibn Hatim said he knew the Prophet ﷺ was not a king precisely because an old woman stopped him on the street and he simply stood there and listened to her — that was how he was. This second type includes the *'abid*, the devout worshipper, who looks down on others because of his worship — he becomes *mutakabbir* through his religiosity.

The third type is *takaббur* on account of *hasab* and *nasab* — one's personal qualities and one's lineage. These two traditionally went together. Western culture has the concept of *noblesse oblige*: those born into nobility are obliged to behave nobly. Most traditional cultures hold something similar. Among the Arabs, if you were a *sharif* of the Ta'i clan, you were expected to be generous — generosity was the hallmark of Ta'i. If you were Qurayshi, you were expected to carry dignity, to be a *za'im*. If you were Yemeni, you upheld the qualities associated with Yemen. Each group had its defining virtues, and *nasab* carried an obligation to embody them.

But *nasab* also becomes a source of arrogance. People with noble lineage begin to see themselves as superior to others. Some Arabs, simply because they are Arab, look down on Indians — this is common. *Al-haqq yuqal* — to tell the truth — there are many Arabs who think this way. One Arab once said plainly: I never thought a non-Arab could truly be a real Muslim. He said that directly. Similarly, in Pakistan, Indians who did not make *hijra* may be seen as lesser. If you are Hyderabadi, certain Hyderabadis consider themselves automatically superior to all other Indians by virtue of being from Hyderabad. Americans carry their own version — "we're number one." In Hausa land, many Hausa people are convinced of their superiority over the Yoruba and Igbo, and probably over the Fulani — though the Fulani, for their part, see themselves as superior to the Hausa. In Mauritania, the Kunta see themselves as better than the Massouma. In Greater Syria, the Damascenes consider themselves superior to the people of Aleppo — they even guard the name *Shami* jealously, reluctant to let anyone else claim it. This is a human disease, widespread and indiscriminate.


This is a disease of the heart — thinking yourself better simply because of some quality you happen to possess, like being from Damascus. Or being a Nejdi. The Nejdis deem themselves superior to the Hijazis; they call them *Akhlaf al-Hujjaj*. You find this kind of arrogance embedded in the language, in the terminology of every culture. It is all Takabbur.

Allah says, إِنَّ أَكْرَمُكُمْ عِنْدَ اللَّهِ أَتْقَاكُمْ — the most honored of you before Allah are those with Taqwa. Yet this disease deludes a person into assuming that someone else couldn't possibly have Taqwa — how could he? He's a Hindi. He's a Nejdi. He's whatever.

This is not a modern phenomenon. It goes back a long way. Even among the Sada — those who trace their lineage to the family of the Prophet ﷺ — you find Takabbur. And of all things to feel superior about in terms of lineage, that would have to rank first. I'd put it at number one in Nasab. And that is precisely the danger, because sometimes these distinctions do carry genuine weight.

There is something real and honorable about descending from the family of the Prophet ﷺ — it is a tremendous distinction. But there are hadith that should put fear in your heart. One hadith says: إِذَا أَبْطَأَكُمُ الْعَمَلُ لَمْ يُسْرِعْ بِكُمُ النَّسَبُ — if your deeds hold you back, your lineage will not carry you forward. In other words, if you have the Nasab but no deeds to go with it, it will get you nowhere. A fine car with no fuel. If you're a Sharif, it's a Mercedes — but deeds are still the petrol. You are not going anywhere without them. There are many hadith warning of this danger.

<!-- page 144 -->

Now, as for the cure — one of them is to reflect on where you actually came from. Allah says that you are from مَاءٍ مَهِينٍ, a vile fluid. Your very origin is a vile fluid. One of the Salaf, speaking of someone who was being arrogant, said: سُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ يَحْمِلُ بَيْنَ جَنْبَيْهِ الْعَذِرَةَ — glory be to God, he carries between his two sides nothing but filth. Where does the تَكَبُّر come from? He is a bag of blood like everyone else. Every culture has its own crude ways of making this point, which I won't go into — but people everywhere understand it. Nobody likes تَكَبُّر.

The problem is that the مُتَكَبِّر never sees his own arrogance. That is why, when someone is arrogant with him, he loathes it — even though he himself is guilty of the same thing. This is the meaning of the saying: التَّكَبُّرُ فِي وَجْهِ الْمُتَكَبِّرِ عِبَادَةٌ — to show arrogance in the face of an arrogant person is itself a form of worship. It is throwing it back at them. You give them a taste of their own medicine — but it should be understood as a دَوَاء, a remedy, just enough for them to feel what it is like.

That is Kibr. It is a serious problem. And if you feel that you are a righteous person — that itself is Kibr, right from the start. That is why, when you look at the early generations, they were people who genuinely saw themselves differently. One of the companions of Abu Hanifa was sitting with him when a man moved his foot aside out of deference. The companion asked why he had done so. The man said, out of respect for someone like you. He replied: وَاللَّهِ لَا أَرَى أَحَدًا هُنَا أَشَرَّ مِنِّي — by God, I see no one in this gathering worse than me.

That is how those people were. It was not performance. They genuinely saw themselves that way.

Look at Sayyidina Ali. There is a sound hadith in which a man asked him: who is better, you or Abu Bakr? He said, Abu Bakr. The man asked, then what about you and Omar? He said, Omar. Then the man asked about himself, and Ali said: مَا أَنَا إِلَّا رَجُلٌ مِنَ الْمُسْلِمِينَ — I am just a man among the Muslims. No claim, no station.

For us, looking at this from the outside, it reads as humility. But for the Imam himself, he was not calculating: *I should say this in order to appear humble.* No — that way of thinking is itself a disease. Ibn Ata'illah says: if you are aware of your humility, you are متكبر.

This is why the character of Uriah Heap in Dickens' *David Copperfield* is so remarkable. He is a complete hypocrite who is forever saying, "In my very 'umble opinion, sir" — always performing lowliness, always deferring — while simultaneously stealing money and scheming behind everyone's back. The point is: if you think of yourself as humble, you are متكبر, because you are observing your own lowliness from a position of height. Humility means to be low. If you can see how low you are, you must be looking down from somewhere above. That is Kibr by definition.

We are diseased. We simply have to accept that and work with it. Our scholars have said: if you cannot yet be like the true people of virtue, at least imitate them — because there is benefit even in the imitation. The Imam himself acknowledges this: pretending at humility is better than being an outright متكبر.

<!-- page 145 -->

One of the things Imam al-Ghazali, rahimahu Llah, says is this: if you want to master calligraphy, you go to the master calligrapher and you repeat what he does, over and over again. It feels completely unnatural at first, but eventually you become a master calligrapher. Interestingly, a neurosurgeon at Stanford said something that maps onto this perfectly: the brain learns through repetition. Whatever you do most consistently becomes embedded in the brain — like driving. Most people drive in a nearly subconscious state; they are not aware of their own driving. That is the ideal of humility — it becomes so woven into your nature that the brain is no longer conscious of it.

When you first start learning to drive, you are actually more careful. Those poor learners with the dual-control car on top, checking the mirror, checking the blinker, anxious and alert — they are often safer than the experienced driver who is singing along to music, talking on the phone, and thinking about other things. The experienced driver is the one who gets into the accident, precisely because it has all become unconscious. And what the neurologist pointed out was striking: stroke patients and those with dementia often retain the things they did most repetitiously. They may forget almost everything else, but they can still drive — because those neural pathways are so deeply fixed that they remain.

This is the idea behind repetition in spiritual practice: إِنَّمَا الْعِلْمُ بِالتَّعَلُّمِ — you acquire it through learning, through تَكْرَار, doing it over and over again. You must force yourself to act with humility until it genuinely becomes your nature. Most of us do not have these qualities naturally — if we did, we would not need to study any of this. That is why we are studying it: we all recognize these diseases in ourselves.

Now the fourth type is التَّفَاخُرُ بِالْجَمَالِ — arrogance on account of physical beauty. One of the cures for this is the old adage: beauty is only skin deep. Beauty is an illusion. First, no one retains their beauty forever — everyone loses it in the end. Second, Allah gave it to you; it is not something you earned. So taking arrogant pride in beauty is a particularly shallow and foolish form of Kibr.

What I find genuinely fascinating is a study that examined what makes people beautiful. Researchers looked at large numbers of faces and found that what people consistently identified as beautiful was, in fact, average — statistically. An average nose, average eyes, average mouth — all placed together on one face. Beautiful people are, in a real sense, mean-looking. They represent the median. I thought that was remarkable: beauty is the average. And there is a kind of truth in it — the median holds something. It is a foolish thing to take pride in.

There is also a deeply sobering hadith involving Aisha, رضي الله عنها, in which a woman entered and the Prophet ﷺ asked who it was. She indicated the short one. The Prophet ﷺ said: اغْتَبْتِهَا — you have backbitten her. The scholars explain that the reason it constituted backbiting was that it arose from Takabbur: she had, in that moment, a sense of her own greater height, an إِعْجَاب with her own stature — مُعْجَبَةٌ بِقَامَتِهَا — and looking at the other woman from that place of self-regard made the remark an act of تكبر. The Prophet ﷺ rebuked her for it.


The fifth type is كِبْرِ بِالْمَالِ — pride in wealth. This afflicts kings with their treasuries, merchants with their goods, and people who own vast tracts of land, fine clothes, horses, or — in our time — expensive cars. They look down on the poor, and even on other wealthy people who are less wealthy than themselves. Think of Leona Helmsley, who famously said that only the little people pay taxes. I once read a remarkable interview — I cannot recall exactly who it was — one of those figures this culture calls "beautiful people." They were being airlifted by private helicopter to a ski slope while a lodge for less-wealthy skiers sat below, and someone looked down and said, "Look at all the little people down there." At that very moment, the helicopter tipped and they crashed. The point is: she got the message. Some people would not have seen it that way. So be very careful about how you regard others, because we are all little people. The homeless man on the street looks up at the CEO and sees a little person; the CEO looks down at the homeless man and sees a little person. It is all perspective. That is why the mutakabirun are transformed into ants on the Day of Judgment — because they looked at everyone else as ants to be crushed. It is a very dangerous thing.

<!-- page 194 -->

The sixth is kibr bil-quwwa — pride in physical strength. An example is Rukkana al-Musa'rib, who challenged the Prophet ﷺ to a wrestling match. The Prophet threw him twice, and still he did not accept Islam. There is a narration suggesting he did eventually embrace Islam — inshallah he did, though I cannot say with certainty. The point is that he had never been thrown by anyone, and that fed his arrogance. Such a person will inevitably grow old. The strength will leave him. He will no longer be able to do what he once could.

The seventh is takabbur bil-atba' — pride derived from one's followers, supporters, students, and servants. A teacher who has many students may look down on a teacher with fewer. Someone with many powerful friends, influential relatives, or a connection to royalty — even if he is not the king himself — may feel superior because of that association. This is a very dangerous form of kibr rooted in social standing and prestige.

And then there is takabbur through knowledge, which the imam identifies as among the most insidious forms of all. The reason is precisely that knowledge is so honored in the tradition. People naturally revere and defer to the learned. So when someone genuinely possesses knowledge — especially a significant amount of it — people show him respect, and that deference becomes dangerous for the heart. He begins to think, "I am somebody. I matter." This is what Shaytan does to him.

Allah سبحانه وتعالى says in the Quran: how has man been destroyed — what makes him such an ingrate? And then Allah says: let man think about what We created him from. He came from a fluid. He did not even exist before that fluid. He was not even a thought on anyone's tongue fifty years ago. He was nothing, nonexistent. Then he came into existence, and when he first arrived in this world, he needed his diaper changed seven or eight times a day — all he did was defecate, urinate, sleep, cry, and nurse. That is the human being. Then he grows, reaches the peak of his strength, and becomes arrogant. And then it reverses — because the trajectory is curvilinear. If you live long enough, you end up where you started. People reach that forty-year peak and think everything is settled, everything is fine. Then Allah says:

مِن نُّطْفَةٍ خَلَقَهُ فَقَدَّرَهُ

He created him from a nutfa — an ejaculated fluid — and then measured out everything for him. Man did not arrange any of that himself. And then Allah سبحانه وتعالى says:

<!-- page 195 -->

أَوَلَمْ يَرَى الْإِنسَانِ أَنَّا خَلَقْنَاهُ مِنْ نُطْفَةٍ فَإِذَا هُوَ خَصِيمٌ مُبِينٌ

Has man not considered that We created him from a nutfa, and now he has become an open adversary against Us? It is astonishing. Allah created the human being, and then that human being rises up in rebellion against his Creator — and he is nothing. Allah can stir up a hurricane — like Hurricane Mitch — and it is just a small wind in one corner of one planet in one solar system within a vast universe. It is nothing. And yet for those struck by it, entire homes, wealth, everything — gone. From a wind that Allah sent. And Allah says in the Quran that when people see such a wind approaching, they think it is a good wind bringing rain — and it turns out to be a torment.

The point of all of this is that we are simply foolish to be in rebellion against Allah. He gave us a tongue, two eyes, the capacity to think — all of these gifts. He guided us and showed us two roads, telling us plainly: at the end of this road is a painful punishment, and at the end of that road is a reward. Choose. And yet people choose the road of ruin, and at the root of that choice is Takabbur.

Look at the Sira — I guarantee you, every single person who refused to submit, the reason was Takabbur. It is unmistakable. The reason people cannot submit is Takabbur.

Consider the prayer. There is a kind of servitude embedded in it that some people find deeply offensive. Many Western people struggle profoundly with the concept of being a slave of God — Abdullah. They recoil from it: "I am free." And yet that same person is a slave to his desires, a slave to his appetite, a slave to his petty ambitions — completely unable to control them. He is enslaved to all of these things. But a slave to Allah? No. He will gladly be a slave to his own passions but not to Allah. It is a thoroughly delusional state.

<!-- page 196 -->

So the imam is saying that the way to uproot this disease is: first, to know your Lord, and second, to know yourself. That is, admittedly, an enormous undertaking. Some scholars say the only way to know your Lord is to know yourself — and Allah points us in that direction: *in yourselves, do you not see?* If you reflect deeply on the self, what you come to realize is that you are completely bound. Even 'Isa ibn Maryam was not too proud to acknowledge that he was 'Abdullah — he accepted it. And the Prophet ﷺ said, *ana sayyidu waladi Adam wa la fakhra* — I am the best of the children of Adam, and I say this without boasting. The ulama explain that he said this not out of pride, but because he was commanded by Allah to inform the Companions of his station — and precisely because the Jahili Arabs used boasting as an act of arrogance, he was careful to add *wa la fakhra*, making clear this was not that. The ulama draw a further ishara from this: not only was there no fakhr in it, but his true source of honor was his 'ubudiyya to Allah — his being the most perfect slave of Allah — not his being the master of creation. That is where his glory lay. This is why there is a hadith — some scholars question its chain, but Mulla 'Ali al-Qari affirmed that its meaning is sound — that his empowerment before Allah was his glory.
