For Imam Malik, it is a bid'ah to recite قُرْهُوَ اللَّهُ أَحَادُ at every Maghrib prayer, whereas Shaykh Abdullah al-Haddad, in one of his treatises, mentioned that it was a good thing to do, and there is a hadith indicating that one of the Sahabah did exactly that. So you have to be a little careful with these matters.

<!-- page 238 -->

There is also the hadith about Sayyidina Abdullah ibn Umar, may Allah be pleased with him. The Prophet ﷺ once walked past him and said, نعم الرجل لو كان يقوم الليل — "What an excellent man — if only he would spend some time at night in prayer." The scholars have clarified that this is not simply a compliment; the statement contains the word لَيْتَ, a particle of wishing, as in لَيْتُهُ كَانَ يَقُومَ اللَّيْل. What the scholars took from this is that a person can still be a good person even without praying at night — like Abdullah ibn Umar — but how much better would he be if he did? The believer who hears that desires perfection. And so, when Abdullah ibn Umar heard those words — and he himself is the narrator of the hadith, which he related to his son — he never abandoned qiyam al-layl again after that.

The scholars also drew from this hadith that it is permissible to praise someone who will not be made conceited by that praise. The Prophet ﷺ knew that Abdullah ibn Umar would not hear those words and think, "I'm already excellent, so I don't need to pray at night." Rather, it spurred him on to do more — which is exactly what happened.

<!-- page 239 -->

Then there is the well-known hadith of Aisha, which is a sahih hadith, in which she describes how the Prophet ﷺ would pray so long at night that his feet would crack and become slightly swollen. What the physicians call venous stasis — when you stand for extended periods, blood can pool in the feet and cause mild edema. This is why people who are on their feet all day, such as nurses, often wear compression stockings to help the blood return upward. It happens to many people. So Aisha said, "Ya Rasulallah, why do you do this when your Lord has forgiven you everything — past and future?" And he replied, "Should I not be a grateful servant?" In other words, that very fact should make me want to show even more gratitude. Worship, then, is a way of expressing gratitude to Allah. The scholars say this hadith shows that one of the greatest ways to give thanks for the ni'mah of Allah is to do some night work — sahar al-layali. And they say: من قرب المعالي سهر الليالي — whoever seeks high things stays awake at night.

<!-- page 240 -->

For students especially, the night is the best time to study. If Fajr falls at a particular hour, divide the night from Maghrib onward into six equal parts. Each part, in a twelve-hour night, would be roughly two hours. The last sixth of the night — the final two hours before Fajr — is the best time for qiyam al-layl. If we take, for simplicity, Maghrib at six p.m. and Fajr at six a.m., then four a.m. to six a.m. would be that optimal period. That said, any prayer after Isha counts as qiyam al-layl, and some scholars say even after Maghrib.

For people who find it genuinely difficult to rise in the early morning — those with what the tradition calls a "heavy liver," meaning a slow constitution — it is perfectly valid to pray some rakats before going to bed. You should do something in the night hours. Interestingly, many people find it easier to wake up around three or four in the morning than right before Fajr, because the sleep immediately before Fajr is recognizably the heaviest sleep of the night. This is a hikmah from Allah, and it also corresponds to what science tells us: cortisol levels in the brain are at their peak at that time. Ibn Ata'illah said the wisdom in this is that Allah wants you to be able to pull yourself out of sleep precisely when it is most difficult to do so.

<!-- page 241 -->

As for the number of rakats: eight and ten are both established by sahih hadith, and I was told ten is the best. You pray two by two — two, four, six, eight, ten — and it is better done after Isha, unless you are someone who by nature rises every night without fail, in which case you may wait. Sayyidina Abu Bakr, who was very strong in his spiritual practice and certainly prayed the night prayer, used to pray before he went to bed.

What if you are staying up late — do you have to sleep first and then get up? No, you don't have to. Obviously it is better to sleep and then rise, because the Prophet ﷺ said that was the best way — the way of Dawud, peace be upon him, who would sleep for a portion, rise for a portion, sleep again, and rise again. That is harder on the self than simply staying up, and he maintained that practice just as he maintained his fast of every other day. It is true mujahidah — real mastery over the nafs — because it is genuinely difficult to fall asleep and then get up again.

<!-- page 242 -->

And never forget — A'udhu Billah — that Shaytan is always nearby. Shaytan Yaqidu Aqdan Tharatha — he ties three knots on your head while you sleep, in the unseen world, and whispers, Amamika Laylun Tawil — "You have a long night ahead of you, just relax." In winter especially, this is all too convincing. Those three knots are heavy. The Prophet ﷺ said that if you wake up and recite the du'a of waking — الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ الَّذِي أَحْيَانِي بَعْدَ مَا أَمَاتَنِي وَإِلَيْهِ النُّشُورُ — the first knot is loosened. If you get up and make wudu, the second knot is loosened. And if you then pray, the third knot is loosened and you are freed entirely.

The Prophet ﷺ was also asked about a man who slept straight through until morning without praying — and the fact that the Sahabah asked about it shows this was not their norm. He said, ذَاكَ رَجُلٌ بَالَ الشَّيْطَانُ فِي أُذُنِهِ — "That is a man in whose ears Shaytan urinated." Alhamdulillah. Anyone who does not pray the night prayer has Shaytan using his ear as a toilet. We should not want that. The way out is simply to do something — get up in the night, pray even a few rakats for Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala, inshallah.

<!-- page 243 -->

Some of the scholars said this should be understood haqiqatan — literally — that Shaytan literally urinates in the ear. And the principle in Islamic jurisprudence is that الحقيقة لا تُصرف إلى المجاز إلا لضرورة — the literal meaning is not set aside for a metaphorical one unless there is a necessity to do so. Some scholars found no such necessity and took it literally. Others interpreted it metaphorically, saying it means Shaytan deprives that person of all the good that comes from spending part of the night in worship. Wallahu ta'ala a'lam.

Can you remind us of the du'a? الْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ الَّذِي أَحْيَانِي بَعْدَ مَا أَمَاتَنِي وَإِلَيْهِ النُّشُورُ — Praise be to the One who brought me back to life after He caused me to die, and to Him is my return.

<!-- page 244 -->

There is also the practice of reciting the closing verses of Surah Ali Imran upon waking. I spent many nights in Murabbir Haaj's tent, and every single night he would get up — usually four, sometimes five hours before Fajr — and he would wake you with those verses: إِنَّ فِي خَلْقِ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ وَاخْتِلَافِ اللَّيْلِ وَالنَّهَارِ لَآيَاتٍ لِأُولِي الْأَلْبَابِ الَّذِينَ يَذْكُرُونَ اللَّهَ قِيَامًا وَقُعُودًا وَعَلَى جُنُوبِهِمْ وَيَتَفَكَّرُونَ فِي خَلْقِ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ رَبَّنَا مَا خَلَقْتَ هَذَا بَاطِلًا — and I can still hear his voice reciting them. Inshallah, Allah give us tawfiq. It is all tawfiq from Allah — the ability to do these things is itself a gift from Him.

What prevents us from qiyam al-layl — what blocks it — is ghiba, eating what is haram, and similar violations. These are what close the door. This is a gift from Allah, and those who have experienced it and then lost it know that when they had it, it truly felt like a gift, and when it was taken away, it felt like a punishment. So make tawbah, ask Allah to restore it, and inshallah He will.

<!-- page 245 -->

The people of the Qawm used to hold themselves to such rigorous muhasaba that whenever something afflicted them they could trace the cause. One scholar said, "I once said something about someone that I should not have said, and I was deprived of tahajjud for forty days because of it." They could see the direct connection between cause and consequence. Another scholar said that near the end of his life he went bankrupt, and he realized it was the result of something that had happened forty years earlier — he had once said to a man, "Ya Muflis," meaning "you bankrupt one," and he understood that Allah had given him exactly what he had called that other man. As the saying goes, the good actions of the righteous are the bad actions of those who are near to Allah — meaning what is an ordinary slip for most people carries far greater weight for those whose hearts are refined.

<!-- page 246 -->

On the question of witr: do not pray the witr twice. Witr should be the last prayer of the night — that is the correct practice. It is perfectly valid to pray additional prayers after witr, but witr should come last. So if you go to bed early, it is better to pray your witr before you sleep, since witr should be completed before Fajr. If you wake up and forgot to pray it, you can still make it up after Fajr, but it is better done before. In the Hanafi madhhab, witr is wajib; in the other madhhabs, it is a sunnah mu'akkadah — a strongly emphasized sunnah.

The shaf' and witr should be prayed before you go to bed. Then if you rise during the night, do not repeat the witr; simply pray tahajjud in pairs — two by two, matna matna, as the hadith says. You do not pray four together; you pray two, then two.

If you are asking whether you can do tahajjud after witr — yes, you can. The preferable practice is to delay the witr so that it is the last prayer of the night. That is the ideal. But put it this way: if you are as certain as one can reasonably be that you will wake up before Fajr, then it is better to delay your witr. If your consistent, reliable practice is to wake up half an hour or an hour before Fajr and pray — and you are confident in that pattern — then it is better to delay. If, on the other hand, you are not consistent in waking up, then it is better to pray your witr before you go to sleep.

<!-- page 247 -->

And if you do wake up during the night before Fajr, you pray your tahajjud without ending with witr, since you have already prayed it and there is only one witr in the night.

— Ingrizi nahi malum hai. That's all the Urdu I know. There is an Afghani joke along these lines. An American traveled to Afghanistan having heard that the country was home to great spiritual teachers and saints. He wanted to find the greatest of them. So he approached an Afghani man and asked, the way Americans do — assuming everyone speaks English — "Who is the greatest spiritual teacher in Afghanistan?" The man replied, "Nami fahman." So the American went around asking after Nami Fahman, treating it as a name.

At some point a funeral procession passed by, and he saw a crowd watching solemnly. He went up to one of the Afghans and asked, "Who just died?" The man replied, "Nami fahman." Nami fahman, of course, means "I don't understand" in Persian.

<!-- page 248 -->

On another point — about taking things easy and not driving yourself into the ground — Shaykh Habib said in al-Qasida that you should travel the path with ease toward yourself. You need yourself to complete the journey. Your horse has to carry you to where you are going, so you treat your horse well. Do not wear it out to the point where it cannot carry you any further. He is not saying be lazy — he is saying do not exhaust yourself so completely that you lose the capacity to continue.

The goal is to move along the path with yearning. If you are not too hard on yourself, your self will actually begin to do these things. If you try to stay up the whole night, it is too much for the self. If you try to stay up half the night, it is still too much. The self loses energy and gives up entirely. But if you start small, something shifts — you begin to taste the sweetness of it, you feel the benefit, and you start to want it. The self begins to look forward to it. That is the point he is making.

And then he says: two rakats prayed by a lover are better than a thousand prayed without love. If you pray two rakats with shawq — with genuine longing, with a real desire for Allah's pleasure — Allah will love it. But if you are going through the motions thinking, "I have to do this, it is important, I must," with no love behind it — there is no love there. The action is driven by an idea in your head, not by your heart. The whole point is that all of this should arise from a yearning for Allah, as a mahabba from Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala.

<!-- page 249 -->

And then he says: was-samtu — silence. One of the diseases he addresses is lust for speech — al-kalam — which is, for most people, a deeply ingrained drive. The Prophet ﷺ said, مَنْ أُوتِيَ صَمْتًا فَقَدْ أُوتِيَ الْحِكْمَةَ — whoever is given silence as part of his nature has been given wisdom. There is wisdom in silence itself, simply in being silent. Even an ignorant person, if he is given the gift of silence, possesses a kind of wisdom — because at the very least, if you are ignorant, silence keeps you from making a fool of yourself.


If you have something to say, speech is better than silence. If you have nothing to say, it is permissible — mubah — but there is no benefit in it. And if that permissible speech amounts to casual pleasantries — how are you, where have you been — that is fine.

But if it turns to "did you hear about so-and-so," it slides toward either makruh or haram. Learning to govern the tongue is a serious discipline.

Imam al-Shafi'i said that whenever he was in a gathering and felt moved to speak, he would first look inward and ask himself whether he was speaking out of a desire of the nafs — to prove a point or display his knowledge. He would check whether he was content for someone else to say the very same thing. If he could honestly say yes, he would speak. If not, he would stay silent, as a tarbiyah for his nafs. He also said that whenever he entered a debate, he would make du'a that Allah cause the truth to appear on his opponent's tongue, so that he himself could submit to it. He had no wish to win — he would have preferred to lose, because losing would make him humble. That is a rajul, that is a man.

The Prophet, peace be upon him, was the most reserved of people. When he spoke, he spoke the truth. He was not what is called farfad. He said, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, inna ab'adakum minni wa ab'adakum ilayya yawm al-qiyamah, al-farfarin, al-mutafayhiqeen — the most distant of you from me, and the most distant to me on the Day of Judgment, are the farfarin and the mutafayhiqeen. The companions recognized the other terms, but did not know mutafayhiqeen, and he explained it as mutakaddar. Farfad is someone who speaks incessantly — farfara, yutharfiru — they simply never stop talking. He disliked the chatterbox. If you are teaching or saying something of use, that is another matter entirely. But speech for speech's sake is a waste.

<!-- page 243 -->

Imam Malik, rahimahu Allah, said of one of his students: ni'ma al-rajul, illa annahu yatakalamu kalam al-shahr fi yawm wahid — he is a fine man, except that he speaks a whole month's worth of words in a single day.

Then there is al-fikra wa huwa khali — reflection in solitude. Khali means to be in a place with no one around, engaged in reflection. In English we call this meditation, and it is a genuine practice — not yogic, but simply sitting and meditating. Within the Islamic tradition there are various forms. One is al-tafakkur fi al-mawt, or contemplating the states of the hereafter: you sit and walk through the raising from the grave and the scenes of the Day of Judgment. Another is tafakkur in the divine names: you move through them — ya Allah, ya Rahman, ya Rahim — and reflect on each attribute. So fikra in solitude benefits the heart.

Then there is suhbat al-akhyar — keeping company with the righteous. This practice is rooted in a Quranic injunction: يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَكُونُوا مَعَ الصَّادِقِينَ — O you who believe, have taqwa of Allah and be with the truthful ones. This is what is called suhba in the Persian tradition: keeping good company with righteous people in order to purify the heart.

Imam Ibn 'Ata'illah says in the Hikam: لَا تَصْحَبْ مَن لَا يُنْهِضُكَ حَالُهُ وَلَا يَدُلُّكَ عَلَى اللَّهِ مَقَالُهُ — Do not take as a companion someone whose state does not lift you, and whose speech does not direct you toward Allah.

Sidi Ahmad Zarruq notes in his commentary that this is not referring to spiritual masters — it is referring to the friends you spend ordinary time with. You should seek people who will elevate your state, not drag it down. And when they elevate yours, their own state rises too.

What this means in practice is that such people are in a good state with Allah — a state of gratitude. When you are around people who are genuinely grateful, it becomes difficult to complain. They are saying, alhamdulillah, subhanAllah, I feel truly grateful, Allahu Akbar, Allah has given us so much — and you find yourself sitting there unable to say, sorry, I can't agree, life is hard. They are in a different hal from you. So if you are the one in shukr, do not keep company with the one in shakwa. And if you are the one in shakwa, seek out the one in shukr, so that you may become like him. Do not drag him down — let him lift you up. That is the teaching.

And as for their speech directing you to Allah: there are people who, if you did complain to them, would gently say, subhanAllah — وَإِن تَعُدُّوا نِعْمَةَ اللَّهِ لَا تُحْصُوهَا — if you try to enumerate the blessings of Allah, you cannot count them; that applies to everyone. So perhaps instead of cataloguing your troubles, you might try counting his blessings. They help you shift your focus. And it is remarkable how a person's entire state can be transformed simply by sitting with someone who offers sincere and good counsel. A heart that was utterly dark and negative can be turned around entirely, because the effect was real and valuable — the person's perspective changes, and that is guidance.

That is what you want. And on this, Sidi Abu al-Hasan said: مَن دَلَّكَ عَلَى الدُّنْيَا فَقَدْ غَشَّكَ — whoever directs you toward this world has cheated you. If someone's entire counsel to you is about financial planning, acquiring property, securing your future in this life — that is a sign that he has cheated you, because you could die at any moment.

The example I like to use is this: imagine two salesmen come to you. The first says, "I have a house for sale. The only drawback is that it could disappear at any time — I cannot tell you exactly when, maybe fifty years from now, but it will be gone within seventy at the most, and it could vanish at any point before that." The second says, "I have another house. It will last forever and is guaranteed — but it is more expensive." Which one do you buy? Seriously — which one? Any person of sound intellect is going to buy the second one.

That is the Akhira. And you purchase it with your soul. The other house, you also purchase with your soul — but you buy it by destroying the soul, not by preserving it.

So: keeping good company. Then Sidi Abu al-Hasan also said that the one who tells you to rush and pile up deeds has merely exhausted you — if you believe you will reach Jannah through your deeds alone, you will wear yourself out. وَمَن دَلَّكَ عَلَى اللَّهِ فَقَدْ نَصَحَكَ — but the one who shows you the path to Allah has given you sincere counsel. That path is the contentment of Allah. If you are content with Allah, a small amount of action will be enough. If you are not, no amount of action will ever suffice.

And finally, he speaks of taking refuge — of returning to the One to whom all affairs return, since Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala says that to Allah all affairs return. Seeking refuge in Allah is, above all else, the truest remedy.

This is why a scholar of the path says that the only real cure for all these diseases of the heart is to go before Allah in a state of complete neediness — please, I need to change, and I need You to help me change — with a genuine sense of urgency. If that is the state a person is in, then insha'Allah, Allah will help them change.

For my own part, I truly believe that my coming to Islam was the result of a state I entered before I was Muslim — a state of complete iftiqar. I called on Allah by the name I knew, which was God, and I simply asked: give me guidance, show it to me. I was in a state of utter neediness. Most of the people I have met who became Muslim will confirm something similar: that there was a moment of absolute — just show me what to do. And then the guidance opened up, and it became very clear. Khalas. After that it becomes easy, insha'Allah.

I believe Allah is merciful, and that where there is sincerity, even within a person's limited and imperfect understanding, insha'Allah that is enough. There is an apocryphal story — and because it is apocryphal, no 'aqida is based on it, I want to be clear about that — but it carries a wisdom that has been told: about the Prophet Musa, alayhi al-salam, encountering a man who is speaking to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala about wanting to wash His feet, give Him a massage, and tend to Him — a man who is an anthropomorphist without knowing it.


And Musa, moved by righteousness, rebuked the man and explained the true meaning of tawhid. Yet Allah reminded Musa that he had been harsh, for the man had spoken those words out of love — he was simply ignorant. The right response to ignorance is to teach. So the point is that everyone is at the level where they are. Even the one bowing before an idol — that is his understanding; that is where he stands. This is why Allah says:

وَلَا تَجَعَلُوا لِلَّهِ أَنْدَادًا

Do not set up rivals to Allah —

وَأَنْتُمْ تَعْلَمُونَ

— while you know it is wrong. Once knowledge reaches a person, that is when shirk destroys him. If someone has no knowledge, and Allah's messenger has not yet reached him, Allah will not punish him:

مَا كُنَّمُ مُعَذِّبِينَ حَتَّنَا بَعْثَ رَسُولَهَ

So if someone does not know Islam and is genuinely seeking guidance, that guidance comes, inshallah.

وَاللَّهُ تَعْلَ عَنَهُمْ

Then the author says:

بِأَنْ يَكُونَ كَغَرِيقًا أَوْ كَمَنْ ضَلَّ بِتِيهِنْ لَا يَرَى الْغِيَاثَةِ مِنْ سِوَى الْمُهَيْمَنَ الْعَظِيمِ الْقَدْرِ وَهُوَ الْمُجِيبُ دَعْوَةٍ مُقَرِّي

He is saying that you must be like someone who is drowning, or like someone lost in a barren desert who sees no source of help except the Almighty God. This is precisely what happened to Yusuf Islam. I heard him describe it in a lecture: he was caught in the undertow at Malibu, being dragged out to sea — genuinely terrifying — and in that moment he called on God completely and promised: give me guidance and I will follow it, whatever you ask of me. That is the ghariq — the drowning man — with nowhere left to turn.

And then there is Ikrimah ibn Abi Jahl, whose guidance came on a boat carrying Abyssinian Christians. When the waves began to overwhelm the vessel and everyone feared they would drown, Ikrimah started calling on Hubal. The Christians on board laughed at him. They said: you are calling on an idol made of wood, sitting in Mecca, to save you out here? And in that moment it struck him — how absurd it was. He realized he could only call on Allah. And tawhid entered his heart right there. That is the state of the drowning man. And the author says this is in fact the most beneficial state — the one that will truly help us. It must reach the point where you are like a man going under.

So that is that.

<!-- page 193 -->

Now, on the actions that purify the heart, he says:

وَمَابِهِ لَلْقَلْبِ صَفْوً مِنْ عَمَلٍ أَنْفَعَهُ هُوَ الْمُدَامُ وَلَوْ يَقِلْ

As for the action most beneficial for purifying the heart, none surpasses the one that is consistent — even if it is small. This comes from the hadith:

خَيْرُ الْعَمَالِ أَدْوَنْ بَهَا وَإِمْقَلْ

The best actions are the ones maintained continuously, even if they are slight.

Think of it this way: if you take a coin and polish it hard with a few vigorous strokes, then set it down, versus taking the same coin and polishing it gently but without stopping — the second coin becomes bright and shiny in a way the first never will. One burst of effort cannot do what quiet consistency achieves over time. The heart works the same way. What encrusts it through accumulated wrong actions is not undone by one intense push. A little effort, maintained steadily, is what gradually breaks that crust away. That is why small deeds done consistently far outweigh large deeds done once.

The proof is in how the Companions conducted themselves. They were always consistent in their practices. The man who asked the Prophet about fasting every other day — the fast of Dawud — came to regret it in his old age. But the scholars took that very regret as evidence: the Companions held even voluntary practices so seriously that they would not abandon what they had taken upon themselves. It was not that the practice was obligatory — it was that they had committed to it.

And if you read the ahadith, you find that the Prophet used to fast so much that the Companions thought he never broke his fast — and then there were times when they thought he never fasted at all. He used to spend so much of the night in prayer that they thought he never slept — and then there were times when they thought he barely did any qiyam.

<!-- page 194 -->

There is something very important to understand about the Messenger of Allah. He is the universal prophet — his being encompasses the possibilities of every good believer. So in him, every good believer has a model. And different believers are opened to different things. There are people for whom qiyam is not the door Allah opens for them. There are people for whom recitation of the Quran is not their particular opening — their opening may be in hadith. Others are opened through sadaqah, not fasting. Others through fasting, not sadaqah. Some through wisdom. It is very rare for anyone to be opened through all of them simultaneously.

This is why, when the Prophet described the different gates of paradise — the gate for the fasters, the gate for the mujahideen — Abu Bakr asked: ya Rasulallah, a-yadkhulu ahadun hadhihi al-abwab kullaha? Can anyone enter through all of them? And the Prophet said: wa arjoo an takuna minhum — yes, and I hope you are one of them. As they say, I hope he was. The point is: keep working. That applies to all of us.

So some people are given broad and wide-ranging tawfiq. Imam Malik, for instance, wore fine clothes, used the best scents, burned oud — and to an outsider, especially among the ascetics, this looked extravagant. One of the zahids wrote him a letter saying: I cannot believe your state. You eat the finest flour, you wear expensive clothes, the perfume alone must cost a fortune — you would do better giving all of that up and spending less time with people. He was, after all, teaching extensively in the masjid.

So the zahid was essentially telling Imam Malik: compared to me, you are in a bad state. This is what is called marjab — essentially a kind of spiritual boasting. And Imam Malik wrote back an extraordinary letter. He said: ya Abdullah — servant of Allah — Allah has opened for His servants many doors of goodness.

<!-- page 195 -->

He then says: include action done in the absence of witnesses, and action done out of love for Allah or in awe of Him. So one of the things that benefits the heart is performing acts that no one else can see. Giving charity privately. Praying in a way that no one observes. This protects you from riya' and ujb and everything that flows from them.

There is a story — it was actually Fudayl ibn Ayyad, not Bishr al-Hafi — who was once walking down a street and overheard someone say: there goes a man who spends the entire night in prayer. When Fudayl heard it, he burst into tears and said: I cannot remember ever spending a single complete night in prayer. And then he said: how great is the honor Allah has bestowed on me by veiling me and making people think I am better than I am. That idea — of concealing one's state, of hiding — is the better path.

And then action done out of love: that is the highest. The scholars say there are two principal motivations. One is khawf — fear — which, when it reaches the level of khawf maqamihi, becomes Jalal: awe of Allah's greatness, a profound sense of hayba in the divine presence. The other is mahabba — love — born of Allah's mercy, yearning for Allah, doing things out of that longing. That is the highest motive, and the author tells you that the final maqam is mahabba.

He also says that the purest actions are those of the zahid — the one who is free of worldly wants, who does what he does seeking nothing in return, no thanks, no gratitude, not even reward in the akhirah. He does it solely for Allah's sake. That is the highest and best action for the heart. Zuhd is not an outward condition — it is the absence of attachment within the heart. The zahid is not like the Christian ascetic who wears rough clothing and practices flagellation. In Islam, the ascetic is the one for whom the world has no grip on the heart. That is why Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf was a zahid, even though he was one of the wealthiest men in Mecca.

<!-- page 196 -->

Then the author says that the actions of those who strive out of hope are higher and more exalted than those driven by fear alone. If fear is your only motivation, that is still valid — Allah tells us to have khawf — but hope lifts the action higher. And the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, is the Habibullah — the beloved of Allah — and Allah loved him. That is the pinnacle: mutual love between the servant and his Lord.

He then says: particularly, action whose benefits extend to others. An action that purifies the heart is made greater when it reaches beyond the self. Charity does not only benefit your own soul — it benefits another human being. And in every creature that possesses a moist liver — that is, every living thing — there is reward. Water an animal and there is reward. Plant a tree and every creature that eats from it, for as long as that tree stands, earns you reward. All of these things are elevated precisely because they extend outward.

Then he speaks of fasting in relation to one's character. If you are someone who has poor control over his appetite — if gluttony is your weakness — then fasting is particularly powerful for you. It strikes at the root of that disorder. But for someone who already has reasonable control over his relationship with food, fasting is still good, yet it will not work the same transformation as it does on the one who genuinely needs to discipline that aspect of himself.

Similarly with the miser: for a generous person, giving in charity is natural. For someone who is stingy, giving out is a far greater struggle — and for that very reason, it carries more weight. The hadith on this is direct: the best sadaqah is what you give when you are healthy, when you want to hold on to it, and when you fear poverty. That is when giving out really costs you something. That is the sadaqah that truly rubs shaitan's nose in it.

<!-- page 197 -->

Then he says: you earn reward in proportion to your avoidance of wrong. And this brings up a related point. There are people in the ummah who spent the better part of their lives in disobedience, then in old age made tawbah and began lecturing the young. But that person does not carry the same maqam as the young person who had every opportunity and every impulse toward disobedience — and chose otherwise. The older person no longer has the energy for most of those wrongs anyway. Tawbah comes more easily when the temptation is largely gone.

There is also a real danger in the opposite direction: the heart that has been in disobedience for decades sometimes becomes so encrusted that a genuine return becomes very difficult. And yet alhamdulillah, many Muslims do make tawbah as they grow older. They reach forty, forty-five, and something shifts. In Tunisia, they say the year of responsibility is forty — because that is when the wahy came to the Prophet. Which has given some people the mistaken impression that you can do whatever you like before forty. This is ignorance, pure and simple. And yet it has become a kind of cultural artifact in parts of the Muslim world — people who say: when I go to hajj, I will grow my beard and become a proper Muslim. Some of them do come back changed. But these attitudes are a marker of how far we have drifted from our tradition.

What Allah loves is the young person who is raised up in obedience. Shabab — youth — the Arabs generally put between fifteen and thirty-five, some say up to forty. After forty you are no longer a shab; you are a rajul, a man.

<!-- page 198 -->

Then he says: and also the contribution of a miser — giving out of the best of his wealth, while he is in sound health, seeking the pleasure of Allah — all of these things purify the heart. Moreover, anything done with discretion and concealment is also purifying. And the best of endeavors is the one that purifies the heart. So the best thing you can do with your life is what is genuinely good for your heart.

Likewise — and this is the other side — the worst things are those that harden the heart, especially when a person persists in them or takes pleasure in them. The nafs that enjoyed them: those are the things most destructive to the heart.

One thing that hardens the heart is speaking at length without mentioning Allah. This is recorded in the Muwatta' of Imam Malik. Sayyidina Isa is reported to have said: do not sit in gatherings without remembering Allah, for a gathering in which Allah is not mentioned will harden the heart — and you will not even notice it happening. The hardening comes gradually, spiritually and even, one might say, physically — and you are unaware. But the more the tongue is occupied with remembrance of Allah, the softer the heart becomes. That is simply the reality. The more dhikr someone does, the more compassion flows from them, and the more they benefit.

<!-- page 199 -->

Now this brings us to a truly important point. Allah says that a wrong action that produces in its wake a sense of shame and brokenness before Allah is better than an act of obedience that produces pride and arrogance in the one who performs it. And this is the secret of wrong actions — the wisdom concealed within them. Allah permitted us to stumble so that through the stumbling we might arrive at this state. The Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, said: if you did not commit wrong actions, I would fear for you something worse than wrong actions — namely, being pleased with yourself, admiring yourself, vanity, the feeling that you are something wonderful. That is worse.

Now, Sidi Ahmad Zarruq clarifies this so there is no confusion. He says the good of ta'a is in the very essence of ta'a, and the evil he is describing is what obedience results in when it goes wrong. So do not misread this as saying that there is such a thing as good ma'asiyah or bad ta'a. Ta'a is good. Ma'asiyah is bad. Full stop. What is being said is about the secondary results. If the secondary result of an act of obedience was arrogance — and the secondary result of a disobedience was tawbah — then in terms of what was ultimately produced, the tawbah was the better outcome. The obedience generated poison; the disobedience generated a return to Allah.

Think of people in America who commit terrible crimes, go to prison, and find their way to Allah there. That happens often. The disobedience was genuinely wrong. But it became part of the causal chain through which Allah guided them. It was not a good thing — but it resulted in a good thing. And that is what he is saying: a wrong action that results in humility is preferable to an obedience that results in arrogance, in terms of what they produce in the heart.

Then he says:

وَذَرَّةٌ مِنْ عَمِلَ الْقَلْبِ الْعَلِي مِثْرُ الرِّضَى وَالزُهْدِ وَالتَوَكْرِ أَغْضَرُوا عَنَدَ اللَّهِ مِنْ جِبَالِ شَمَخْنَ مِنْ ظَوَاهِرَ الْعَمَالِ

An atom's weight of a lofty inward action — such as contentment, zuhd, or trust in Allah — is greater with Allah than mountains of outward deeds performed without any heart. And this is the same principle as:

فَإِنَّ رَكَعَتَيْنِ مِنْ مُحِبٍ خَيْرَ مِنْ أَلْثٍ مِنْ غَيْرِ حُبِّ

Two raka'ats prayed with love are better than a thousand prayed without it. Immeasurably better.

<!-- page 200 -->

Then he mentions what Imam Suhnoon, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

وَتَرْكُوا دِرْهَمٍ لِكَوْنِهِ حَظَرَ أَظْدَرُ مِنْ تَصَدُقَاتٍ وَعُمَرَ

Leaving a single daniq of what Allah has made haram is better than performing seventy thousand hajj and umrah. A daniq is the smallest unit of currency in the Arabic tradition — a farthing, a penny. And they also relate that Imam Ja'far al-Sadiq said there are four things whose very best still carries a kind of diminishment:

أَرْبَعَةٌ عِزُّهَا وَظُلُّ

الدَّيْن وَلَوْ دَانِق

A debt, even if it is only a daniq.

أَبِنْتْ وَلَوْ مَرِيَمْ

A daughter, even if she is Maryam — not because daughters are lesser than sons in worth, but because in that social context she lacks the physical strength and social protection a man possesses. And the word aziz in Arabic, let us not forget, also means qawi — physically strong, or strong in position. عَزِيزُ النَّصَر.

وَسُؤَادْ وَلَوْ كَيفَ الطَّرِيق

Asking a question, even if it is only: which way do I go? There is a humility inherent in asking that is itself a small diminishment of the self — not a wrong thing, just an honest reality.

وَالْوَحْشَةَ وَلَوْ لَيْلَ

And being alone — even for a single night.

<!-- page 201 -->

Now we come to the root causes of the diseases. And what I will do is cover the treatment of diseases through dhikr next week — inshallah, Shaykh Khatri will be here, and I will ask him to teach that class. He knows this book deeply. He loves it. He has spent a great deal of time with it. I have read sections of it with him — and I read much of it with Shaykh Abdullah as well. We actually completed a khatm of it on hajj, on the day of Arafat. We went through every disease, named them all, and made dua asking Allah to purify us of all of them. Inshallah, for all of us.

Now he says: the comprehensive root — in the view of both Imam al-Hilali and Imam Ibn Askar — of all these diseases is love of the temporal world, hubb al-dunya. There is some difference of opinion on this, as we saw: Ibn Abbad considered the root to be tama' — desire. But you can see how tama' flows from hubb al-dunya. And when Ibn Askar says the root is hubb al-riyasa — love of status and power — even that is hubb al-dunya, because why do you want status? You want power, you want the world. So in the end it really does come back to hubb al-dunya. There is a hadith on this — though it is classified as weak — ra'su kulli khatiy'atin hubb al-ajila: the love of this world is at the root of every wrong action.

Ibn Ata'illah, on the other hand, considered the root to be a man's self-satisfaction with himself. That is a distinct and fascinating take. He was a Maliki jurist who died in 709 AH, corresponding to 1309 CE, and he is considered one of the greatest masters of this science. He wrote the Hikam. Imam al-Banna said that had it been permissible to recite something other than the Quran in prayer, it would have been permissible to recite the Hikam — a clear hyperbole, but an indication of the profound esteem in which the work was held.

<!-- page 202 -->

In the thirty-fifth aphorism, Ibn Ata'illah says that the source of every disobedience, every indifference, and every passion is self-satisfaction with the self. And the source of every obedience, every vigilance, and every virtue is dissatisfaction with the self. He says: it is better for you to keep company with an ignorant man who is dissatisfied with himself than with a learned man who is satisfied with himself. And then: what knowledge is there in a self-satisfied scholar? And what ignorance is there in an unlearned man who is dissatisfied with himself — and who therefore keeps asking, keeps wanting to learn?

This connects directly to something strange about modern culture. Think of the whole industry of self-help books aimed at getting rid of shame, building confidence, feeling good about yourself. But why is the person standing in that self-help aisle in the first place? Because he is dissatisfied with himself. And if he were completely self-satisfied, he would never pick up a book. The author of the book, incidentally, has no real interest in your becoming self-satisfied — because then you would stop buying books. Most of that industry is built on keeping you just dissatisfied enough to keep purchasing. But the point stands: dissatisfaction with the self is what drives a person to want to change and improve.

If anyone here were truly satisfied with themselves, you would not be in this class studying the diseases of the heart. The very fact that people have these struggles is what moves them to seek help. If you feel you lack guidance, you seek guidance. The one who submits to Allah begins seeking because he knows he needs Allah's direction. But if you are walking through life perfectly content — I am already guided, I have no need — you will never reach out.

<!-- page 203 -->

Now, Sidi Ahmad Zarruq identifies three signs of contentment with the soul.

The first is seeing that the haqq belongs to oneself. This is interesting to consider in the modern context: we have a bill of rights but no bill of responsibilities. Islam is far more focused on your responsibilities than on your rights. The Quran, in a real sense, is a bill of Allah's rights over us. And the concept of human rights, as understood in the secular modern framework, carries a very different set of assumptions than the Islamic one. Human beings do have rights — but in the Islamic understanding, those rights originate with Allah. They are Allah's rights embedded in His servants. If someone is protected from oppression, it is because Allah has a haqq that His creation not be oppressed. If someone takes a life in accordance with shari'ah, it is because Allah demands His right be fulfilled. The human being has a right — but it was given by Allah, and the other person fulfills it because he fears Allah.

This is why taqwa — fear of Allah — is the very foundation of a Muslim society. The first sign of self-satisfaction, then, is always seeing the rights as belonging to the self: they wronged me, it is all their fault, I am always the injured party. This is an extremely common psychological pattern.

There is a Moroccan story about a shaykh and a self-satisfied scholar who disliked him. This scholar came to visit one day and said: the least of my students is better than you. The shaykh said: show me. Come to my madrasa tomorrow. The next day, the shaykh secretly instructed all his students: when the visiting scholar enters, do not return his salam. And when my student comes in — who had been set up to visit — do not say salamu alaykum to him either.

<!-- page 204 -->

The self-satisfied scholar walked in, no one returned his greeting, and his reaction was immediate outrage: are these your students? Have they not heard what Allah commands? Have they not heard His words — return a greeting with what is better, or at least return it in kind?

Then the shaykh's own student came in. No one returned his salam. He said: salamu alaykum — again. Still nothing. Then he said: O brothers, please forgive me. I must have done something wrong. What did I do? Why have you not responded? Whatever I have done, please excuse me.

Those two responses reveal everything. The self-satisfied man arrived saying: I have a right, and you have violated it. The student arrived thinking: what right of yours did I fail to fulfill? One looked outward in demand; the other looked inward in concern. That is why, even as the least of the shaykh's students, the young man showed something the scholar — for all his learning — did not possess. This is the hikmah behind Ibn Ata'illah's words: what knowledge does a self-satisfied scholar truly have? And what real ignorance remains in an unlearned man who is genuinely dissatisfied with himself and wants to be taught?

The second sign of contentment with the self is ignoring one's own faults by interpreting them away — convincing yourself that you are already in a state of purity, even if you have to construct a justification to get there. For instance, a person gets angry and tells himself: well, the Prophet said anger can be from the best of the ummah, and I am from the best of the ummah, so this is fine. He sees the fault but reframes it so that it no longer registers as a fault. This is what the poet meant when he said: the eye of contentment does not see faults. Just as the eye of one in love cannot see the beloved's flaws — and one of the signs of falling out of love is that you begin noticing them all: I never noticed before that one ear is larger than the other.

<!-- page 205 -->

The third sign of self-satisfaction is excessive self-pity — poor me, I am having such a hard time — chronic complaining and self-indulgent lamentation.

Those are the three signs of a person who is satisfied with himself. The three signs of the opposite — of a person genuinely dissatisfied with himself — are these:

The first is constant self-scrutiny, a persistent suspicion of one's own intentions. And this is modeled even by Sayyidina Yusuf, peace be upon him, who is a prophet — masoom. There is a scholarly disagreement about the precise meaning of his statement, which Sayyid Ahmad Zarruq and others discuss. But Sayyidina Yusuf said: I will not declare my nafs innocent — for by its nature, the nafs commands to evil. So always examine your intentions. Ask yourself: am I doing this for riya'? Hazrat Abu Bakr said: the only one who feels safe from nifaq is the hypocrite. The true believer is always watchful over himself, always alert to the blemishes of the soul. This is reflected in the dua the Prophet made: do not leave me to my own soul even for the blink of an eye, or less than that.

The second sign is forcing the self to do difficult things — things it dislikes. Eating less. Giving out money. Going out in the path of Allah. These are makruh to the nafs — the self naturally recoils from them. Jihad is genuinely hard. Charity is genuinely hard. The nafs wants to hold on to what it has. Compelling it to do otherwise is one of the marks of a person who has not surrendered to self-satisfaction.

<!-- page 206 -->

Abu Uthman said: whoever sees anything good about himself has not truly seen the faults of his soul.

Now there is an obvious question here — and it is an important one, because the whole Western discourse around self-help, psychology, and modern wellness is built on the opposite premise. Is this not just self-loathing? Is this not low self-esteem? Is this not exactly what the self-esteem movement told us is harmful?

And the answer is: look at the actual people shaped by this tradition. Look at the real shuyukh. They are not miserable. They are not broken. They carry genuine humility alongside genuine dignity. Consider Aisha, may Allah be pleased with her. She was the wife of the best of creation. She was physically beautiful. She was among the most brilliant minds in Islamic history — and in human history. She came from a noble family, the aristocracy of Quraysh. And yet, when she yearned for Allah to declare her innocence in the incident of the slander, she said:

وَكُنتُ أَرَى نَفْسِي حَقِيرًۭا

I considered myself so insignificant that I would never have imagined the Quran would be revealed on my account.

Despite all of that — beauty, lineage, brilliance, position — she did not regard herself as someone for whom Quran would descend. And yet Aisha was not a woman without self-confidence. She was a woman of enormous strength. She was one of the very few people who questioned the Prophet directly — she challenged him on a point of Quran when he said that a person given an easy reckoning would enter paradise:

يَدْخُلُ الْجَنَّةِ بِلَا حِسَابٍ وَلَمْ يَخُلُ اللَّهُ فَسَوْفْ يُحَاسِبُ حِسَابٍ يَسِيرًۭا

She pressed him: but how can you say that, given what Allah says? Most of the Companions would not have done that. It takes a person of extraordinary inner confidence to question the Prophet of Allah on a matter of Quranic interpretation.

<!-- page 207 -->

She also led an army out. She later regretted it, but in the moment she said: he is wrong and we are right — let us go resolve this. She rode out in her hawdaj to the Battle of the Camel. And when one of the men addressed her as ya umm al-mu'minin, she replied:

لا أُمِّنك

Which in the Arabic of the time carried the force of: do not call me your mother — you are no son of mine. That is a sharp, strong response. She was a strong woman.

And yet, with all of that, her sense of her own standing before Allah was one of profound smallness. That is what this teaching is about. It is not about collapsing into self-loathing. It is not about erasing confidence. It is about never becoming content with where you are — always knowing there is more, always wanting to improve. Be confident, yes. But do not be complacent.

No matter how accomplished you become, there is always someone better. No matter how much Quran you have mastered, there is always more mastery to pursue. The master calligrapher looks at his work and sees room to improve. If he stops seeing it, he stops improving. And every one of these sciences — every field of knowledge, every spiritual state, every skill — is endless. True mastery belongs not to the one who has arrived, but to the one who never stops. The one committed to constant improvement is the real master, because they are always moving forward, always refining their technique. But the moment a person is self-satisfied, the improvement stops — and in a very real sense, so does the life of the heart.

<!-- page 208 -->

This is what he means when he says that self-dissatisfaction acts as an impetus to seek virtuous character and to remain vigilant about what is unbecoming. So you remain watchful, alhamdulillah.

We will stop there. Next week we will finish this section, and then the week after that I will go through the adhkar al-sabah wa al-masa' — the morning and evening remembrances — which everyone should be doing. I will get you a copy of those. And then we will do the khawatir. That is how the remaining sessions will be structured.

Now there was a question from the previous class about women and male slaves — specifically whether a woman may own a bondsman. Yes, that is permissible in the fiqh. But they are not mahram, and all the normal rules regarding seclusion and mixing apply. There can be no sexual relationship between the two. That was the question.

Now before we close, let me do the mafatih — the keys — because I did not cover that last week.

Mafatih is the plural of miftah, which means key in Arabic. The root, if you look at the word, is the same root as the verb to open. And the wazn — the morphological pattern — it sits on is that of a tool: the thing you use to do the action. Miknasa is the thing you use to sweep. The miftah is the thing you use to open.

<!-- page 209 -->

In Arabic, the word for key does not just mean an object that unlocks something — it means the thing that causes an opening. The metaphor is richer than the English word "key" carries. And it connects directly to one of the names of Allah: al-Fattah — the One who opens. People who carry spiritual keys have the ability to open things that are otherwise closed. And there are many ahadith on this: "such and such is the key to such and such." Sabr is the key to al-faraj — to the opening, to relief. If you want faraj from Allah, the key is patience


The shari'ah, then, is what tells us what is good and what is bad or ugly — the intellect has no independent access to that. It can sometimes arrive at the right answer, but not always, which makes it a relative and limited source of guidance rather than an absolute one. The shari'ah indicates what is hasanah and what is sayyah. And when a person recognizes that his nafs will not save him or help him, he turns to what actually will help him — namely, doing what Allah has commanded — and that recognition leads him to act in accordance with Allah and His message.

The poem's point, the sheikh explains, is that the origin of both heart disease and spiritual well-being is the company one keeps, for the hadith states that a man is upon the deen of his companion. If you keep company with someone who delays his prayer, who neglects zakat, or who commits wrong actions, you will inevitably drift toward those same things. Conversely, good company will pull you in a good direction. This is precisely why people must examine who they spend time with, because one's companions are a reliable indicator of one's own spiritual state. The sheikh offers a vivid illustration: if you sit at the door of a bar, you will smell the foulness of alcohol; if you sit at the door of a perfumer, you will breathe in the fragrance of perfume. Whatever you are near, you are affected by. Proximity to goodness brings goodness; proximity to corruption brings corruption.

For this reason, Luqman al-Hakim — mentioned in the Quran — is recorded in the Muwatta of Imam Malik as having told his son: sit with the ulama. Keep their company. The ulama are people who possess knowledge and live by it, and sitting with them is like rain falling on dry earth and bringing it back to life. That is what their company does to the earth of your heart — it revives it, and it does so in a way you may not even notice. Simply being in the presence of righteous, knowledgeable people allows the heart to stir back to life, and iman begins to grow and increase within it.

He says that sitting with the ulama — the people of true knowledge, the arrifin — will take a person from six things to six things: from doubt to certainty; from doing things for other than Allah to doing them with sincerity, with ikhlas, for Allah alone; from heedlessness to the remembrance of Allah; from desiring this world to desiring the next; from arrogance to repentance; and from corrupt inner character and bad intentions toward people to sincere, wholesome character and genuine goodwill. Ibn Ata'illah makes a related point in his hikam: do not keep company with someone whose state — his hal, his condition — does not elevate you, and whose speech does not direct you toward Allah. If a person's presence neither lifts your himma nor points you toward Allah, that company is harmful.

Of all the diseases examined in the Mathara, the comprehensive cure — the one that addresses all of them — is the remembrance of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala. More than anything else, dhikr is what heals the heart. Dhikr includes recitation of Quran, salah, and all the formulas of praise: subhanallah, alhamdulillah, subhanallah al-adheem, la ilaha illallah, allahu akbar, la hawla wa la quwwata illa billah — as well as the supplications the Prophet ﷺ has transmitted to us. But the highest and best form of dhikr is qira'at al-Quran. A person who is in a state of dhikr will have a tahir heart — a pure heart.

Sayyidi Ahmad ibn Hanbal is reported to have seen Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala ninety-nine times in his dreams. In one of them he was asked: of all the things that draw a slave near to Allah, which is the greatest? Allah answered him: recitation of Quran. He then asked: Ya Allah, with understanding or without understanding? And Allah answered, according to the Imam, bi fahmin aw bi ghayri fahmin — with understanding or without. This means that even someone who does not understand the words he is reading is still drawing close to Allah through the Quran.

In Sahih al-Bukhari, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said that the difference between one who remembers Allah and one who does not is like the difference between the living and the dead. Whoever remembers Allah — qalbuhu hayy — his heart is alive, and so he is alive. Whoever does not remember Allah — qalbuhu mayyit — his heart is dead, and so he is considered dead. A sound hadith also says: make remembrance of Allah until people say you are mad. This is because one thing that marks people who are troubled in their minds is that they talk to themselves constantly. Someone who remembers Allah all the time may appear, outwardly, very similar — a non-Muslim who sees you walking down the street doing dhikr may well think you are talking to yourself. That is what the hadith points to.

There is also the sound hadith, transmitted in various narrations, in which the Prophet ﷺ asks: shall I not tell you what the best of all your actions is — the purest in the sight of your Lord and King, the highest in rank among all the deeds that carry degrees, better for you than spending gold and silver, and better even than going out to meet your enemy in jihad, striking them and being struck, killing them and being killed? They said: of course, tell us, Ya Rasool Allah. He said: dhikr Allah ta'ala — the remembrance of Allah. Some people wonder how remembrance could be better than jihad. The ulema — may Allah have mercy on them — explain that dhikr Allah is the ghaya, the end itself, the very purpose for which we were created. Jihad and everything else are wasa'il — means. And no means, however noble, is greater than the end it serves. We were created to remember Allah, which is why dhikr is the highest thing in the dunya.

Allah says: اذْكُرُونِي أَذْكُرْكُمْ — remember Me, and I will remember you. Whoever remembers his Lord, Allah remembers him. And whoever Allah remembers has been enriched in both this world and the next, and stands in need of nothing. Another distinguishing feature of dhikr — unlike hajj, fasting, or even formal prayer — is that it is not bound to a specific time or place. It can accompany every human act. There are dhikrs related to going to the toilet, to eating, to marital relations — every moment of life has a dhikr belonging to it. And it is said that whoever is in a state of remembrance of Allah is never struck by calamity in a way that harms him, because whatever befalls someone who was remembering Allah only raises him in rank. If he dies while remembering Allah, he dies as a martyr. This is the treatment for the diseased and foul soul — remembrance of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala.

When you wish to engage in dhikr, you begin with istighfar — seeking forgiveness — because the purpose of dhikr is purification, and the reason purification is needed is the wrong actions one has accumulated. So the process begins by asking Allah to remove those wrong actions. Once He removes them, the dhikr that follows will then remove their lingering effects, for wrong actions leave traces and effects on the heart.

You then send prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ, because salah upon the Prophet acts as a murabbi — a nurturer — for the heart and as a means of its purification. This is why most of the later ulema specifically encouraged these two practices: istighfar for the wrong actions most of us are engaged in, and salah upon the Prophet because by the ijma' of the ummah, it is the only dhikr that is absolutely guaranteed to be accepted by Allah. Anyone who says Allahumma salli alayhi wa sallim Muhammad is maqbool — accepted. Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala commanded this with a command He Himself initiated, which is unique among all of His commands — He does not begin with the action Himself except in the case of prayer upon the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. He said: إِنَّ اللَّهَ وَمَلَائِكَتَهُ يُصَلُّونَ عَلَى النَّبِيِّ — and then enjoined the believers: يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا صَلُّوا عَلَيْهِ وَسَلِّمُوا تَسْلِيمًا — pray upon the Messenger of Allah and greet him with peace. Allahumma salli ala Sayyidina Muhammad wa sallim. Whoever prays upon the Prophet ﷺ once, Allah prays upon him ten times — this is established in sahih hadith. And whoever Allah has prayed upon ten times, what else could he possibly need?

أَلصَّلَاةُ عَلَيْهِ، عَلَيْهِ نُورُهُ فِي قَدْرِهِ، وَنُورُهُ فِي قَدْرِهِ، وَنُورٌ عَلَى الصِّرَاطِ — the Prophet of Allah ﷺ said that prayer upon him is light in this world, light in the grave, and light on the sirat. It gives you guidance — and that guidance — when you need it most. It illuminates the grave so that it is light rather than dark. And it illuminates the sirat so that you can see your way across it. Light in the heart, as the Prophet ﷺ said.

Because we have been commanded to pray upon the Prophet ﷺ, it is generally good to begin by reciting the actual ayah, for in doing so you are fulfilling the command of Allah — this is called imtithal amrillah. That command is obligatory at least once in a lifetime, but you can fulfill it anew every single time you send prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ. The one who prays upon the Prophet should observe the same adab he would observe when remembering Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala: it is mandub for him to be in wudu, mandub to face the qiblah, and so on — the same courtesies that are mandub for prayer apply here as well.

He also mentions istihdhar — that when you pray upon the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, you should do so as if you were in his presence. In the same way that ihsan in prayer means worshipping Allah as though you see Him, istihdhar in salah upon the Prophet ﷺ means bringing his presence before you as though he were there — calling to mind the guidance he gave us, all the things we owe him as our wasila for teaching us la ilaha illa Allah and for conveying to us the knowledge of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala. And you remember that Allah Himself and His angels send prayer upon this Messenger, so in doing so you are participating in something that Allah Himself declared He does.

Another thing to be mindful of in dhikr is what is called lahn — incorrect pronunciation. You should avoid it to the best of your ability. Some people simply cannot pronounce every Arabic sound correctly, but the effort must still be made. He notes that some mispronunciations constitute a ma'siya or a khata', a sin or an error, while others can reach the level of ridda — potentially taking a person out of Islam. As for those who cannot distinguish between sounds like dad and dha' — the ajamiya — if there is a genuine heaviness in the tongue, a physical impediment that persists despite every effort to correct it, then that person is ma'dhur: excused by the shari'ah. He should still engage in dhikr even if the pronunciation falls short, because the incapacity is real and Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear.


He receives the reward, and no wrongdoing is entailed by performing the dhikr in that way. Among the greatest acts of worship is also meditating on Allah's creation. Allah mentions in the Quran those who remember Him standing, sitting, and reclining on their sides, and who reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth. Dhikr and tafakkur — remembrance and reflection — are thus joined together.

It is said that the action of the heart, which is tafakkur, is better than all the actions of the limbs. This is why one narration states that a single moment of reflection is better than a year of worship. The signs of genuine reflection fall into four directions.

The first is reflecting on the signs of Allah. The second is reflecting on Allah's creation and what those signs point to — and this leads to love of Allah, because one sees everything Allah has done for the human being. The third is reflecting on Allah's promised rewards and the signs connected to them; each sign kindles a desire for the akhira, so that the mark of this reflection is a yearning, a raghba, for jannah. The fourth is reflecting on Allah's promised punishment; the sign of this reflection is that fear takes root in the heart and the person turns away from disobedience.

There is also reflection on the weakness of the self — the harshness of the nafs toward Allah despite His unceasing ihsan. When one contemplates all that Allah has given and then honestly weighs how little one has offered in return, this reflection produces haya, a deep sense of shame before Allah — shame that He has given so much, while one has given so little in return.

The heart is what distinguishes good from evil, and the source of all these matters lies in the inrushings that come to the heart. These inrushings arrive like a guest descending upon the heart, and they take different forms.

The question Qais raised concerned what people today call the mutasawwifa. Imam al-Maqari distinguished between the Sufiya and the mutasawwifa. The mutasawwifa are those who merely claim to be Sufis. A common sign of such people is that they engage in abundant dhikr while neglecting the wajibat — the obligatory duties.

The Shaykh's answer was direct: the first and foremost obligation on every human being is to acquire knowledge — before anything else. A person who has not learned the fara'id al-'ayn, the individually obligatory matters, has no maqam before Allah. It is as though he stands on the same footing as an animal, or worse, because an animal is not morally responsible, whereas this person is astray and is making no effort to correct himself.

Shaykh 'Abd al-Khadim cited many scholars on this point — Imam al-Sha'rani, Imam al-Suyuti, and all the great imams who mastered both the outward sciences and tasawwuf — all of them affirm that fiqh must precede tasawwuf. If a person pursues tasawwuf without first grounding himself in fiqh, he will go astray. There is no tasawwuf without the knowledge of the fara'id. Tasawwuf comes after fiqh.

Even birr al-walidayn — dutiful care of one's parents — though it is the highest virtue Allah mentions in the Quran after tawhid, still comes after learning what Allah has made obligatory. If caring for one's parents is preventing one from acquiring that obligatory knowledge, then one is committing a wrong by abandoning what is more right.

Regarding the question about women: a woman in a state of menstrual impurity may perform all forms of dhikr that men perform. She may not recite from the Quran extensively, though there are nuances — if she has memorized the Quran, she may recite what is needed to retain her memorization, or she may recite short phrases such as ta'awwudh or verses like Surat al-Mulk in that capacity. She may not touch the physical Quran during that period. Beyond these restrictions, she may freely recite the adhkar, la ilaha illallah, and the prayers upon the Prophet ﷺ.

Regarding the question about non-Muslims in America who are drawn to Islam through its spiritual teachings — those who hear about the inner meanings of the tradition and feel themselves drawn closer — the Shaykh's answer was that the best path for all people is submission to the will of Allah and acceptance of His commands as He has made them binding. When the Prophet ﷺ called people to Islam, he began with tawhid and then told them that Allah had made five prayers obligatory. He called them first to the unity of Allah and then to prayer. That is the methodology of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, and that is what we should be calling people to.

In contemporary culture there is a pervasive false or pseudo-spirituality built on the notion that one can access haqiqa without shari'a. This is a Dajjalic phenomenon — it misleads people and draws them away from the truth. It is, in effect, anti-spirituality, because genuine spirituality is protected by the Shari'a. The fruit is protected by its peel; if you strip the peel before the fruit has ripened, you ruin it. Likewise, if you reach for spirituality before you have shielded yourself with the Shari'a, you will destroy your soul and become one of those false figures — the mutamalli'in — people who project an outward appearance of spiritual attainment while their hearts remain filled with every disease.

The Shaykh also addressed the importance of concealing one's faults. If a person has committed wrong, he should veil it and make tawbah; Allah will forgive him. But if he publicizes it openly, the harm multiplies, because the sin spreads as a tribulation beyond himself.

He cited the hadith of the man who came to the mosque and told the Prophet ﷺ that he had committed zina and asked to be given the prescribed punishment. The Prophet turned away from him. The man followed him outside and repeated himself. Finally the Prophet looked at him and said: "Did you not just perform wudu and pray with us?" — meaning: that is what Islam is for, to purify one's wrongs. The man, however, persisted until the punishment was carried out. The Prophet ﷺ also said that whoever commits such offenses should cover himself with Allah's covering and make tawbah, but whoever comes forward and openly admits his sin, the prescribed punishment will be applied.

The Shaykh then turned to the question of companionship. For worldly matters, one should keep company with those who have less, not those who have more — because constantly comparing oneself to those who have bigger houses and better cars feeds love of the dunya. But for matters of the akhira, one should always seek the company of those who are better. If one only keeps company with people whose deen is weaker, one begins to think one's own deen is sufficient and grows complacent.

The people of Allah, he noted, tend to see themselves as lesser than everyone around them, so the danger of pride does not really apply to them in the same way. Keeping their company benefits you precisely because their humility makes you feel how much further you still have to go.

This is also why one should never look down on anyone. A person who appears to be in a worse state today might make a tawbah tomorrow that Allah accepts and rise to become a wali of Allah — while the one who despised him might later stumble. No one can know another person's standing with Allah. So one should never feel superior to anyone in this dunya, and should always seek out the company of those who are better in their akhira.


those are rightly guided, if they truly follow what those imams brought — because those four imams وَالْمُؤْمَى عَلَيْهِمْ أَرْضَعَهَا وَقَفُوا غَيْرِهِمْ جَمِعُوا مَنَعَهَا — it is مُؤْمَى عَلَيْهِمْ; those four imams are مُؤْمَى عَلَيْهِمْ, meaning it is an absolute consensus that they are rightly guided. So those who call to something other than that are on shaky ground. In matters of aqeedah, take a simple, sound creed from a straightforward source — Ibn Ashar, Imam al-Tahawi, any of those — and do not wade into فروع, فرعيات: where is Allah, what is the hand like, all of that. Someone came to me and asked what I say about the verse الرَّحْمَٰنُ عَلَى الْعَرْشِ اسْتَوَىٰ, and I said that the very fact they are asking that question is, by the testimony of the Quran itself, a sign of deviation in the heart — because the Quran states that anyone who pursues those verses has deviation in their heart. So whenever you hear people arguing over the verses of Allah that belong to the متشابهات, the ambiguous ones, just leave them alone. That is guidance.

<!-- page 209 -->

The question had been: what do we do in a time when shuyukh have disappeared and people of tarbiyah are scarce? What Shaykh Hafri — may Allah bless him — said was that the very asking of that question is a sign that the person cares about their soul, so it is a good question, and it reflects a real condition. He said that if you are in this country, then things like the classes you are attending now are good — this is the right kind of thing to do. He said to make sure the source of your knowledge is sound. The things we teach, like these mutoon — these are mutoon that are thabit, rooted in our tradition, and they come through a chain of shuyukh. His shaykh and my shaykh is Murabit al-Hajj, and whoever has seen Murabit al-Hajj knows who he is. His knowledge is pure. The source of the knowledge is pure. No one who has lived with Murabit al-Hajj for over seventy years can say they ever saw him do something even makruh. He has never been heard to speak an ill word about anyone by name. His knowledge is pure Shariah, and he teaches people the remembrance of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala, because he is in a state that one of the people described this way: al-julusu 'indahu kal-julusu 'inda habar al-ghayb — sitting with him is like sitting with someone who is simultaneously present and absent. He is present in that he will teach you and answer your questions; he is absent in that his heart has moved away from this world — it is no longer in this world.

<!-- page 210 -->

People like that are rare to find. There is a question here — Alhamdulillah, he is Murabit; we love him. A woman asked: is it possible for a woman to come and learn from you, to study with you? He said that anyone who wants to go to Mauritania may go. He said the hidden aspiration of a mu'min is aqta'u min al-sayf — more cutting than the sword. If the hidden intention is strong, people can do remarkable things. He gave the example of Yahya, an American man who became Muslim here. He was young — nineteen or twenty — when he took his shahada. He was, as the shaykh put it, a spoiled American, from a wealthy family, with older brothers. He became Muslim and wanted to seek knowledge. He had an expense-paid place at Berkeley, had already completed two years of college. He came to me, and I told him initially to just finish university for his parents' sake, and then go.

My sister Nabila — and I told Shaykh Haddameen this — said to me: you did him an injustice. I said: why? It is only two years; it will pass quickly, and then he can go do what he wants. She said: how do you know what will happen to him in those two years? Maybe he will encounter a fitna at Berkeley. Maybe he will lose the desire for knowledge right now, while the fuel is still burning in his heart. When she said that, I realized she was right. I went to him and said: that advice I gave you — I was wrong. He said: I am really glad you said that, because I was not going to take it anyway. That is what he told me.

<!-- page 211 -->

When I told Shaykh Haddameen — who is the cousin of Shaykh Khatri and the cousin of Murabit al-Hajj, having learned all his knowledge from him — when I told him what I had said, and what my sister had said, he replied: Subhanallah, I had wanted to tell you that you were wrong, but my respect for you held me back. I am glad your sister said it. And he said he loves her for the good counsel she gave. So Shaykh Haddameen said that Yahya — who is the son of Murabit al-Hajj — now lives in a small hut made of tree branches with rags tied together. He said: learn for one year and you can learn four times over. It is not very much. And once you have learned those four times, go wherever you want, do whatever you want. Once you have your four times, practice it in your home, do your work, live your life. But if you have only four times, do not squander your time on other things, because it is all تجويد تجويد تجويد تجويد. Hadith should be transmitted with tajweed, and when you recite the adhkar of the morning and evening, you should observe the proper makhaarij al-huruf — where each letter originates — along with all the rules that apply to them.

<!-- page 212 -->

The best of ibadah is tafakkur, and the highest station of tafakkur is what is called al-fana'. Al-fana' comes from Imam al-Junaid — Imam al-Junaid called it al-fana', which in Arabic literally means extinction. This concept arises from the fact that each of us experiences a sense of separation from other people. One of the things you notice about a newborn is that this sense of separation does not yet exist in them. There is no "I," no fixed focal point of self. You can see it in the unfocused way their eyes move — there is no centralization. As the child grows into its identity, into its sense of "I-ness," it begins to perceive itself as separate from everything else.

Imam al-Junaid said that in deep spiritual practice, certain experiences can arise. One type is called hal, which occurs when someone is overcome by a spiritual state that is often beyond their control — it simply is uncontrollable. The scholars of this science distinguish between hal and maqam, and maqam is actually higher than hal. The maqam is where you are fixed in a condition. The maqam of tawbah, for instance, is where you cannot commit disobedience, because you are established in the station of tawbah — you are in a maqam, and so you cannot knowingly do wrong. The hal of tawbah, by contrast, is when someone who has been doing wrong actions is suddenly seized by an intense state of remorse and turns to Allah for forgiveness. That is a hal, not a maqam. The maqam is better because it endures. The maqam of zuhd is when someone is settled in a station of detachment; the hal of zuhd is a state that comes upon them and does not last. A person may experience a hal of zuhd or a hal of tawbah, but it passes. So the maqam is above the hal.

<!-- page 213 -->

Then there is what is called awarid — an inrush that enters the heart, very powerful, flooding it with light and bringing with it an extraordinary expansion. That too is a temporary state. What Imam al-Junaid is saying is that the highest is al-fana', and beyond fana' there is something called baqa'. Al-fana' is when the self is no longer aware of itself — there is a ghayba, a disappearance, a loss of the experience of the self. This does not last permanently, because if it did, you would call it insanity. And this is precisely why spiritual psychosis is real. There are people who go into experiences they cannot control or understand, and they become ill. It happens regularly in this culture — many people who take up meditation, who go into Zen Buddhism or similar paths, and practice intensively, can literally lose themselves completely. This is why it is so dangerous to pursue these things without the protection of prayer, without wudu, without the grounding that Islam provides. Islam is all about grounding. We are not chasing spiritual experiences — we are meant to be grounded. That is what Allah has commanded. But if you do what Allah commands, and you do it properly, then certain things will often happen inwardly, in the interior self. And there is a science for understanding those things — part of which is the science of al-khawater, knowing whether what arises comes from Shaytan, because Shaytan has many games he can play on those pursuing this path, or whether it is angelic, or whether it comes from the nafs.

<!-- page 214 -->

This is one of the problems with New Age practitioners: most of what happens to them — and they do have genuine experiences, people who go into New Age religion, or Zen, or Kundalini with its fire yoga and breathing practices — they cannot distinguish between the satanic, the nafsanic, the angelic, and the rahmanic. They have no tools to tell them apart. And most of what is happening to them is from Shaytan and the nafs. Possession? Yes, absolutely — possession is very real. People open themselves up to a jinn to enter them. That is absolutely the case.
