As for group dhikr — I will address that in a moment. So this idea, what al-fana' is, is simply that the person engaged in dhikr is no longer aware of their own self. And that can genuinely happen. Imam al-Junaid says this is the highest form of tafakkur. This is why when Sayyidina Ali's son — not al-Hasan or al-Husayn, but one of his other sons — was struck by an arrow, those who wanted to remove it waited until he entered prayer. When he went into prayer, they pulled it out, and he felt nothing. That is someone truly in that state where the self is absent — completely absent.

There are people in India, yogis, who can do very radical things through meditation — endure extraordinary pain and the like. So these experiences are not exclusive to Muslims in terms of the underlying psychology. The human psyche has remarkable capacities. What Muslims recognize, however, is that if a person is sincere and is genuinely remembering Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala, then Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala can bring that person into a state of absolute witnessing with Allah, where they are no longer aware of their surroundings. Many of the Sahaba were in that state — clearly, many of them were.

<!-- page 215 -->

This is one of the interpretations of the hadith: "My slave does not draw near to Me by anything more beloved to Me than the fara'id, and he will continue to draw near to Me through the nawafil until I love him. And when I love him, I become the eye with which he sees, the ear with which he hears, the tongue with which he speaks, the hand with which he grasps. And if he seeks refuge in Me, I give him refuge. If he asks Me, I give him." That hadith never means that God becomes man — which is called hulul. Hulul is haram to believe, and whoever believes it is outside of Islam. Hulul is the doctrine of divine incarnation, and that hadith does not mean that. Anyone who interprets it that way is a zindiq — a heretic, outside of Islam. We do not believe in divine incarnation. We do not believe that God becomes creation. That is the position of Christianity, Hinduism, and similar traditions, not ours.

What we do believe is that a human being can reach such a state of awareness of the fi'al of Allah — the acts of Allah — that it transforms their perception entirely. The whole world is the fi'al of Allah; we do not say the world is Allah, but we say the world is His act. And there are people who enter a state of absolute witnessing in which they see everything as the fi'al of Allah — they see Allah behind all of creation. They no longer perceive otherness the way other people do in this world of separation. They recognize that everything comes from Allah — the good and the bad — and so they see it all as good. For the moment, everything is good. That is what that experience means. Even though good and bad exist, it all comes from Allah, and because it all comes from Allah, it is all, for the moment, good — because even the bad holds wisdom: it causes growth, purification, and drawing closer to Allah. So even the bad is seen as good. This is why Allah says:

وَعَسَىٰ أَن تُحِبُّوا شَيْئًا وَهُوَ شَرٌّ لَّكُمْ

Maybe you love a thing and it is bad for you.

وَعَسَىٰ أَن تَكْرَهُوا شَيْئًا وَهُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ

And maybe you hate a thing, but it is good for you. And Allah knows, and you do not know. That is the reality of being in a state of مُشَاهَدَة or مُرَاقَبَة with Allah.

<!-- page 216 -->

Now, what you need to do is go through the sheet in front of you. You will find many different versions of these, which are called أَذْكَارُ الصَّبَاحِ وَالْمَسَاءِ — the morning and evening remembrances. My recommendation, for myself and for all of you, is to do these every day to the best of your ability. If you cannot do all of them, there are certain ones you should absolutely not miss, and I will point those out. Now this particular collection does not have a specific narration from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ specifying that he did this, then this, then this in sequence. So whenever you see these compiled collections, the formulations will vary. Sidi Ahmad Zarruq has one — سَفِينَةُ النَّجَاءِ مِنَ الِالْتِجَاءِ إِلَى اللَّهِ — which is a very fine one, drawn entirely from the Book and the Sunnah.

One of the things Sidi Ahmad Zarruq says is that you should observe the awrad that came down from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ before turning to any du'as written by other people. It is permissible to read du'as composed by others, provided those people knew what they were saying — meaning they were known to be true scholars and people of Allah. The Sahaba had du'as, the Tabi'un had du'as, and these have come down to us. There are salawat that come from other than the Prophet as well. But what Sidi Ahmad Zarruq said is this: if you are neglecting the du'as and adhkar that the Prophet told us to observe every day and preferring other people's compositions over them, then something is wrong. That is his point.


And Qadi Abu Bakr Ibn Arabi said, at the end of his book *Qanun At-Tawil*, that Shaytan's primary task with any Muslim is to pull them away from the remembrance of Allah altogether. But if he finds them persisting in much remembrance, he will then try to steer them away from the remembrance drawn from the Book and the Sunnah, toward remembrance composed by other people.

That is Shaytan's strategy. And so the beautiful thing here — and this is the world of Shaykh Abdullah Al-Haddad, who is from the noble lineage, one of the Shurafa of Yemen — is that he is considered a Mujaddid of the twelfth century as well. His grandson was one of my teachers, Shaykh Ahmad Mashhood Al-Haddad, who wrote *Muftah Al-Jannah*, which has been translated into English. I received this from him, and he took it from his father, who took it from his grandfather, who took it from the author himself.

He begins it with Bismillah Ar-Rahman Ar-Rahim. This follows the hadith that anything worth doing, if it does not begin with Bismillah Ar-Rahman Ar-Rahim, is cut off from barakah. So anything that is truly worth doing should be done in the name of Allah, in the name of compassion.

He then begins with Qul Huwa Allahu Ahad, recited three times. This comes from a hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ said: *Ayyajuzu Ahadukum Al-Yakhtamul Qur'ana Kullayoun* — is one of you incapable of reading the Qur'an once every day? The Companions asked, "Who could do that, Ya Rasulullah?" He replied that Surah al-Ikhlas — *Ikhlasu Adilu Firath Al-Qur'an* — is equivalent to one third of the Qur'an in its weight and meaning. By reading it three times, we hope for the reward of having read the entire Qur'an. It should therefore be read every day — ideally after every prayer, and at the very least in the morning and evening.

The next two are called the Mu'awwilatain. *Mu'awwil* means that which gives you refuge — from *ta'weeda*, meaning protection. So the Mu'awwilatain are *Qura'udu Bi Rabbil Faraq* and *Qura'udu Bi Rabbil Naq*, and these should be read every day. There is a hadith indicating they should be recited before sleep, after which you cover yourself — a protection from the jinn and the shayateen.

He then prescribes reciting, three times, the supplication of seeking refuge in Allah from the hamazat of Shaytan. *Hamazat* means to goad someone on — "come on, do it, do it." The hamazat of the shayateen are those whisperings within the soul urging you toward what you should not do. So in this supplication you seek refuge in Allah سبحانه وتعالى from the hamazat of Shaytan.

One of the signs of the end of time is *taqthirul hawsat* — that people's minds will be overwhelmed by constant whispering, until that is all they hear. When you consider this now, it is striking: so many people have music, films, and television running constantly around them, and all of that is from Shaytan — all of it, without exception. By exposing yourself to it, you are allowing Shaytan not merely to whisper but to send visual images streaming into you as well. All of it goes into the heart. In doing so, you are slowly destroying your heart.

People walk around carrying all these images that surface in their minds — songs, jingles, advertising music — and everywhere you go now, in stores and shopping centers, it is all there, constantly bombarding people. This supplication is a shield against the hamazat of Shaytan. The nafs carries its own impulses as well.

Recited three times:

رَبِّ أَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنْ هَمَزَاتِ الشَّيَاطِينِ وَأَعُوذُ بِكَ رَبِّهُمْ يَحْضَرُونَ

*"My Lord, I seek refuge in You from the insinuations of the shayateen, and I seek refuge in You, my Lord, lest they be present."*

You say it all together, three times separately — recite it once, then return to the beginning and recite it again.

As for sports: most sports involve the exposure of 'awrah. People's flesh that one should not be looking at is on display — shorts that reveal the thighs, and so on. Allah said: لَكُمْ وَلِيَ دِينٍ — and Allah says, do not keep company with those who take their lives as لَعِبْ وَلَهُ, play and pastime. In fact, they call baseball in this country the national pastime. اللَّهُ الْدَيْنِ — اللَّهُ الْدَيْنِ ها الوَطَنِ — the national pastime.

Now, if you want to go out and actually play some sport, at least you get some physical exercise from it. But to sit and watch — I will tell you honestly: when I was growing up, baseball was my life through high school. It was all I cared about. And I simply cannot watch a game now. You could not pay me to watch a game, and I am completely serious. I loved baseball. Football too. You could not pay me. I was once at someone's house and they were watching the Iranian-American soccer match, and I did unfortunately indulge a little — but it was at someone else's house; I would not do that on my own. I would not waste my time that way. Those things are simply a waste of your life.

You need to ask yourself: how much is an hour of your life worth? How much are two hours worth? People are selling their kidneys now. If someone could buy a year of your life from you, what would you sell it for? Once you begin to realize that you will be accountable not only for your life but for every minute of it — and that life itself, along with health, is the greatest gift Allah has given you after iman — you start to ask: what are you doing with what Allah has provided you?

We were created to use our time well. That does not mean you cannot enjoy yourself — there are *mudahat* things, permissible forms of enjoyment. That is precisely why the Prophet ﷺ, when attending a wedding, asked whether they had brought some entertainment. They said yes. He said that the Ansar love entertainment, and he wanted to show the Jews and the Christians that there is *fusha* in our religion — that we are not joyless, not prudish, not people who refuse to enjoy themselves or mark occasions. But what is happening now — this entire society has become one vast entertainment machine. People work solely in order to be entertained; they earn money so they can go out and spend it on entertainment. We all need to disengage from that. Wean ourselves away from this society. Just wean yourself — cut the cord. Stop nursing from the breast of dunya and the breast of lahut.

And it is pleasant, no doubt about it — it is pleasant. You sit back and watch a good film for a couple of hours. And then what? There is always an empty feeling afterward, the sense that you have just wasted two hours of your life. Everyone knows this feeling at the end — you watch it, and then what? Why would you do that? It is all a trick. They just trick you into it.

So: أَفَحَسِبْتُمُ — or as it appears in the next passage — أَفَحَسِبْتُمُ أَنَّمَا خَرَقْنَاكُمْ عَذَثًا — do you think We created you عَذَثًا, just for nothing? Did We create you in vain?

أَفَحَسِبْتُمُ أَنَّمَا خَرَقْنَاكُمْ عَذَثًا وَأَنَّكُمْ إِلَيْنَا لَا تُرْفِعُونَ

And you will not be returned to Us? You are not going to come back to Us? Exalted is Allah, the King, the Truth. There is no god but He. رَبِّ الْعَشِدْ كَرِيمِ — the Lord of the Noble Throne.

وَمَنْ يُدْعُو مَعَ اللَّهِ وِلَهِ الْآخَرَةِ لَا تُرْهَمَ لَهُ بِهِ فَإِنَّمَا حِسَبُوا عَنْدَ رَبِّهِمْ وَهُمَا يَتْرَحُوا الْكَفِرُونَ

This is a verse of the Qur'an that the Prophet ﷺ would recite every day.


<!-- page N --> markers must be preserved — however, I note that the INPUT content provided contains NO `<!-- page N -->` HTML comments (other than the context-overlap block which I must not re-emit). I will preserve that absence faithfully and output only the refined prose.

وَقِرَّبَكُ فِي الْوَرَحَمُ وَإِنتَ خَيْرُ الرَّاحِمِينَ Then comes another verse — a supplication: "O Allah, forgive, have compassion, for You are the best of those who show mercy." And then yet another verse the Prophet would recite every day: فَسُبْحَانَ اللَّهِ حِنَ تُمْسُونَ — "Glory be to Allah when you enter the evening" — وَحِنَ تُسْبِحُونَ — "and when you enter the morning." This is the foundation of the adhkar said at those two times — مساء والصباح — morning and evening are the times to say subhanallah. وَلَوْا الْحَمْدُ فِي السَّمَوَاتِ وِالْأَرْضِ وَعَشِيًّا وَحِنَ تَظْهِرُونَ — "And His is all praise in the heavens and the earth, in the evening and at midday." Scholars have also cited this verse as a proof for the five prayers: حِنَ تُمْسُونَ corresponds to Asr, حِنَ تُسْبِحُونَ to Fajr, عَشِيًّا to Maghrib and Isha, and وَحِنَ تَظْهِرُونَ to Dhuhr.

As for رَبِّ الْخَيْرُ الرَّحْمِينَ — "Forgive, show mercy, for You are the best of those who show mercy" — is this for oneself alone or for all Muslims? In principle it is general, but when you are making this supplication the اغفر لي is specifically for yourself.

Then comes: يَخْرِجْ الْحَيَّ مَنَ الْمَيْتِينَ وَيَخْرِجْ الْمَيْتِ مَنَ الْحَيِّ — "He brings the living from the dead and the dead from the living." The mufassirun mention several meanings here: one is the literal example of a chick emerging from an egg, or the human being arising from a dead, inanimate source. But there is also a spiritual meaning — Allah brings iman out of kufr, and kufr can extinguish iman. Dead hearts come alive through faith, and living hearts can die by falling into disbelief. The Prophet also said that Allah brings the earth back to life after its death, and that in the same way He will raise us up — and this too points to the heart's capacity for revival. In the Quran, the word ard is used مَجَازٍ بِالْقَلْبِ as a traditional tafsir — so Allah revives the heart through His remembrance after it has died through heedlessness and forgetfulness of Him.

The Prophet also used to recite this ta'weezah: سَمِيرْ عَلِيمٍ عَلِيمُ بِاللَّهِ سَمِيرْ عَلِيمٍ مِنَ الشَّيْطَانِ الرَّجِيمِ — three times. That was among his ta'weezat.

Then there is the verse that the Prophet would recite in both the morning and the evening: لَوْ أَنزَلْنَا هَذَا الْقُرْآنَ عَلَى جَبْلٍ بَرَأَيْتُهُ خَاشِعًا مِن تَصَدِّعَ مِنْ خَشَّةٍ دَاءٍ — "Had We revealed this Quran to a mountain, you would have seen it humbled, torn apart by the khashya of Allah." Khashya is fear grounded in knowledge — it is different from khawf. A rat can experience khawf, but a rat cannot have khashya. That is why the scholars are described as those who have khashya of Allah, and why the Messenger of Allah said اَنَا اَعْلَمُكُمْ دُنْيَا — "I am the most knowledgeable of you concerning Allah" — and therefore اَخْشَاكُمْ — the most filled with khashya. What Allah is conveying here is that the Quran carries such a weight of knowledge that even a mountain — an inanimate object — would be shattered and humbled by khashya if the Quran were revealed to it, because of the meanings and knowledge contained within it. This is precisely why تَبَدَّرَ الْقُرَانِ — deep reflection on the Quran — leads to khushu', a humbled and awed awareness of Allah.

Allah then says that these are similitudes He strikes for humanity so that they might reflect. Does this mean the mountain has knowledge? Some scholars say that the mountain here is a symbol of a scholar, because in Arabic a great scholar is compared to a mountain — هو جبل من الجبال — "he is a mountain among mountains." They would say of someone like Imam al-Shafi'i: امام شافع كان جبلا من الجبال. So the meaning is that if this Quran were revealed to anyone of immense knowledge and understanding, it would shatter them. Yet Allah also tells us in the Quran that He offered the trust to the heavens, the earth, and the mountains. Scholars note that Allah begins with the heavens because they are His most awe-inspiring creation in terms of sheer magnitude — the Messenger of Allah ﷺ is the greatest in terms of maqam, but in terms of awesomeness the heavens surpass everything, and only the astronomer can begin to grasp that. Then comes the earth, then the mountains. And that refusal, scholars say, was not a refusal of iman.

Allah then says He is Allah — there is no doubt in it — the Knower of the seen and the unseen. Shahada means "the seen," but it also carries the meaning of testimony, because everything visible is testifying to la ilaha illallah. This is why the Prophet said: how extraordinary that a man could disobey Allah, how strange that anyone could disbelieve in Him — و في كل تحريكة و في كل تحريكة و تسكينة — in every movement and every stillness there is always a shahid, a witness. Everything in creation is testifying that Allah is One. So the shahada — this visible world — is, in its very meaning, saying la ilaha illallah. That is the message the entire world is sending to the human being, and the human being is the one capable of decoding it.

This is why Allah describes Himself as the King, the Holy, the Sanctified, the Complete. Salam here does not mean peace in the ordinary sense — it is wrong to render Allah's name as simply "peace" in that casual way. Al-Mu'min is the one who makes others safe, who gives amin. Al-Muhaymin is the one who oversees everything and holds all things in His control. Al-'Aziz is the Great, the Powerful. Al-Jabbar is the Compeller — the One who compels all things and all beings to do what they do. Al-Mutakabbir is the Exalted, the Magnificent, the Supremely Great. And subhanallah — "Glory be to Allah above all that they associate with Him." In Arabic, subhan means tamzeeh: when you say subhanallah you are declaring that Allah is utterly unlike anything. You are asserting His incomparability.

As for al-Khaliq: I prefer not to use certain terms because of their complexity in tawhid, but al-Khaliq, al-Bari', and al-Musawwir represent three aspects of the creative process — khalq, bar', and taswir. Al-Khaliq is when Allah decides to create: He possesses the qudra, the 'ilm, and the irada, and when these three converge, creation comes into being. In Allah's ultimate reality this capacity is always present — His power to be al-Khaliq is eternally there. When He decides to create, He is al-Bari' — the One who actually brings it into existence. Creation is called bari'ah, which is why the Prophet ﷺ is called khair al-bari'ah — the finest of all that the Bari' has brought into being. Al-Musawwir is the Fashioner: He shapes and gives form. In the context of a fetus, al-Khaliq is the One who determined there would be a human life; al-Bari' is the One who brought about the seed in the man and the ovum in the woman and united them; and al-Musawwir is the One who fashions the form — the cells differentiate, the liver becomes a liver, the brain becomes a brain, the spine takes its shape. That is al-Musawwir.

In reality, however, there is no linearity in Allah. Imam al-Ghazali says these are only facets — and because we are limited, we can only perceive one aspect at a time. Allah is describing for us what is in reality a multi-faceted process, but it is only multi-faceted from our limited vantage point. In truth it is all one; it is all Allah. Attributes are approximations for human understanding, because we exist in time and our intellects are bounded. These are simply different facets of a single creative reality. Is that clear?

لَهُ الْأَسْمَاءُ الْحُسْنَى — Allah has the most beautiful names. يُسَبِّحُ لَهُ مَا فِي السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ — everything in the heavens and the earth glorifies Him, and beyond that, without exception, everything — rocks, atoms, all things — is praising Allah. وَهُوَ الْعَزِيزُ الْحَكِيمُ — and He is al-'Aziz, al-Hakim.

Then: سَلَامٌ عَلَى نُوحٍ فِي الْعَالَمِينَ — إِنَّا كَذَلِكَ نَجْزِي الْمُحْسِنِينَ — إِنَّهُ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا الْمُؤْمِنِينَ. Whether this verse was included based on a hadith or on the compiler's own ijtihad is unclear. But one of its wisdoms, I think, is this: in a certain sense we are living in an age of Nuh. A great flood is coming — and all of us know it. When Nuh alayhis-salam was building his ark, people laughed at him. Building an ark in this age — preparing for the akhira — invites the same ridicule. People think you are a fool. If you are genuinely concerned about the akhira, people assume something is wrong with you. Even Muslims — they would not say outright that they do not believe in the akhira, because they do not want to make that admission, but all of their actions tell you otherwise. Everything they care about is dunya. And when they see Muslims who are focused on the akhira, they say: "Ya akhi, why don't you do something for yourself? For your dunya? دُنْيَاكَ كَأَنَّكَ تَعِيشُ أَبَدًا — live for your world as if you will live forever."

Next comes: أَللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أَصْبَحْتُ مِنْكَ بِنِعْمَةٍ وَعَافِيَةٍ — "O Allah, I have entered this morning in Your blessing, with good health and with my wrongs veiled by You — فَأَتِمَّ نِعْمَتَكَ عَلَيَّ وَعَافِيَتَكَ وَسِتْرَكَ فِي الدُّنْيَا وَالْآخِرَةِ — so complete Your blessing upon me, Your 'afiyah, and Your veiling, in this world and the next." This is said three times. (There are two riwayat — fa-atimna or atmim — but both are transmitted.)

The next supplication: the Prophet ﷺ said that whoever recites it four times receives a reward equivalent to that of freeing a human being from bondage. It goes: أَللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أُشْهِدُكَ — "O Allah, I enter this morning calling You to bear witness" — وَأُشْهِدُ حَمَلَةَ عَرْشِكَ — "and calling the angels who carry Your throne to bear witness" — وَمَلَائِكَتَكَ وَجَمِيعَ خَلْقِكَ — "and all of Your angels and all of Your creation to bear witness" — أَنَّكَ أَنتَ اللَّهُ لَا إِلَهَ إِلَّا أَنتَ وَحْدَكَ لَا شَرِيكَ لَكَ وَأَنَّ مُحَمَّدًا عَبْدُكَ وَرَسُولُكَ — "that You are Allah, there is no god but You, alone, without partner, and that Muhammad ﷺ is Your servant and Your messenger." This is said four times.

Then: اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ حَمْدًا يُوَافِي نِعَمَهُ وَيُكَافِئُ مَزِيدَهُ — "All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all the worlds, a praise commensurate with His blessings and reciprocating all the increase He bestows." This hadith encompasses the complete fulfillment of hamd and shukr for all the ni'am of Allah. When we asked Habibullah al-Hajj for a wasiya — a piece of counsel — to pass on to people, this is the supplication he gave. He would say it frequently: اَلْحَمْدُ لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ حَمْدًا يُوَافِي نِعَمَهُ وَيُكَافِئُ مَزِيدَهُ.

Then comes a supplication the Prophet ﷺ said removes all traces of shirk: "I believe in Allah the Magnificent, and I reject everything magical and every object worshipped alongside Allah. I have taken hold of the firmest handle" — which is the Quran, the Sunnah, and the way of Islam — "and this handle will never break as long as you hold onto it. There is no separation, and Allah is..." This is a protective supplication.

The following supplication: the Prophet ﷺ said that whoever recites it three times receives the ridwan of Allah, because you are saying: رَضِيتُ بِاللَّهِ رَبًّا — "I am content with Allah as my Lord" — وَبِالْإِسْلَامِ دِينًا — "and with Islam as my deen" — وَبِمُحَمَّدٍ — or, in some recitations, بِسَيِّدِنَا — نَبِيًّا وَرَسُولًا — "and with Muhammad ﷺ as my Prophet and Messenger." As for adding Sayyidina: it is not found in the transmitted wording, but adding it out of ta'dub — out of reverence — is permissible, and this is called mukharafah ta'duban, as has been discussed. Just as Sayyidina 'Ali refused to remove the words "Muhammad Rasulullah" from the treaty of Hudaybiyyah, one may add Sayyidina out of the same spirit of reverence. Both forms exist, but the actual wording transmitted in the warid is بِمُحَمَّدٍ صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ نَبِيًّا وَرَسُولًا.

Then the next supplication: the Prophet ﷺ said to recite it seven times in the morning and seven times in the evening — حَسْبِيَ اللَّهُ لَا إِلَهَ إِلَّا هُوَ عَلَيْهِ تَوَكَّلْتُ وَهُوَ رَبُّ الْعَرْشِ الْعَظِيمِ — "Allah is enough for me; there is no god but Him. I have placed my trust in Him, and He is the Lord of the magnificent throne." Seven times.

Then: أَللَّهُمَّ صَلِّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَآلِهِ وَسَلِّمْ — a concise prayer upon the Prophet ﷺ. We know from hadith that Jibril brought the Prophet ﷺ the glad tidings that whoever sends one prayer upon him receives ten times the rahma of Allah in return. So the instruction here is to do it ten times — which equals a hundred instances of Allah's rahma upon you. It is preferable to send prayers upon the Prophet at least a hundred times in the morning and a hundred times in the evening. Shaykh Muhammad, for an extended period of his life, would do it five thousand times a day as a spiritual practice. For fourteen years he maintained a practice of supplication twice daily, a khatm of the Quran once daily, and one hundred and fourteen thousand recitations of بِسْمِ اللَّهِ الرَّحْمَنِ الرَّحِيمِ every single day. Those who were present said that when he emerged from his khalwa, people would make tawba simply by seeing him. He lived in Fes at a madrasa, and he had a room there — a room he occupied so long and so deeply that he memorized one of the most demanding books of Maliki fiqh, a multi-volume work, entirely by heart. He was a great Maliki scholar and a faqih. But people talked — they wondered why he never left that room. They assumed he must be hiding treasure up there, some gold he did not want anyone to find. So they went to his father to persuade him to go up, because he would not allow anyone to enter, and there was a ladder to reach it. His father came up, and he let him in — and when his father entered, he found the word مَوْت — "death" — written across every wall. There was nothing else in the room. His father understood: he realized that some people are in a world entirely their own, and others simply cannot comprehend it. That man was in a completely different realm — Allahu Akbar.

As for the form of the prayer on the Prophet: if you say صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ then the verb is sallama with a fatha. But if you say أَللَّهُمَّ صَلِّ عَلَى سَيِّدِنَا مُحَمَّدٍ وَسَلِّمْ — like that — then سَلِّمْ carries a kasra, because this is the sighatu al-du'a, the form of supplication. Even though it uses what looks like a past-tense verb, it is actually a supplication — like when you say رَحِمَهُ اللَّهُ, you are saying "may Allah have mercy on him," but you use the past tense تَفَاؤُلًا — as if it has already been accepted. Similarly, رَضِيَ اللَّهُ عَنْهُمْ is said of the Sahaba in the past tense, though you could equally say أَللَّهُمَّ ارْضَ عَنْ أَبِي بَكْرٍ وَعُمَرَ وَعُثْمَانَ وَعَلِيٍّ — using the direct form of du'a. So with the direct form — the sighatu al-du'a or sighatu al-amr — you would say وَسَلِّمْ with a kasra. There are various forms one may use; this particular one is shorter and so lends itself to higher volume of repetition. I believe this is the sighah that the Sheikh used.

After that comes a supplication in which the Prophet asked Allah for good surprises and sought refuge from bad ones — fujaa' being something that happens suddenly and unexpectedly. So you are asking Allah to let only good surprises reach you in your day, and to shield you from the harmful ones.

Now we come to sayyid al-istighfar. The Prophet ﷺ said — and it is in the sahih hadith — that this is the sayyid al-istighfar, the foremost of all ways of seeking forgiveness, the best form in which a person can ask for it. You say: "O Allah, You are my Lord; there is no god but You. You created me and I am Your servant. I am upon Your ahd — the covenant of Islam — and Your promise to forgive me and admit me into Jannah, to the best of my ability." In other words: as best I am able, I am doing what You have commanded me to do. "I seek refuge in You from the evil I have done. I return to You, acknowledging that all my blessings are from You — I am returning them to You, admitting they are Yours entirely. And I take full responsibility for my wrong actions: they are mine, I committed them, I admit them. So forgive me, for none can forgive wrong actions but You." That is this supplication.

Then: "Whatever Allah willed came to be, and whatever He did not will did not come to be" — this is sometimes said in times of misiba, calamity, as an acknowledgement and surrender. "And I know that Allah is powerful over all things, and that Allah encompasses all things in His knowledge."

"I seek refuge from the evil of my own soul and from the evil of every creature that You hold by its forelock." This is a reminder of tawhid: every creature is in Allah's grip. Their evil reaches you only because Allah — who holds them by the forelock — has permitted it. So when you seek refuge in Allah from the evil of others, Allah will protect you from that evil. There are many hadith that speak to this, and it is a powerful supplication. There is also a hadith of reciting it forty-one times before Fajr. These two names invoked in it are very powerful names.

"I am seeking through Your mercy, and I am seeking safety from Your punishment — rectify all of my affairs and do not leave me to my own soul, and do not leave me to anyone from Your creation, even for the blink of an eye. Be my sole reliance — do not make me dependent on anyone other than You." This does not mean you will never receive help from others — but you recognize that all help comes from Allah alone. That is the meaning: do not let me think the help is coming from other than You.

Jabir al-Sadiq, may Allah be pleased with him, was once challenged by an atheist who told him he did not believe in Allah. Jabir al-Sadiq said to him: "Have you ever been at sea?" The man said yes — and then told him what had happened: he had been on a boat when a violent storm struck, so severe that the vessel was torn to pieces. All the sailors drowned. He alone survived, clinging to a plank of wood. Then the ocean took even the plank from his hands, and he had nothing. Jabir al-Sadiq said: "What did you do?" He said: "The sea flung me to the shore and I survived." Jabir al-Sadiq said to him: "When you boarded that boat, you put your trust in the boat and in the sailors. When Allah took those away from you, you put your trust in that little piece of wood. And when you lost the wood — where did you put your trust then? Did you hope to survive?" He said: "Yes, I hoped that I would." Jabir al-Sadiq said: "Who did you hope in?" Because in Arabic, raja' is a transitive verb — رَجَوْتُ requires an object. You cannot hope in the abstract; hope must have an object. So he pressed him: "From whom were you hoping for safety?" Then he said: "يَا عَبْدَ اللَّهِ — O servant of Allah — the One who took away every means you had, and then saved you despite the absence of every means — that was Allah." That is لَا تَكِلْنِي إِلَى نَفْسِي وَلَا إِلَى أَحَدٍ مِنْ خَلْقِكَ طَرْفَةَ عَيْنٍ — "do not leave me to myself or to any of Your creation for even the blink of an eye." وَلَا أَقَلَّ مِنْ ذَلِكَ — not even less than that.

أَللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنَ الْهَمِّ وَالْحَزَنِ — Sheikh al-Khatri told me that someone once came to him without a job, and he gave him this supplication. Within a few days, that person had found work. It comes from a hadith in which a man came to the Prophet ﷺ and said: "Ya Rasulallah, I am crushed by debts and overwhelmed by hum and huzn." The Prophet gave him this supplication, and the man said he began reciting it and Allah resolved all his debts and lifted his grief. So: أَللَّهُمَّ إِنِّي أَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنَ الْهَمِّ وَالْحَزَنِ — "O Allah, I seek refuge in You from hum and huzn" — وَأَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنَ الْعَجْزِ وَالْكَسَلِ — "and I seek refuge in You from 'ajz and kasal." Hum is anxiety about the future — worry over what has not yet come. Huzn is grief over the past — sorrow over what has already gone. One concerns the future, the other the past. And Allah says in the Quran: أَلَا إِنَّ أَوْلِيَاءَ اللَّهِ لَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ — the friends of Allah have no fear about the future nor grief about the past. This is about entering the present moment, because there is no past or future for Allah. When you are in a state of awareness of Allah, the past holds no anxiety and the future holds no dread — you cannot change the past, and the future is entirely in Allah's hands. All you have is the present. So there is no point in carrying grief about the past or anxiety about the future.

'Ajz is the condition of someone who has the desire but lacks the ability — irada without qudra. Kasal is the opposite: having the ability but lacking the will — qudra without irada. Both are states to seek refuge from. You want to be someone who has both: the ability to do what needs to be done, and the will to do what should be done.

وَأَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنَ الْجُبْنِ وَالْبُخْلِ — "I seek refuge in You from jubun and bukhl." Jubun is cowardice — the Arabic word comes from the image of curdled cheese: a coward, when firmness is required, collapses. They lack thabat, steadiness. You are seeking refuge from the inability to give yourself with shaja'a when it is called for. Bukhl is miserliness — the cowardice of wealth. So jubun is the cowardice of the self and body; bukhl is the cowardice of wealth and property. And Allah says: إِنَّ اللَّهَ اشْتَرَى مِنَ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ أَنفُسَهُمْ وَأَمْوَالَهُم بِأَنَّ لَهُمُ الْجَنَّةَ — "Allah has purchased from the believers their selves and their wealth, and in exchange is paradise." What He purchased requires jihad with both بِأَمْوَالِهِمْ وَأَنفُسِهِمْ — their wealth and their selves. If you are a coward you cannot give yourself in jihad with your body. If you are a miser you cannot give in jihad with your wealth. So jubun and bukhl are the two things that prevent you from fulfilling what Allah has purchased from you — which is why seeking refuge from them is so important.

وَأَعُوذُ بِكَ مِنْ غَلَبَةِ الدَّيْنِ وَقَهْرِ الرِّجَالِ — "I seek refuge in You from overwhelming debt and from being overpowered by men." These two are paired deliberately. If you study history, you will find that one of the primary means by which the kufar gained control over the Muslims was through debt. They would lend Muslims money — on interest — and when the Muslims could not repay, the debt had compounded to a point where they could no longer meet their obligations. That is when the demands for concessions began. Debt is a mechanism of control. In this country, the entire economic system is designed to put people into debt. The tax laws are structured that way. The banking system was built that way — because those who hold your debt hold power over you. They make people into economic slaves. People will resist physical enslavement — they will rebel against it. But economic slavery is different: it comes with guilt. In German, the word for debt — Schuld — is the same word for guilt. When you are in debt, you feel you owe something; you feel diminished. And through that sense of obligation, people become enslaved. This is a deep wisdom in seeking refuge from debt and from being overpowered by others — because the two go together.

This is also why the scholars said: if there were anything better to ask for than 'afiyah — and 'afiyah in its full meaning encompasses Islam itself, as well as spiritual, physical, and economic well-being — the Prophet ﷺ would have told his uncle 'Abbas to ask for it. So asking Allah for 'afiyah is among the most comprehensive of supplications.

And this is why, when Sayyidina 'Umar went to Sham and found Sayyidina Mu'awiyah wearing a magnificent Shami robe, richly ornamented, 'Umar was troubled by it.


And Sayyidina Uthman said to Sayyidina Umar: these are people — if you come to them dressed the way we dress in the desert, they will think nothing of you. That was the wisdom behind it. Sayyidina Umar did remove his robe and put on a finer one, then mounted a barzoon — but then he dismounted, put his own robes back on, and refused. He simply said: Allah gave us dignity through our Islam, not through outward trappings. And if we seek honor in anything other than that, Allah will humiliate us.

<!-- page 302 -->

Remarkably, in Jerusalem, their own books had foretold that the man who would open the city would have — I believe — seventy-three patches on his robe. When they counted the patches on Sayyidina Umar's robe, the number matched what was written in their books. Seventy-two? Seventeen? I am not entirely certain of the exact figure.

Now, regarding the last point someone raised — about group dhikr: there is a scholarly disagreement on this. Some scholars considered it a blameworthy innovation — a bid'ah makruha — meaning not forbidden outright, but disapproved. What troubled them was the absence of a clear narration showing that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ or the Companions practiced it in that specific form. Imam al-Shafi'i, on the other hand, considered it permissible and saw nothing wrong with it. He drew on hadith indicating that there was a teacher with a group of learners, and that the Prophet ﷺ sat among those learning and said, "I was sent as a teacher" — though he did not rebuke the other group either. However, because of the weakness of that particular narration, it is difficult to derive a firm ruling from it alone.

There is, however, a completely sound hadith stating that Allah has traveling angels — mala'ika sayyara — who roam the earth searching for circles of remembrance, hilaqat al-dhikr. When they come upon one, they descend upon it. Then, when they return to Allah, He asks them — and this is to teach us; Allah, of course, knows — "How did you find My servants?" And they reply: "We found them saying..." The hadith indicates they were gathered in a halaqa.

<!-- page 303 -->

Some scholars derived from this that it is permissible to gather for dhikr, but not necessarily to recite in unison, since the hadith does not specify a single voice. Other scholars held that there is no prohibition on reciting in unison, since dhikr itself is permissible and no clear prohibition of that form exists. They noted that there are hadith showing the Companions did say certain things together — the takbir and the like — so collective vocalization is not without precedent.

These are khilafi matters. The majority of scholars, particularly the later ones, permitted group dhikr. The Muwahiddun actively encouraged it in Morocco, including the communal recitation of the Quran in a single voice. They established a practice in Morocco that continues to this day: completing the entire Quran every month in the mosques. The Muwahiddun initiated this because they observed that people had stopped reading the Quran — and by setting a collective recitation in the mosque, people would join in simply because it was there and it was a good thing to do. Their reasoning was that it was better for people to read and complete the Quran than not to read it at all.

Ibn al-Hajj disagreed with this in his Madkhal. On the question of poetry, he noted that the Prophet ﷺ loved to hear it — there are many sound hadith on this — and that he would listen and say, in effect, "Yes, let us hear more." The Arabs used to sing their poetry — the alhan al-arab — in their gatherings. So in the tradition of nasheeds, such as one finds in Palestine, Syria, and Morocco, there are sung poems in praise of Allah and of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. That is permissible, and it is in fact a wholesome form of

<!-- page 304 -->

entertainment — beautiful to listen to when done well, with good voices. People would be far better served listening to that than to music and the like.

As for a translation — I do not have one written down, but I just gave you one, more or less, so it is on tape. It would need to be polished, since I rendered it on the spot. But even without the meanings, these things carry benefit.

On the question of what Abu Hanifa said about dhikr — as far as I know, the Hanafi madhab accepts group dhikr. And historically, Muslims did practice it. If you study the early books, you will find that group dhikr was indeed practiced.

Murabit al-Hajj does not do it in that form — he is like Ibn al-Hajj in the madhab, cautious about anything that even carries a scent of bid'ah. But he does not make inkar of others, because he is a scholar and he knows the ruling. He himself simply exercises caution — that is all. And yet if you spend time with him, all he does is dhikr. Being in his company is itself a form of group dhikr, because everyone around him is in a state of remembrance. So, alhamdulillah.

The essential point is: remember Allah. Those who remember Allah standing, sitting, and reclining on their sides — that is the standard. And there is a hadith that Sheikh Khatri mentioned in which the Prophet ﷺ said: "Shall I not tell you what the best of your actions is, the purest in the sight of your Lord, the highest in your ranks, better for you than spending gold and silver, and better for you than that you should go out and strike the necks of the enemy and they strike yours?" They said: "Tell us, O Messenger of Allah." He said: "The remembrance of Allah — dhikr Allah."

<!-- page 305 -->

The scholars stipulate, of course, that if a city is under attack, jihad takes precedence over everything. So these matters must always be understood in context. If defenders are needed at the walls and a group instead sits saying "dhikr is better" — that is ignorance and foolishness. Everything has its context. But in its proper place, dhikr is the highest act. This is why the scholars have said that performing voluntary hajj — when the communal obligation of jihad is already being fulfilled — is superior to going out on voluntary jihad. The reason is fundamental: jihad is a means to an end, and an end always ranks above the means. We were not created to kill people. We were created to remember Allah. But if people are preventing us from remembering Allah, killing us for it, then we must go out and stop them — and that is the point.

So jihad holds the immense rank that it holds. Allah says in the Quran:

يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اسْتَجِيبُوا لِلَّهِ وَلِلرَّسُولِ إِذَا دَعَاكُمْ لِمَا يُحْيِيكُمْ

"O you who believe, answer Allah and His Messenger when they call you to what brings you to life." The scholars have said, nearly by consensus, that this ayah is interpreted as referring to jihad — for that is what brings this ummah back to life. So let no one belittle jihad or the station of the mujahid. The mujahid is honored in this ummah, and all of us should carry within ourselves a longing for shahada. The Prophet ﷺ said that whoever dies and never even entertained in his heart the thought "I wish I could go on jihad" dies a form of spiritual death — a nifaq.

<!-- page 306 -->

So jihad is immense. I am not belittling it in any way. But what people have forgotten is this: what will give Muslims the certainty — the yaqeen — they need when they go out to fight, and what will make them capable of fighting solely for Allah's sake, is dhikr of Allah. Because there are many Muslims today who want to go out and fight simply because they are angry. Truly. They are angry at the world, angry at the Americans, angry at one thing or another. And there is much in the world that is legitimately angering. But anger alone is not spirituality. It is not a purified state of the nafs. Dhikr is what rectifies the heart and elevates its motivation from the self to Allah. That is the work.

As for the Mawlid — that is another khilafi matter. Personally, I believe the scholars settled these questions centuries ago, and it is genuinely saddening that we are still debating them. The formal celebration of the Mawlid on a fixed date was not something the first community practiced in that organized form. But there is no doubt that they were celebrating him every single day of their lives. There is a sound hadith in which a man asked the Prophet ﷺ whether he could fast on Mondays, and the Prophet replied: "That is the day I was born" — indicating it was an excellent day to observe the fast. That is a sound hadith.

There is also a hadith in which the Prophet ﷺ mentioned that Abu Lahab — the bitter enemy of Islam — is given a small relief from punishment every Monday in Jahannam, amounting to

<!-- page 307 -->

water between his thumb and forefinger, because he freed a slave girl on the day the Prophet was born. In other words, even Abu Lahab's spontaneous joy at the Prophet's birth earned him a weekly reprieve from torment. So one should never say "we do not celebrate the Prophet's birthday." And I have heard people say that. There is a hadith that a man will utter a word without thinking, and it will carry him further than he imagines. You should never say that. If you wish to frame it in fiqh terms, you may say: "To celebrate his birthday on a specific fixed date is something the Salaf did not formalize." But to say "we do not celebrate his birthday" — no. Never. We celebrate his birth. We should be happy every day of our lives that he was born, because it is only through him that we came to know Allah, and it is only through his sacrifice that we have the deen today. We should celebrate every single day that he came into this world.

Holding a gathering on the twelfth of Rabi' al-Awwal is a later innovation, yes. Most scholars called it a bid'ah hasana — a praiseworthy innovation. Some scholars were against it; that is true. But the majority were not. Imam al-Suyuti wrote a fatwa saying it was a good thing and that people should do it. You can find that fatwa in his collected fatwas, al-Hawi lil-Fatawi.

<!-- page 308 -->

So if you choose not to observe it in that form, that is fine. But consider the person whose priorities are entirely disordered: he attends the Mawlid celebration once a year and does nothing the Prophet commanded for the rest of the year. That is truly shameful. If you want to celebrate the Prophet ﷺ, do what he told you to do — that is what will genuinely honor him. Coming together once a year for a celebration and then ignoring his guidance for the remaining three hundred and sixty-four days is a contradiction. What is genuinely good is to hear the seerah, and traditionally that is what these gatherings consisted of: reciting the account of his birth and his manaqib — the noble qualities and virtues of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. That is a sound and beneficial practice.

On the question of which date the Prophet was actually born — that is a separate dispute between Eastern and Western scholarship. The argument over the precise date is one of the classical objections raised against fixing the celebration to a single day. Time magazine even ran an article about this once, with a photograph of someone celebrating the Prophet's birthday as though it were literally a birthday party — candles and all.


There you go. We should pray Asr. Allahumma la ilaha illa ma a'allamtana innaka tala hakeem al-'aleem Allahumma iftah alayna hikmatak wa inshura alayna rahmatak ya zan al-jalali wa al-ikram Allahumma iftah alayna hikmatak wa inshura alayna rahmatak ya zan al-jalali wa al-ikram Allahumma iftah alayna hikmatak wa inshura alayna rahmatak ya zan al-jalali wa al-ikram wa salillahumma ala sayyidina Muhammad wa alihi wa sahbihi wa sallim taslimah wa la hawla wa la quwwata illa billahi al-'aliyyi al-'azim wa la hawla wa la quwwata illa billahi al-'aliyyi al-'azim wa la hawla wa la quwwata illa billahi al-'aliyyi al-'azim Bismillah ar-Rahman ar-Rahim Bismillah ar-Rahman ar-Rahim

Alhamdulillah. I think it is a blessing that we have come to the end of the Mudhaharāt, and for most of us I believe this is the first day of Ramadan — which is truly the month of Mudhaharah. It feels entirely fitting that we should conclude the study of the Mudhaharāt al-Qulub at the opening of this month, because Ramadan is the greatest practical school for implementing everything we have learned over the past twelve weeks about the heart. The purpose of Ramadan is to purify the heart with Allah subhana wa ta'ala — that is its purpose, that is its blessing, and that is its secret: it exists to purify the heart.

I want to say something about — I'm not sure how many of you were here last night — how many people saw the moon last night? How many went out to look for it? About the same number. For those who were here, I think we all had a truly wonderful experience. Were you here with us? Who was here? Wasn't that a remarkable experience?

I was also thinking this morning about yesterday and seeing the Hilal, and something struck me that I find to be a genuinely interesting perspective. When we saw the Hilal last night for the first time, my initial reaction was uncertainty — I didn't know whether it was really there. I actually wondered whether my eyes were playing tricks on me, because it seemed to come into existence and then vanish again, flickering in and out. I realized that when we first went out, there was nothing there but the light of the sun. We had to wait. Then Venus appeared, and then, after some time, we were finally able to see that small sliver.

What I came to understand is this: Allah says that they ask you about the new moons, and Allah directed the Messenger of Allah to tell them that these moons are simply markers of time. But the Mufassirun explain that the reason people asked about the Hilal was that they were trying to understand the mechanism — how does this new moon come into being? The reason for that question is that people in antiquity actually believed that every moon was literally a new moon: that it disappeared completely and then began again from nothing. They wanted to understand how that was happening.

What I realized is that Allah, in His response, did not negate their belief — He simply explained why the moon is there. It is their mawaqit, their appointed times: a way of marking time for people, for hajj, to determine the times of Ramadan and hajj. And one of the things that Qabiyyullah ibn Arabi said in his tafsir is that the secret of this ummah using a lunar calendar rather than a solar calendar is that the sun governs worldly benefits while the moon governs otherworldly benefits. So the orientation of time for this ummah is toward the next world, not toward this one.

What I also realized is this: if you look at it from a purely direct, non-abstract perspective, what you witness when you watch the new moon is what philosophers call emergence — and this is the real problem for those who deny the Creator. How do things come into existence out of nothing? Why is there something rather than nothing? That is their problem. And there you are, sitting, looking at the sky, and suddenly this thing emerges out of nothing right before your eyes — because you cannot see it initially. The reason you cannot see it is the sun: the sun's light is too strong. The reason you are then able to see it is that the sun's light has dimmed. And what emerges is reflected light. What you are seeing is reflected light — and really, the entire creation is reflected light. Allah is the source; the entire creation is reflected light from Allah. The only reason we can see it is that Allah allows it to exist. He is the one who brings it into existence and the one who takes it out of existence. What you are witnessing is this extraordinary event: Allah is letting you see reflected light come into being. That is the new moon. That is why it is called istihlal — the birth. Just as a child must be born, this is the birth of reflected light, which is creation itself, because all of it reflects Allah. The whole thing is a reflection of Allah.

كيف يجحد الاله يا عجب كيف يعصى الاله عن كيف يجحده الجاهد ولله في كل تحريكة تسكينة أبدا شاهد وفي كل شهر آية تدل على أنه واحد

How strange that we can disobey Allah — and stranger still that anyone can disbelieve in Him — when in every movement and every stillness there is a clear witness for Allah, and in everything itself there is a sign.

So the creation sets up these meanings in these images, and what we are doing is trying to decode what those meanings are. It is a genuine gift to witness the birth of the new moon — a real gift. I think people felt that last night, because instead of ilm al-yaqeen you had ayn al-yaqeen. The Quran speaks of three levels: ilm al-yaqeen, ayn al-yaqeen, and haqq al-yaqeen. Imam al-Ghazali's example is that ilm al-yaqeen is like hearing a trustworthy person tell you there is a fire in the forest — you believe them, but that is not the same as seeing the fire yourself. When you go and actually see it, that is ayn al-yaqeen. And if you go and touch it, that is haqq al-yaqeen. The new moon is an amazing thing in that regard. Imam al-Hakim recorded a hadith in which the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, said: khairu ibadillah alladhina yura'una al-adhina wal-ahinna dhikran lillah — the best of Allah's servants are those who are vigilant about new moons and prayer times as a means of remembering Allah. And I think for all of us last night, the natural impulse when we saw it was precisely to remember Allah — li-tukabbiru Allaha 'ala ma hadakum, Allahu Akbar. And for me personally, there was a real nashab of Ramadan that entered my heart — a feeling, a surge of energy — because it was ayn al-yaqeen. That was the Ramadan moon. It is a real gift.

Anyway, I just wanted to share that with you. So let us review what we covered. We went through the diseases of the heart: we began by looking at the importance of adab, and then we examined the hukm of knowing the amrad al-qulub wa adhiyyatiha and their treatments — which, as Imam al-Ghazali stated, constitutes a fard 'ayn — and then he went through the diseases of the heart alphabetically. He obviously gives the most attention to those that matter most. The scholars who have narrowed it down say that if you can control roughly four or five of these diseases, the rest become relatively easy to remove. But he began with bukhl.

Ramadan is a month that is truly suited to curing bukhl. They said that the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, was the most generous of human beings and was even more generous during Ramadan — described as karee' al-mursila, like a rain-bearing wind that brings gifts and causes things to flourish. So there is bukhl. Then there is batar — batar ma'ishatuhum: being delighted by the dunya, by one's livelihood. The believer's fara'ah, his joy, is hinama yuftiru wa hinama yalqa rabbahu — when he breaks his fast and when he meets his Lord. So that stands in direct contrast to batar.

Then there is bughd. Ramadan is a time when you are supposed to address bughd — enmity between hearts. The Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, said: afdalu sadaqah fi Ramadan — the best sadaqah is in Ramadan — and in another hadith: afdalu sadaqah yuslahu bayna ithnayni — the best sadaqah is rectifying relations between people. So the best sadaqah of all would be reconciling between people during Ramadan. This is the time to remove bughd from your heart toward others.

Then there is baghi — oppression and transgression against others. You are fasting, and the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, told us: if someone provokes you or fights with you — wa anta sa'im, fa qul: Allahumma inni sa'im — while you are fasting, say: I am fasting. In other words, la tabghi: do not fight back. Tell them you don't want to go there. Say: I'm fasting.

Then there is hubb al-jah — love of position and status. Ramadan is about gaining a position with Allah. The secret of fasting is that it is the only act of ibadah that no one can tell you are performing while you are performing it. If you give charity, that is a visible act. If you pray, that is a visible act. If you make hajj, that is visible. All the acts of ibadah that Allah has given us can be seen outwardly. Fasting alone is invisible — you can be standing next to someone who is not fasting and someone who is fasting, and only Allah knows which is which. You can be among all these non-Muslims; they see you and have no idea you are fasting. You look like everyone else. The point is that the manzilah you are seeking is with Allah, not with people. That is the cure for hubb al-jah.

Then there is hubb al-dunya. Ramadan is also about weaning yourself from the dunya. The nafs is like a child: if you neglect to wean it, it grows up clinging to what it loves — love of dunya — but if you wean it, it becomes weaned. Ramadan is the time to wean the soul from the dunya and from the love of the dunya.

Then there is hubb al-madh bila fi'l — loving to be praised for things you have not done. Ramadan is the time to witness the shortcomings of your own actions, to genuinely recognize that you have wasted the whole year, and that now it is time to make up for it. The qiyam you didn't do in the previous year — make it up now. The sadaqah you neglected — give it now. What you are trying to do is perform actions that actually warrant praise. So this disease too has its cure in Ramadan.

Then there is hasad — envy. To purify the heart from envy. But if you can conquer desire — because envy arises from desire — then you can remove envy from your heart. And desire, according to Imam al-Ghazali, is rooted in the two appetites of the farj and the batn. If you can master those two — not destroy them, but bring them under control — then all the other desires become manageable.

Then he mentions that what we are really doing in this month is defeating the enmity of shaitan by taking control of all the things shaitan uses to wage war against the iradah of Allah for human beings — His will for them, which is that they believe in Him and follow Him. That is why he mentions that Allah says: if you give victory to Allah through fasting and conquering shaitan, Allah will give you victory and make you firm — and that is what you want. Sabr is part of thabat — steadfastness. We are learning both. And by doing that, Allah says He is with the muhsineen. He also mentions the ayah that Allah will not take away His blessings from a people until they become preoccupied with their shahawat. And you can see that in this society — it is the best example of a pleasure culture. Everything is oriented toward gratification. The idea of fasting is completely alien to most people now because they eat all day long. Not that long ago, people did not graze all day — there were no snack machines everywhere. This grazing culture is something new.

Now, regarding the shayateen being chained in Ramadan — it is only the shayateen, meaning the bad jinn, as the hadith indicates. And some of the ulama, like Imam al-Qadisi al-Farsi who was a Maliki scholar, was asked why people still experience bad thoughts in Ramadan. His explanation was that the hadiths indicate only the kafir jinn are locked up, while the fussaq from among the believing jinn are not. He admitted, however, that he had no textual basis for that, and said that it seems a high probability — and Allah knows best. On a different note, it would genuinely be interesting to conduct serious statistical studies on whether crime rates go down in Ramadan. That would require the kind of resources and stability you have when there is a functioning khilafah and peace in the land — but someone should attempt a PhD thesis on it and convince a university to fund the research.

Now, a couple of important points. Imam al-Ghazali, rahimahullah, goes into the fiqhi issues of fasting, which we covered last night. There are two levels of fiqh: the outward fiqh, which is the concern of the fuqaha, and the inward fiqh, which is the concern of the qawm — the sufiya. The outward fiqh ensures that your outward fasting is legally valid. The inward fiqh ensures that your inward fasting is sound. A hadith makes this very clear, in which the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, said that many people who fast gain nothing from their fast except hunger and thirst. Their outward fast may be correct, but inwardly they are breaking their fast constantly — because if you are backbiting, and doing all these things, you are breaking the fast of the heart.

He describes three types of fasting: sawm al-'umum — the fast of the general public; sawm al-khusus — the fast of the elect; and sawm khusus al-khusus — the fast of the elect of the elect.

The fast of the common people is simply restraining the stomach and the genitals from pleasure during the daylight hours. That is the fast any Muslim is capable of observing.

The fast of the elect is kafa al-sam'i wal-basar wal-lisan wal-yad wal-rijl — guarding the ears, the eyes, the tongue, the hands, and the feet. Remember that Ibn 'Ashir, rahimahullah, speaks of the seven jawariḥ — the seven limbs — for which we are responsible: the eyes, the ears, the tongue, the hand, the stomach, the genitals, and the feet. Two of those seven are addressed by outward fasting — the stomach and the genitals — and so the person who abstains from food and sexual relations has guarded two of the seven. These are called the madakhil al-shaytan, or the furuq al-shaytan: the entrances of shaytan. Ibn al-Qayyim, rahimahullah, noted that the human body is like a country: the capital is the heart, and the borders are the seven limbs. You must guard those seven limbs with vigilance. The heart is the one that sends out the command to guard the limbs — it dispatches the armies to the borders. Shaytan enters the heart through one of those seven things. So the point of fasting is to protect the borders during Ramadan and guard them so that the heart has a chance to draw near to Allah.

لعلكم تتقون كتب عليكم السيام كما كتب على الذين من قبلكم لعلكم تتقون

Fasting is prescribed so that you may learn taqwa. And taqwa —

التقوى ها هنا وأشار إلى قلبه

— the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, said: التقوى ها هنا وأشار إلى قلبه ثلاث مرات — taqwa is here, pointing to his heart, three times. So taqwa resides in the heart. What you are doing through fasting is protecting the heart — by guarding the entrances through which shaytan would enter it. And Allah has helped you: He has placed constraints on the shayateen, giving you tremendous assistance this month. He has also helped by making it obligatory to abstain from food and relations — the two most fundamental sources of temptation. But He left the rest to us, because there must be some mujahida. It is up to us to guard the eyes, the tongue, the ears, the hands, and the feet. That is the fasting of the khawass: they fast with all seven limbs. You guard your tongue, guard your eyes, guard your hands, guard your feet. Do not look at the haram. Do not speak the haram. Do not listen to the haram. If someone starts to backbite in your presence, protect yourself — don't let it reach your ears. Guard your fast.

The du'a of the Prophet about Ramadan was: Allahumma sallimna Ramadan wa sallim Ramadan minna — O Allah, give us Ramadan safely, and protect Ramadan from our evil. You do not want Ramadan to receive any portion of your evil. You want to receive Ramadan's portion of good. This is why Ramadan carries what is known as hurma zamaniyyah — the sanctity of time. Wrong action committed in Ramadan is graver than wrong action at other times, because Allah has given this month a sanctity that must be honored. And you honor it by refraining from what Allah has forbidden.

One of the things Allah has told the human being is that we are to deliver the amanah to those who are entitled to it — that is one of the signs of the believer. The believer gives back what has been entrusted to him. The word mu'min comes from the same root as amanah. A person who has no amanah has no iman. Allah has given us a trust: our sight, our hearing, our tongue, and above all the heart itself, which generates all the actions of the other limbs. You are mas'ul — responsible — for all of them. They are trusts that Allah has placed in your care, and you must return the amanah on Yawm al-Qiyamah, returning it intact, not having corrupted it. Fasting is Allah teaching us how to honor the trust and return it to Him the way He wants it returned: the tongue free of slander, the ears free of what is haram, the eyes free of what is haram, the hands free of haram acts, the feet free of walking toward what is haram, the genitals guarded, and the stomach free of consuming what is haram. That is what Allah wants. And the madrasa through which He is teaching us to achieve this — is fasting.

Then there is the highest level: the fast of the elect of the elect. He defines it as sawm al-qalb 'an al-himam al-daniyyah wal-afkar al-daniyyah — the fasting of the heart from base aspirations and lowly thoughts. In other words, the fast of the elect of the elect is that the heart's aspiration is not directed toward the dunya at all. That is why in Ramadan, giving out is the mode of the akhirah, whereas in other months we are often busy acquiring wealth. In Ramadan, you should be busy giving wealth away for the sake of Allah. You are not in a dunya mode — you are in an akhirah mode. You have to shift this month. That is the shift.

And regarding al-afkar al-daniyyah: one of the scholars of Andalusia said that the first degree of wilaya with Allah is muhasabat al-khawatir — beginning to hold one's thoughts accountable. That is where it begins: the wali measures each thought against the meezan of the shari'ah. That is what makes him a wali. Ramadan is the time to do muhasabah of the heart.

And understand that an 'ammi — a common person — in Arabic does not mean someone who is ignorant of the sacred sciences. There are scholars who consider themselves above the 'awamm because they are learned, yet they are still 'awamm. The 'ammi can be the street sweeper who knows only a few surahs but whose heart is entirely with Allah. That is why the saying goes: how many people are not fasting yet with Allah they are fasting, and how many people are fasting yet with Allah they are not fasting? There are people throughout the year who guard their eyes, their ears, their tongues, their feet, their genitals, and their stomachs — in reality they are living the purpose of fasting, because fasting is an exercise designed to teach us how to do that properly. Others fast physically but with Allah they are not fasting. That is the point. So when he speaks of the khusus, do not imagine that means someone who has mastered Imam Sibawayhi's Al-Kitab or memorized all the tafaseer. These people can be nearly illiterate — if the heart is with Allah subhana wa ta'ala, that is what counts.

Consider Imam Sahnun — Muhammad ibn Sahnun, son of the Sahnun who wrote the Mudawwana. He had a majlis in al-Qayrawan. Every day during his teaching sessions, a man would come in, walk over people's shoulders to reach the sheikh who was seated in a chair, and whisper in his ear for a while — then leave. This happened every time the sheikh taught. The sheikh would actually ask people to move aside so the man would not have to step over them. One day the man stopped coming. Imam Muhammad ibn Sahnun asked his students about him, sent someone to find him, and the man came. The sheikh asked: what happened, you stopped visiting me? The man said: Subhan Allah — I have to explain myself. People who envy you came to me — I am a very poor man with many daughters I need to marry off. They said: go and disturb him every day in his majlis, and if you can make him angry and humiliate him in front of his students, we will give you money to marry your daughters. So every day I would come and I would curse you in your ear and say the most horrible things. And I realized it was having no effect on you whatsoever. So I gave up. Muhammad ibn Sahnun said: why didn't you simply come and ask me for money? I would have given it to you.

That is the sabr you want to learn. That is the sabr of the people of the Messenger of Allah, the sabr of his family. And Salman is from our family — Salman al-Farisi. He was patient from the very beginning of his search. He was a Zoroastrian, and he used to see the sacred fire go out and be quietly relit, while the worshippers were told it never goes out. His father was in charge of tending it, so Salman knew it was a lie. He set out to find the truth. He went to the Christians, served a monk for years, and witnessed that monk stealing the people's charity and burying it in his backyard. He then went to another monk who was genuinely devout, and that monk told him: there is a prophet who will appear in the land of Arabia in your time.

In terms of this month, here is my recommendation to myself first and foremost, and to all of you. Don't do a lot of unnecessary talking. Utilize the time. Every Muslim should be conscious that this month is unlike any other — don't waste it. The salaf used to spend six months before Ramadan begging Allah to let them reach this month. This is not an ordinary time. And none of us knows whether this is our last Ramadan; none of us even knows whether we will make it to the end of this Ramadan. So seize the moment. Utilize this time. Don't let the month slip by and find yourself in the last few days full of regret about what you didn't do. We will all feel that to some degree regardless — human beings are such that we never do anything without realizing afterward we could have done it better. But hopefully the regret will be mild because we did a great deal, not because we did very little.

Utilize the night. The khatm of the Quran in the taraweeh is the tradition, but even praying one surah is effective. Even if you prayed your taraweeh with Qul Huwa Allahu Ahad in every rak'ah — if that is all you know — and you completed your rak'ahs, that counts as qiyam. So do qiyam every night, even if it is twenty rak'ahs with Qul Huwa Allahu Ahad. Do qiyam at night. In fact, one scholar said that toward the end of time it is good to pray the night prayer with the shorter surahs that carry great reward, simply because there is so little time and most people no longer have the himma for long recitation. So utilize the time. Try to pray the Duha prayer. Try to rise before Fajr, do some qiyam before Fajr, and then utilize the time after Fajr — try not to sleep during that period, which is makruh in any case, and in Ramadan that time is even more precious than at other times. Then try to do the Duha. The Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, instructed Abu Hurayra never to abandon the Duha, so you should try to pray it — it is mandub and has a very strong basis in practice, since the Prophet did it consistently: two, four, six, or eight rak'ahs before the zawal, all the way up to the zawal. For the Malikis and the Hanafis, there is the period during which the nafilah cannot be prayed, but otherwise pray nafilah freely.

And then: prayer on time. Prayer on time. We heard earlier that the Messenger of Allah, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, on his deathbed — his final wasiyyah to his ummah — his last words were: al-salah, al-salah. Prayer is above everything. Guard your prayer. Use this month to discipline yourself to pray on time. If you were not praying on time before Ramadan — and you should have been — then use this time to build that discipline. Pray on time.


If you're working, just stop — your prayer matters more than your job. I guarantee it. Prayer is more important than your job.

Pray first. And pay particular attention to the time between Asr and Maghrib, which is a powerful time throughout the year — a time of dhikr. During Asr especially, it carries a particular weight. It is, as the Quran tells us, a time for the people who believe — a time of love for one another.

<!-- page 302 -->

And that is why in this culture you see people at the end of the day completely worn out, needing to go out and drink. They call it happy hour. No, seriously — that is what they call it. They need oblivion so they don't have to think about how empty their lives are. For us, that time is about preparing for sunset, which is a metaphor for death. Asr to Maghrib represents the later years of a person's life. That entire period carries symbolic weight. And breaking the fast is like what the Messenger described — there are two joys in it. The analogy being made is that breaking the fast resembles meeting your Lord: when the day is over, when death is over, you return to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala.

After you break your fast, the best thing is to break it on rutab, or tamr, or a few sips of water. If you have none of that, you can break it on whatever is available. If there is no food at all, then go ahead and pray. Delaying the iftar is only makruh when the delay comes from believing it is somehow better to delay — at that point it becomes a bid'ah. But if there is a valid reason to delay, it is perfectly fine. Just do not eat before praying Maghrib.

When you visit Muslim households, they will sometimes ask: do you want to eat first or pray? Tell them — let's pray first. Do not be shy about that. Because if people sit down to eat, the Maghrib prayer can slip out of its time. Maghrib has a very narrow window, and you do not want to delay it. The preferred time is right at the beginning. After the adhan comes in, make your du'a, find a clean place, and pray. During the fast you are given a small window — break the fast with something to drink, make the du'a, and then when you finish, move to prayer. These are good habits to build: learning the du'as, practicing them, carrying them with you.

As for the Isha prayer — if you have a strong resolve, it is better to pray your night prayers at home. But if you know yourself well enough to know that your resolve is not strong, or if there is a muqri' doing a khatm of the Quran in the masjid and you want to be part of that khatm, then going to the masjid for it is also a good thing. It is mandub.

<!-- page 303 -->

What you should avoid — and unfortunately many of our brothers have fallen into this — is the idea that Tarawih is only eight rak'ahs, and so once the eight are done, they say, "Alhamdulillah, we're following the Sunnah," and then spend the next hour standing outside the masjid talking. Just be honest with yourself: it is fine to say you find twenty rak'ahs long. But do not dress your tiredness up as following the Sunnah. Because the Sunnah is certainly not to loiter at the door of the masjid and make idle conversation — I guarantee you that. The Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, did not waste his time. And empty talk at the door of the masjid is — that is suq-level behavior. That is what the marketplaces are for. We do not want to be part of that.

And do not belittle people, do not look down on them. I am not trying to be judgmental — everyone is where they are for their own reasons. The point is simply this: do not see yourself as better than someone else. Instead, thank Allah that He has not afflicted you with that affliction — because it is an affliction. When the Prophet, sallallahu alayhi wa sallam, saw fifty people doing such things, he said what he said. Recognizing a ni'mah that Allah has given you is not arrogance. Arrogance is thinking you deserve it more than someone else does. It is simply a favor Allah has extended to you, for reasons known only to Him — and He could take it from you tomorrow, give your state to that person, and leave you standing at the door of the masjid talking.

There is a story about one man who accused Mu'adh ibn Jabal of doing something haram, and Mu'adh — who was known as mustajab al-du'a, someone whose supplications were answered — made a du'a against him. In his old age, that man was seen on the streets of Medina, pinching young women as he walked, because his eyelids had literally fallen over his eyes. He could not see. That was the ibtila' Allah gave him in his old age, for falsely condemning Mu'adh ibn Jabal.

So I am not asking anyone to be self-righteous — myself included. But the point stands: do not go down that road. If people are gathered and talking, greet them. It is a joyous time and there is a place for that. But keep it in its proper place.

<!-- page 304 -->

And remember our brothers and sisters who are in great tribulation right now — the people of Palestine, the people of Iraq, the people of Algeria, the people of Bosnia, the people of India. I could go on and on, and I would rather not. Those people are genuinely suffering. So temper your joy with some gravity as well.

One thing I learned a long time ago about spiritual light is that sometimes, when a great deal of it descends on people, their reaction is to become giddy — to laugh, to get frivolous. You can see it happen. And it is a way of spilling the light, of wasting it. Ramadan can do that to people. Because there is so much light pouring in, and they have not learned how to contain it, they end up laughing through the whole month, socializing, having a good time — and by the end they have spilled it all out. So try to hold on to what you are receiving. Try to contain the benefits of this month.

I think we are about to break. Is the bus here? Yes. You are supposed to do the Isha of the evening. I will come back after Isha. Are they leaving now? Okay. Then before we pray, since there is still a little time, I want to read from the section on fasting in the Muwatta' — for the barakah of it.

What is that? I am glad you mentioned that. The other thing: Imam Malik, rahimahullah, during Ramadan did not like storytelling, did not like idle talk. He said: this is a time of qiyam and qira'ah. So the best dhikr during Ramadan is Quran. You should still be doing your awrad and wird — those are important in Ramadan as at any other time. But you need to have your reading of Quran.

Imam Abu Hanifa, rahimahullah, said that if you read the Quran at least twice a year, you are not considered to have abandoned it — meaning you will not fall under the ayah in which the Messenger of Allah complained that his people had left the Quran. Ramadan is the time to spend with the Book of Allah. Ideally, if you can complete more than one khatam, that would be excellent. I know some of you personally — and one person I know completed twenty-six khatams in Ramadan. But that was essentially all he was doing. For people who work, that is clearly difficult.

<!-- page 305 -->

If you are not proficient in recitation, there is also the option of listening to a muqri' — if you have a recording. When you are driving and you do not have much memorized, it is better to recite what you do know from memory, but if you do not have much to draw from, then simply listen to recitation. And also do some tadabbur of the Quran during Ramadan — not only the ta'abbud of it, but genuine reflection on its meaning.

If you want a structured approach, there is the seven-day wird of the Quran. The pattern goes: three, five, seven, nine, eleven, thirteen, and then the mufassal. On the first day, you read the first three surahs. On the second day, the next five.
