Then seven, then nine, then eleven, then thirteen, and then the mufassal — which runs from Surah Qaf to the end of the Quran, or according to some, from Surah Hujurat. In seven days you can complete the Quran. This is how many of the salaf approached it: breaking it into those odd-numbered portions — three, five, seven, nine, eleven, thirteen, and the mufassal.

Question. If you owe fard prayers, I would not pray Tarawih. Use Ramadan as the time to make up what you owe. Do the fard. Instead of qiyam, use that time to make up your missed fard prayers. The only nafl prayers you should prioritize if you owe fard prayers are the witr and the raghiba — the pre-dawn prayer. Everything else should go toward making up what is owed.

According to the reckoning of the scholars: if you owe prayers and you are performing fewer than five make-up prayers at each prayer time throughout the year, you are not doing enough. If you owe a great deal — years' worth — then do the minimal valid form of the prayer, just the arkan and the mu'akkad sunan. That is the wager you owe. Do that consistently, and insha'Allah, Allah will accept it.

<!-- page 306 -->

As for estimating, the scholars say: do your best to figure out when you stopped praying. Most people remember roughly when they went seriously astray in their lives — that tends to be a recognizable turning point. Start from there.

By minimal prayer, I mean: perform the arkan — the sixteen pillars — and the eight mu'akkad sunan. That is it. There are sixteen arkan and eight mu'akkad sunan. That is the form you focus on when making up a large number of prayers.

If you are still learning and you have doubts about whether you are making mistakes in the prayers you are currently practicing — I think you just have to move forward. There are scholars who would say you must make those up, but my view is: if you are learning fiqh now and your errors were from ignorance, then make tawbah and ask Allah. If there was an obligation you were not even aware of — because your parents never taught you, you genuinely did not know — then in a real sense, that responsibility traces back further. A Muslim child is an amanah, and one of the obligations of that amanah is to teach them the fara'id. Many Muslims were simply never told any of this. They thought they were doing right.

For those people — insha'Allah — I would say: make tawbah, ask Allah to excuse you for your ignorance, and ask Him to accept those prayers. I would not burden them beyond that. I think that is the appropriate position.

Question. Can a raghiba include the two rak'ahs after Maghrib? No — the raghiba is not that. Some scholars say you should pray at least two rak'ahs of nafl after Maghrib, and there is a khilaf about that. But the witr and the raghiba should definitely be maintained even when one owes fard prayers.

<!-- page 307 -->

Question. If you owe fard prayers, what about Quran recitation in the meantime? If you have time that you would use to make up prayers — that time should go to the fard. The fard takes precedence. As the hadith states: "My servant does not draw near to Me with anything more beloved to Me than the fard." So the fard is above everything. Truly. If you are performing nafl and not the fard, you are wasting your time.

If the number owed is fewer than five, do them in order. If more than five, you do not need to do them in order. And some scholars say: if you owe a large number, simply commit to a set amount at each prayer time — for example, five prayers at Dhuhr time, five at Asr time, five at Maghrib time, and so on. You do not have to go in sequence.

Is the bus here?

Alright. Insha'Allah, a few closing words — this is the last class for those going on the intensive. Jazakumullah khayran, and tawfiq insha'Allah. I want to thank all of you for your attendance, your respect, and the wonderful spirit that has characterized this entire course. Please excuse me for any shortcomings or breaches of courtesy on my part. And please, make du'a.

<!-- page 308 -->

Bismillah ar-Rahman ar-Rahim.

By the blessing of Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala, we were able to go through this text and complete it. We ask Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala to grant us, insha'Allah, the ability to implement what we have learned.

In closing this text, I would like to share some counsel written by one of our great scholars, Sidi Ahmad Zarruq, who died in 1492. He was a great scholar from Morocco who died in Masratah in Libya. He said that the fragrance of his teaching would spread throughout the world long after his bones were laid to rest — and this has proven to be one of his karamat, his saintly miracles, because his teaching is still reaching us and shaping us today.

He said — rahimahullah:

Know — may Allah grant you and us success, rectify our worldly and otherworldly lives, and grant us adherence to the way of truth in our journeys and in our sojourns — that repentance is a key, and taqwa is vast, and uprightness is the source of all rectification. A servant is never free of blunders, or shortcomings, or lassitude. Therefore, never be neglectful of tawbah. Never turn away from returning to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala. Never neglect the acts that bring you closer to Him. Every time one of these three occurs — repent and return. Every time you make a mistake — listen and obey. Every time you display shortcomings or lack of enthusiasm — do not give up. Let your primary concern be to remove from your outward state anything that is displeasing to Allah, and then to maintain that outward state through continuous self-counsel. Keep doing this until you find that you naturally flee from anything outwardly displeasing, and that your avoidance of the boundaries of the prohibited has become like a protective net placed before you.


At this point, the time has come to turn inward — toward your heart's presence and its reality — through both reflection and remembrance. Do not hasten the end result before you have completed the beginning, but likewise, do not begin without keeping the end in view. The one who seeks what belongs to the outset at the end loses providential care, and the one who seeks what belongs to the end at the outset loses providential guidance.

Act in accordance with principles and the appropriate legal rulings, not in accordance with stories and fantasies. Do not consider accounts of how things went with others except as a tonic to strengthen your resolve — certainly not as a reference to imitate based on their outward forms or what they appear to be saying.

In all of this, rely on a clear path you can refer to and a foundation you can depend upon in every state. The best of these is the path of Ibn Ata'illah, for it contains a clear direction toward Allah. Do not take from others' words unless what they say accords with your own path, but submit to their implications if you desire realization.

Avoid all forms of vain and foul speech to your absolute utmost. Set aside anything whose benefit you cannot discern immediately. Beware of being excessively hard on yourself before you have attained mastery over it, but equally beware of being too lax in anything that concerns sacred rulings.

This is because the self is constantly fleeing from moderation in everything, inclining toward extremism in both deviance and guidance alike. To pause and reflect on this is extremely important, because what the Shaykh is telling us is that the nature of the self is extremism — and he is warning us to be aware of this in both good deeds and foul ones, in both obedience to Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala and disobedience to Him.

So he is telling us: beware of being excessively hard on yourself before you have attained mastery over it, but also beware of being too lax in anything that concerns sacred rulings. He continues: seek out a companion to help you in your affairs and take his counsel in matters that arise from both your inward states and your outward affairs. If you do take his companionship, then treat him in a manner commensurate with his state, and offer your counsel according to his limitations and capacities, for the perfected friend is no longer to be found.

Indeed, in these times even a suitable, agreeable companion rarely lasts. Beware of the majority of people in matters concerning your religious and worldly affairs, unless you have established that a person has a sound relationship with his Lord — grounded in knowledge free of caprice or love of leadership, and in a sound intellect free of hidden agendas. Do not be heedless of the machinations of others or their hidden states.

Consider people from the vantage point of both their origins and their actions. A person of character and distinguished lineage will rarely affect you with anything other than good, whereas a person of low origins typically lets those roots lead him to disregard you when times grow difficult. Be extremely vigilant of the dominant qualities of the people in any given land, do not be heedless of the divine wisdom in creation, and take notice of both gatheredness and separation.

Some of this we have already covered in our book Al-Qara'ib, so refer to it there. Engage with any matter only in a manner appropriate to what that moment specifically calls for, using gentleness and toleration, and be very wary of both harshness and laxity. Too much laxity in permissible matters pulls the heart backward in its journey until even a person of firm resolve ends up resembling a foolish boy.

Work for this world as if you will live forever, but work for the next life as if you will die tomorrow. Do not neglect the externals of your worldly needs, and in the meantime do not be heedless of your end and final resting place.

Be extremely vigilant about avoiding positions of leadership, but should you be tried with such responsibilities, at least know your own limitations. Be utterly sincere — with the sincerity of one who knows full well who is placing demands upon him. Surrender completely to His decree with the submission of one who knows he can never overcome Him.

Have a firm foundation for all your affairs and you will be safe from their pitfalls. Organize your devotional practices and you will find your time extended through the barakah — the blessings — within it. Never be fanatical about anything, whether it is truth or falsehood, and your heart will remain sound toward others.

Never claim anything you are entitled to, let alone what you are not entitled to, and you will be safe from tricks and treachery. Anyone who claims a rank above his own will fall in humiliation; those who claim a rank they do warrant will have it stripped from them; while those who claim a station less than their true rank will be elevated to levels even higher than they actually deserve. Never give your companion anything of your inner state beyond what his own state warrants. If you descend to his level, he will show you contempt, whereas if you attempt to raise him to your level, he will abandon you.

Never demand a right from anyone, whether an intimate or a stranger. A stranger in reality owes you nothing, and someone close to you is too precious to be the target of your blame. Never assume that anyone in this world can truly understand your circumstances except through the lens of their own. In reality, everyone sees things in accordance with their own frame of reference and personal path. Yet when aims, purposes, and aspirations are similar, people tend to work together toward a common goal.

Never belittle any talk that involves people in their absence, even if it seems harmless, given the possibility that harm may enter into it. Guard your secrets even when you feel safe with someone, for the person you confide in is not a safer vessel than your own heart from which the secret first arose. Never abandon even an atom's weight of your regular devotional practice. Never be lenient with yourself, whether in times of laxity or times of high resolve. Should you miss some of your practice at a given time, make it up at another. If you are unable to perform your usual practice, at least occupy yourself with something comparable.

Never obey your ego, even for a moment. Never believe any of its claims, no matter what it tells you. Be vigilant about your resolve in all your affairs to your utmost. Should you resolve to do something, do it immediately before the resolve wanes. Examine your soul constantly regarding what you are obliged or need to do. Anything you have no need to do, leave it — even if it is recommended. That means not involving yourself in anything beyond what is absolutely necessary and what constitutes a real, discernible need.

Treat others exactly as you would wish to be treated, and fulfill what is due to them. All of this is beautifully captured in the words of the poet:

*If you desire to live such that your religion is safe, your portion full, and your honor sound — guard your tongue, and never mention another's faults, remembering that you yourself have faults and others have tongues. Watch your eye; should it ever reveal to you the faults of others, say to it: O my eye, other people have eyes too. Live treating others well, and avoid aggression, and should others aggress against you, leave them — but in the best way.*

The source of these words is nothing other than the traditions of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, when he said: *Be vigilant of Allah wherever you are, and follow a misdeed with a good deed — it will remove it — and treat others with the most excellent of character.* In another narration he said, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him: *Every child of Adam makes mistakes, and the best of those who make mistakes are those who seek to redress them.* And again: *The Holy Spirit inspired my heart's core that no soul will die until it fulfills its decreed portion of this world and its appointed time here. So be conscious of Allah and make your requests with dignity.*

In summation, repentance, awareness of Allah, and uprightness are the foundations of all that is beneficial. The truth is clear, and its details are weighty and significant. The affair belongs only to Allah; success is in His hands. Peace be upon you.
